# Volume 05 Chapter Summaries — *The Nine Locks*, Batch 0001, Chapters 201–210

**Compact continuity records for the next batch, not substitutes for the chapter text. The canon contract is `outline/batches/volume-05-batch-0001.md` and the block record is `state/volume-05-batch-0001-summary.md`.**

## Chapter 0201 — The First Of April

**The month turns, and it is said once, in the yard of Lot Seventeen at about eight in the morning, and the board outside the room has carried the twenty-fourth of December for ninety-eight days and the train on the siding has stood four hundred and fourteen days.** The eighteenth year after the Long Fracture, and the derivation of the train's day-count is restated with the year named, because **a count taken inside the eighteenth year alone gives forty-eight and is wrong.** A clerk of nineteen years entered the turn and entered that the month before it was the third and the one before that the second, and that nobody asked. **The word steward is asked out loud: it is a word, a date of the thirty-first of March, and a name in the ward market's own book, and the date has passed, and the second half of the word is a season and the season started this morning and nobody has stood in a frame since the nineteenth of October, and a clerk entered that this is not a delay for the third time in eleven weeks and was not asked to.** The woman of fifty-eight is asked in the six things, in front of about nine people, for the third time and it is not a new question, **and she says in sixty-four words that she cannot say what a day of it is worth and is not going to take a thing she cannot say the worth of, and a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale said on the thirty-first of the third month that she has offered her four houses and cannot weigh a day.** **The fifth of the five things this district does not have comes out of the mouth of a person who weighs sacks for a living and the count did not move.** The six households at the sluice end stand at one signed, two refused, three not asked again, and **a man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households says a man who is not asking has not been refused, and both halves went into the minute in their own words, and the record says not asked.** A man of about thirty-one goes out at first light with a stub about the size of a coin. **A route keeper walks nine miles and says in forty-five words that somebody opened his road on the night of the twenty-second of the second month, that it was not him, that he has not seen who it was, and that he is not going to say a name.** The tank four miles down a lane is at nothing for the first time since the first of the second month and the crack is dry and a man of about fifty-five refuses a fifth mend for the third time in four months with no reason. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says a man who is proud of a door is a man who has not counted what comes through it, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam says a person who opens a road and is not asked who opened it is not the same as a person who is not asked, and this district has spent four months finding that out about a name on a page and is now finding it out about a road.** The road is nine miles from the fourth holding on the delivery sheet and the two are a coincidence until somebody says otherwise. **AND ON THE FIRST OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE MONTH TURNED AND WAS SAID ONCE, THE BOARD HAD CARRIED THE TWENTY-FOURTH OF DECEMBER FOR NINETY-EIGHT DAYS, THE TRAIN HAD STOOD FOUR HUNDRED AND FOURTEEN DAYS, A WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SIX OFFERED A WOMAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT FOUR HOUSES OF A ROUND AND COULD NOT SAY WHAT A DAY OF IT WAS WORTH, AND A ROUTE KEEPER SAID IN FORTY-FIVE WORDS THAT SOMEBODY HAD OPENED HIS ROAD ON A NIGHT IN THE SECOND MONTH AND IT WAS NOT HIM.**

## Chapter 0202 — A Man With A Portfolio

**A man of about forty-four comes up the lower terrace road at about half past eleven with a flat case the size of a hymn book, and walks past a yard with nine people in it to go to a door with a fanlight over it.** **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing follows him at about eleven yards' distance without being asked to and without being told not to, and nobody stops him.** The name on the case is the name of a Registry, and a Registry is a body and not a house and not a person. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says in the lane that a book a stranger can walk up to is a door, that we built it, that we told nineteen people it was the best instrument in this district, and that it is the best instrument in this district and it is a door. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says eighteen months, four hundred yards, four hundred yards and a stranger, and that nobody has come through it who was not a neighbour or a creditor, and that the good half ran out this morning.** The man comes back into the yard at about twenty past twelve on his own account, puts the case on the trestle table, does not open it, and then opens it. **Eleven sheets. He turns one round so that the writing is the right way up for the people in the yard, and a man of fifty-six entered that a man who turns a paper round so that four people can read it has done one thing for free.** He says in thirty-nine words that he holds a renewal, that the lease it renews wants a bearer named, and that a Registry does not keep a book a stranger can walk up to and he has come to the one place in this basin that does. **The registrar of this district's records office comes into the yard on her own account, was not sent for, and says in sixty-four words that she will tell him what this office prints and not what is in it, and that an office may print a house and may not print a person, and that a name off a sheet is not a person who agreed to be there.** He does not say where he got the name. **She refuses him a page in about four seconds and gives the reason in thirty-seven words: you may look at anything in this office, you may not carry it out and you may not have a copy of a page, and that is not about you and is about the hand on it.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says a man who will not say where he got a name is not the same as a woman who will not give a page, and the man who digs loam says they are the same, and **he says the two sentences out loud on purpose and the clerk entered that he did and that it was not asked for.** He is put in the six things: he does not answer the third, which is the year, and gives no reason. **He says in forty-one words that the lease names the place, that this is not the lease, that he is not going to say it today, that he is not refusing to say it, and that both of those are true — and a clerk entered that this district has a column for a person who will not read a thing and that the count in it is nine, and that it does not have a column for a person who will not name a place and never had one, and that the second is a hole and not a blank.** The sixth of the six is who he came for and he answers it before it is asked: **there is a man in this basin whose name I have and I did not get it here and I am not going to say where I got it and that is not a refusal, it is a door on my side.** He is sleeping at the second house up the lane with a sign over the door that is not a sign. **AND ON THE SECOND OF THE FOURTH MONTH A MAN CAME UP THE LOWER TERRACE ROAD WITH A PORTFOLIO AND WENT PAST A YARD WITH NINE PEOPLE IN IT TO A DOOR WITH A FANLIGHT OVER IT, THE REGISTRAR CAME INTO THE YARD ON HER OWN ACCOUNT AND REFUSED HIM A PAGE IN FOUR SECONDS AND GAVE THE REASON, AND HE SAID THAT HE HAD A NAME AND DID NOT SAY WHERE HE HAD GOT IT.**

## Chapter 0203 — The Renewal

**A clerk of nineteen years reads a stranger's sheet out in a yard in front of about nineteen people at about ten in the morning, and a man of fifty-six says a document is read out and not discussed, and that a sheet brought by a stranger is read the same way a sheet brought by a neighbour is read, and that the two being read the same is the only protection anybody in that yard has.** The sheet has three lines: **a lease is renewed; the term ended on the last day of the second month and this renewal is dated the first of the fourth month; the use is a road, the toll is paid at the ninth place, the holder is the Registrar-General, Cael Orin; the place is named on the lease, this is not the lease.** **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing and the man who digs loam say two halves of the same worry at the same time: the renewal is late, and nobody knows it is late because nobody has ever seen the day it was due on.** The third line is read again and he says in thirty-one words that he is not going to say it today and is not saying it today either and has not refused and is not going to refuse. **A clerk entered that this is the second time in two days that a person has said both halves of a refusal in the same breath, and that the second of those refusals has no count at all and that is a hole and not a blank.** There are ten other sheets: eight about places nobody here has heard of, a schedule, and a lease, and **the man says a stranger carried ten documents into a yard and one was read and nine were not, and a document a stranger will not read out in a yard is a document the stranger owns and is not a fourth of the three nobody owns.** The man who digs loam says the ninth place on the delivery sheet is the one nobody can account for and that he has been to that counter eleven times in eight weeks and been refused five times with no reason, and that he is not saying those are the same. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said in the lane at about half past three that a name said once in a yard is a name four hundred people have, and that this district has had one of those since the twentieth of December and did not know it was the good kind. The name was said once and not written down, and the minute says a holder and does not carry the name.** In the lane at the sluice end, the six households stand where they stood, and **the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat, one of the two that refused, says in eighteen words that she would rather be in the water with her name not written on anything than pay for it.** **The man of about thirty-one comes home at about half past two with a strip of paper about the length of his hand with a line ruled across it and a word at the top and nothing under the line, puts it on the table unasked, and says in fifty-one words that the counter took his name off the sheet in about nine seconds, cost nothing, asked nothing, and that he has not been released and has been found.** A clerk entered that a man has been found and not released and that the two are not joined, and that **a name put on a figure cannot be taken off it by the person it is put on, and what came off here is not the figure and is the name, and the figure of twenty-one years came off this district's page on the thirty-first of March in the same line as the date and was not asked for.** He says in thirty-four words that it can be taken off where they put it on and not by him, and that he is not glad, and that he is not going to be asked what he is going to do with it. **And the Registry man, at about four in the afternoon, asked what a Registry is for, says in thirty-one words that a Registry holds records of holdings, that that is all it does, that it is not a small thing to do, and that it is not a thing you can do in a yard.** **AND ON THE THIRD OF THE FOURTH MONTH A CLERK OF NINETEEN YEARS READ OUT A SHEET OF THREE LINES AND THE THIRD OF THEM SAID THAT THE PLACE IS NAMED ON THE LEASE AND THAT THIS IS NOT THE LEASE, THE HOLDER ON THE SECOND LINE IS THE REGISTRAR-GENERAL, CAEL ORIN, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE CAME HOME WITH A STRIP OF PAPER THAT SAYS HIS OWN NAME CAME OFF A SHEET AND SAID IN FIFTY-ONE WORDS THAT HE HAD BEEN FOUND AND NOT RELEASED.**

## Chapter 0204 — The Bearer

**A Registry asks a yard for a person, in the six things, at about ten in the morning in front of about nineteen people, and the man with the portfolio is not sent for and comes.** He says in thirty-seven words that a Registry does not name a bearer, that a lease names a bearer, and that a lease is a page in a book you may look at and may not carry out, and that that is the same rule yours has. **A man of about thirty-four who digs loam says the answer out loud before the man with the portfolio can get to it: the man at the back of the book, there is one of him in this district and he is standing in this yard and everybody in it knows it and two people thirty-four miles east and four hundred yards from here know it and neither of them was in this yard when it happened.** A clerk entered that he said the whole of a thing in a yard that had taken four months and eleven pages to arrive at and that she is not going to put a number on it. **Adrian says no himself, unasked, in sixty-seven words: he stood at the back of a book about water because he could not list what he had posted, he has listed it since, this is a different thing, and a man who cannot say no to a stranger with a portfolio is not a man who is standing at the back of a book, he is a person holding a piece of paper.** A man of fifty-six says that is the second time a man has refused a bearing in eleven days and that the first was a route keeper standing on a bank saying nothing. **The man who digs loam says in twenty-two words that he has said no and said why and that the why is the only reason anybody in this yard has given for anything in four months that was not about a figure.** A man of fifty-six says a man who brought a lease into a yard and was asked for a bearer by a yard and did not need one is a man who came here to be asked, **and a body which sends a man to be asked is a body that expects a no and has a paper for the no.** The Registry man then says, unasked and uncounted, **that the bearer does not have to be from this district, and that it is on the second line, and that it is a fact about the page and not a rule, and that he has not read the page and the yard cannot read it either.** **The registrar refuses in four seconds, in seventy words, not to have this district entered in a Registry, not to have a man in the yard entered under a holder, and not to have the ninth place entered under a toll.** Then she says one more thing to him and not to the yard and nobody counts it: **you walked past a yard with nine people in it this morning to go to a door with a fanlight over it, there are two doors in this district with a fanlight over them, you went to the wrong one for what you came for, and I am not going to tell you that in a yard.** **A man of about thirty at the counter comes down the eleven mile road for the tenth time in eight weeks with four sheets, and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says in thirty-nine words that a man carrying sheets for a house and a man carrying a case for a Registry are two functionaries and that neither of them is a party and that a yard is a room and a room is not a party and is not a seventh of the six instruments.** He is asked about the ninth holding for the sixth time in eleven weeks and refuses with no reason, **making six refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given, and six are six and are not a column and are not added to the thirteen.** He is asked a seventh, new thing — where the four sheets go when he has put them down — and does not answer, and he looks at the man who digs loam and then at the man with the portfolio and **the two men did not look at each other at any point in the afternoon.** The nine holdings are read out at about three in the afternoon: **one, two and three hold and take, four is the top field four miles out and has not taken a drop since the first of the second month, five and six hold and take, seven was given up on the twenty-fourth of the second month and has a bar down, eight holds, nine is the one nobody can account for.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says in thirty-nine words that she has weighed nine sacks a day for nine years and has never weighed a day. **The man of about thirty-one says in thirty-eight words that the strip of paper is in his coat, that he is not going to pin it up, not going to burn it, and not going to have it read out in a yard again, and that he has had it read out in a yard and it did not help.** **AND ON THE FOURTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM SAID IN A YARD THAT THE BEARER OF A LEASE ANYBODY WANTS IS THE ONLY PERSON IN THIS DISTRICT WHO STANDS AT THE BACK OF A BOOK, AND THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST REFUSED IN SIXTY-SEVEN WORDS AND GAVE A REASON, AND THE REGISTRAR REFUSED IN FOUR SECONDS TO HAVE THE NINTH PLACE ENTERED UNDER A TOLL.**

## Chapter 0205 — The Ninth Row

**The fourth of the eleven sheets is read and it is a schedule and not a lease, at about ten in the morning, in front of about nineteen people.** A sheet with nine rows ruled across it, a column on the left with a number in each row, and a column on the right with a heading over it. **The heading is one word and it says OPEN.** The schedule reads: **the schedule of nine gates, the first is at the first place and the ninth is at the ninth place; eight of the nine carry a day in the column headed OPEN, the ninth carries a word; a word in a column headed OPEN is not a day and is not a figure and the schedule does not say what it is.** A man of about thirty-four who digs loam says a row that says it is not a road is a thing that rhymes with a holding nobody can account for and **a rhyme is not a join and he is not going to make it one at ten in the morning.** The man with the portfolio says in five words that it is not a road. **The panel at about eleven says that a schedule that names eight roads names eight things and not nine, that the ninth is not unnamed but named as something that is not a road, and that naming a thing as what it is not is a finding and not a blank; and that a person may be asked for a bearing once in the same six things and not twice, and that a second asking is not a new question and is not added to any count in this district.** A man of about thirty-four who digs loam **disagrees with the last line out loud, in twenty-three words: a second asking on the next day is a new question if the answer changed, and mine changed, and I gave a reason.** The board has carried the twenty-fourth of December for **a hundred and two days** and has four dates on it and no fifth, **and the seventh line at the ninth place is ruled and has nothing on it and has been ruled since the second of January.** Nobody put a line on a board on any of the five days from the first of April. **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale asks what is opening, having heard the word OPEN read out four times, and the man with the portfolio says in twenty-three words that his Registry calls them gates, that the word on his sheet is gates, and that the word on the yard's sheet is not on his sheet.** A man of fifty-six says this district has been using the Registry's word for four days without knowing it was the Registry's word, **and that a word a stranger lends you is a word a stranger can take back, and that nobody in this yard had asked where the word came from and that the asking was done by a man who keeps a scale at a weighbridge because a column said OPEN.** A man of fifty-six says a man who keeps a road is nine miles off and would be the man to ask and **nobody sent for him and the record says not asked.** The man of about thirty-one is asked what a person at the back of a book is for and says in forty-four words: **I do not know. I said on the thirtieth of the third month that it stops at me and I said the same thing to nine people and I have had four days to find out whether I meant it and I did not.** The man who digs loam says the fifth of the five is a man and was never a bearer and that he is going to stop saying that, **because a man who is a security and is not a bearer is exactly the thing the ninth row is, and I am not joining them and I am not letting go of that.** The man of about thirty-six says he has been in this yard three days, said OPEN four times, and been asked what was opening by himself, and that he is not going to be made a finding. **The man of about fifty-five, asked whether the water that came down a channel on the twenty-ninth of the third month came from above him or from below him, says in forty-six words that he has watched that tank for nine years and has never once been able to say where the water came from, and that a channel is a channel and the road beside it is a road and the two of them meet about a mile up.** He then says in thirty-five words that he said no to a fifth mend in January and is going to say no to a fifth mend in April, and that a tank with a mark on it is a tank he can see and a tank with a mend in it is a tank that comes out in three pieces. **At about half past four the Registry man is asked, as the sixth of the six, when the lease has to be answered, and gives the last day of the fourth month, and says in twenty-five words that he has to be at the place on it and there is nobody at the place but me.** **A man of fifty-six says a stranger has now given this district a day for the first time in four months and that a day is a figure and a figure is not a schedule, and that the man has been in this yard four days and has cost this district a page, a name, a word it had been using and a morning, and that the district is not going to count any of that because a clerk of nineteen years would have to have a column and there is not one.** **AND ON THE FIFTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A CLERK OF NINETEEN YEARS READ OUT A SCHEDULE OF NINE ROWS UNDER A COLUMN HEADED OPEN, EIGHT OF WHICH CARRY A DAY AND THE NINTH OF WHICH CARRIES THE WORDS IT IS NOT A ROAD, A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM DISAGREED WITH THE LAST LINE OF THE PANEL THAT CAME AT ELEVEN IN THE MORNING IN TWENTY-THREE WORDS, A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SIX WHO KEEPS A SCALE ASKED WHAT WAS OPENING, AND THE MAN FROM THE REGISTRY SAID THAT THE LAST DAY OF THE FOURTH MONTH WAS THE DAY HE HAD TO BE AT A PLACE WHERE THERE WAS NOBODY BUT HIMSELF.**

## Chapter 0206 — What A No Does

**A man says no for the second time and nothing happens, and the man with the portfolio is on his fourth day with a bench outside the door with the fanlight over it that nobody gave him.** A clerk entered that a stranger has slept three nights at his own cost and has not been charged and has not asked. **The list of the five is read out again: a charter of four sheets closed on the eighteenth of December with a page of it in the ward market's own book and no word certified on any of the four; a third form of a withdrawal notice, twenty-nine words on its face, printed and fixed to a counter and not kept by a person; a food agreement of six sheets on seasonal terms of the seventh and eighth of December, witnessed by two people who were not in the room when the reason was read out, and the sheet says so on its face; a receipt in a building thirty-four miles east that a stranger may read and may not walk off with; and a man of about thirty-one who mends a dray at a forge end off Salt Row.** **The man with the portfolio says in fifteen words that it is a good list and that his is nineteen pages and none of it is that, and a man of fifty-six entered that this is the first time in four months a man from outside this basin has said anything about this district that was not an ask.** The man who digs loam says **a Registry does not want a list, a Registry wants a person, this yard has spent four months getting to the end of a list, and a stranger walked in and asked for the one thing the list was never going to be.** They ask him again, on a new day, what a bearer is, and he says no again in fifty-eight words: **I said no on Thursday in sixty-seven words and I am saying no again in fewer and I am not going to be asked a third time this week, and a man who says no twice in a yard has not stopped anything and I would like that written down in a line where somebody will find it.** A man of fifty-six says in seventy-two words, at a counter's voice, that **a no in a yard is a thing that happened, it is not a thing that holds, the lease is on a page a stranger may look at and may not carry out and it does not know this yard happened, and a man who says no in front of nineteen people believes he has done something and he has done one thing and it is not the thing.** The man who digs loam says the part that had not been said: **he is the only person in this district who can be fastened to a toll, he has said no in a yard twice, a house thirty-four miles east already knows his name, and the only reason that has not cost anything this week is that the house has not asked for it. That is not a defence. That is a week.** The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing says in twenty-three words that he has asked for eleven coppers a week three times and been refused three times for the same reason, that there is no money in this district that anybody can pay anybody with, and **that he is not asking today and is telling the yard the number so that it knows he knows the number.** Then he does the thing nobody asked for: **he copies the schedule of nine rows in about fifty minutes, in his own hand, on the back of a sheet of his own buying, column heading and eight days and the ninth row with its two words, and gives it to a clerk of nineteen years and says twenty-one words: I gave it up, nobody took it, put both of those down and put down which one of them is true.** A clerk read the copy against the sheet and got nothing wrong. **The man who digs loam says a man who copies a stranger's schedule for nothing is the fourth instrument this district has built and not named and that he is not going to count him, and the count is six.** The registrar says in fifty-three words: **I will not put a holder in this book. I will not put a toll at the ninth place in this book. I will put a day and a sheet and a person who came and asked, and the fourth of those three things is the only one of them that is mine.** **She did not look at him and he did not look at her, and a clerk entered that this was not one of the nine times they had been in the same yard on purpose and is not going to be entered as one.** The man of about thirty at the counter comes for the eleventh time in eight weeks, is asked one question and not asked about the ninth holding, and says in **four seconds** that his house had never heard of a Registry. **A clerk entered that he said it in four seconds and that the registrar said no in four seconds on the fifteenth of the second month and that a clerk is not going to put a number on the two, and that the two bodies are not joined and the refusals to join two things are thirteen and the registrar's own count is twelve and neither of them moved.** A man of fifty-six says two bodies that have never heard of each other are going to meet over a place with no name on a sheet, **and that this district has four documents nobody owns and none of them is a prediction.** **AND ON THE SIXTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE LIST OF THE FIVE WAS READ OUT AGAIN IN A YARD AND A MAN FROM A REGISTRY SAID IN FIFTEEN WORDS THAT IT WAS A GOOD LIST AND THAT HIS OWN WAS NINETEEN PAGES, THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST SAID NO FOR THE SECOND TIME IN FIFTY-EIGHT WORDS AND ASKED FOR IT TO BE WRITTEN DOWN, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM SAID THAT A NO IN A YARD IS A THING THAT HAPPENED AND IS NOT A THING THAT HOLDS.**

## Chapter 0207 — The Fifth Day

**The seventh of April, the fifth day of the man with the portfolio, about fourteen people in the yard. The board has carried the twenty-fourth of December for a hundred and four days, the train has stood four hundred and twenty days, and the days nobody has entered since the twenty-fourth of November stand at a hundred and thirty-four.** The man of about thirty-one says in twenty-one words that the man on the bench has been there since eight, has not asked anybody for anything, and that he has asked him for nothing. **The panel at about eleven says: a lease names a holder and a lease does not name a bearer; a bearer is a person who owns a toll under a stated term; a holder is a name a body has printed, and a name a body has printed is not a person who agreed to be there.** The man of about thirty-one disagrees with the whole of it before he is asked, in forty-four words: **I have been written on four times and I did not ask for any of them and I am not a holder because I do not pay a toll and I am not a bearer because nobody has asked me, and I would like somebody to write down that I do not know the difference and that the four of them do not know it either.** **The registrar puts the first question she has ever put to the stranger, in thirteen words: does a lease say who keeps the thing it is a lease of.** He says no in four seconds and gives the reason in fifty-six words: **a lease says what the holder may do with it; a keeper is not in a lease; a keeper is a person who is on a hill and is not on a page, and there are four generations of them where this lease is and none of them has ever been on a page of mine.** **That four generations is the first stranger's figure entered in this yard, and it came with no date, no year and no place, and it is a rhyme and not a join.** Then a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asks, in the six things, what a Registry does with a no, and the man on the bench comes into the yard for the first time in five days and says in thirty-four words: **we enter it; that is what a Registry does with a no; it writes the no down in the place the asking came from and it dates it and the asking is the answer.** And then he says the rest of it, unasked, and it is the turn of the block: **I did not come for a bearer. I have said since the second day that I did not come for a bearer and your man has refused me one twice in front of nineteen people, and I would like it written down that in five days in this district nobody once asked me what I came for.** **A man of fifty-six says a man who has been refused the wrong thing for five days is not a man who has been refused, and that the man the figure of twenty-one years is against stood in the middle of that yard and said nothing at all for nine seconds, and a clerk entered that he was not asked and that the nine seconds are not a refusal and not a consent and the record says not asked.** The man who digs loam says it flat: **he came for the page; she refused him the page on the second day in thirty-seven words and it was the right refusal and it is still the right one, and this yard has spent five days refusing him a man instead of a page, and the man was never what he asked for, and a yard that cannot tell the difference between a refusal and a habit has not got an instrument and has got a bench.** At about half past two the man with the portfolio says he is going to walk to the place himself and says in sixty-nine words that he does not know how, **that a man who will not name a place cannot be given a road to it, and that he has been sitting on that bench for four nights working out how to be given one without giving the other thing away, and has not managed it.** **The man of about thirty-one then offers a dray at a forge end off Salt Row that has not been out of that lane since the year before last, and says in fifty-seven words that nobody has to pay him and that he would like that written down too.** A man of fifty-six says a favour is not a toll and this district cannot refuse to confuse the two this afternoon. **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale is asked what would go on the dray and says in forty-four words: nothing yet; I have not thought about it and I am not going to think about it in a yard, and a man who offers a cart and has not thought about what goes on it has offered a cart and not a trip.** At about four, in a lane, with no clerk within nine feet: **he asks her whether she refused him the page or refused the Registry, and she says in thirty-two words that a stranger may look and may not carry, that he is not a stranger, that that is the whole of the difference, and that she is not going to say the rest of it in a lane. A man of fifty-six entered that a man who says he is not going to say the rest of it in a lane has said in a lane that there is a rest of it.** **AND ON THE SEVENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE MAN FROM THE REGISTRY SAID IN THIRTY-FOUR WORDS THAT HIS BODY ENTERS A NO AND DATES IT, SAID OUT LOUD THAT HE DID NOT COME FOR A BEARER AND THAT IN FIVE DAYS NOBODY HERE ASKED HIM WHAT HE CAME FOR, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE OFFERED A DRAY HE HAS NOT DRIVEN SINCE THE YEAR BEFORE LAST AND ASKED FOR IT TO BE WRITTEN DOWN THAT NOBODY HAS TO PAY HIM.**

## Chapter 0208 — The Grade

**The eighth of April, the sixth day of the man with the portfolio, about nineteen people in the yard. The board at a hundred and five days, the train at four hundred and twenty-one, the unentered days at a hundred and thirty-five, and the fifth thing this district does not have named out loud again and not paid since.** The strip of paper is on the trestle table, **although the man of about thirty-one said in this yard on the fourth that he was going to keep it in his coat and was not going to have it read out in a yard again, and nobody asked him about it and a clerk is not going to.** The road comes up because a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asks how a man means to walk to a place he will not name: **there is one road in this district that goes up, it goes out past the loams and up past the top field of a farm, and I have known that since I came down the eleven mile road and I have been waiting five days to see whether anybody in this yard would work it out without me.** **The top field is the fourth holding on the delivery sheet and the fourth holding has not taken a drop since the first of the second month, and the toll on the lease is paid at the ninth place, and the ninth holding is the one nobody can account for. A man of about thirty-four who digs loam refuses to join two of those three, out loud and on purpose, and says he is not doing it a third time for a man with a case the size of a hymn book.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says the other half: **a lease pays its toll at the ninth place, the ninth place is not a road, the ninth row of a stranger's own schedule says so in two words, this yard has been using a word it borrowed off a body forty miles from here for five days, and if we are walking up the only road that climbs we are walking past a holding that stopped taking water in the second month and the water that is not coming is going somewhere and nobody in this district can say where — and a road with two hundred yards of soft ground on the top of it and a holding that has not taken a drop are two things a person can put a hand on.** The grade comes up because a man of about thirty-one comes back at about eleven with the answer: **two hundred yards of it at the top, soft since the water came down a channel on the twenty-ninth of the third month, and you cannot pull a dray up two hundred yards of that on four hands and a chain, and I know because I have tried it once.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says there is one yoke in this district and it is on the stones at the top of a hill and has been there since the twenty-fourth of the third month, and the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says in fifty-four words that it is a carrying yoke, that it has two hooks, that it has carried buckets for nine years, that it has never pulled anything in its life, and that a dray wants a draught yoke and this district has not got one.** **The woman of fifty-eight was on that hill at about eight and nobody sent for her and the record says not asked, and the four columns of not-askings are four and none has been ruled since the second of January.** Then Adrian makes his second error of the block: **he asks the registrar to put a name on a line — a day, a road, two hundred yards, a yoke, and the man of about thirty-one — so that the thing about to be done has a person at the end of it instead of a hole.** **She refuses in four seconds and gives the reason before she is asked, in eighty-two words: a name on a line is a name off a sheet; I told a man from a Registry on the second of this month that a holder is a name a body has taken off a sheet; you have spent four months saying a person who has not been asked has not refused; you are asking me to make a man into a bearer by writing him down; I will not do it; and you knew I would not.** A man of fifty-six says a registrar who says no in four seconds has told this man the same thing three times. Then, with no clerk within nine feet: **he says he knew it would not work and asked anyway because he wanted to see whether the yard would do one useful thing by writing, and it will not, and that is the answer and he is not going to pretend he did not know the answer before he asked. She says: then you asked a question you knew the answer to in front of nineteen people, and that is a thing clerks do, and I have been one, and I did not enjoy it either.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam then says the part that had not been said, unasked: **you are about to take a piece of wood off a woman's stones and put it on a cart, and a piece of wood on a cart that came out of a yard is a job, and the job is hers because it came off her stones, and she is not in the cart and she is not in the yard and nobody has said her name since the twenty-fourth of the third month. That is the fifth of the five and it is not a wage, and nobody has priced it because there is no figure in this basin that prices a person who is not in a household.** And the man of about thirty-one says in fifty-one words: **I will go up and get it myself. Nobody has to come with me and nobody has to be asked and I will put it on the cart and I will not burn it and I will not break it and that is all I am going to say about it.** **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says a man who says nobody has to be asked has just said the sentence this district has been saying for four months with a different mouth on it, and that a man who has been told the answer is not the answer and has gone up a hill for it anyway is a man and not a security, and a clerk entered that nobody stopped him and that the not-asking is entered and that she did not rule a fifth column and is not going to.** The woman of fifty-eight is at the top of the hill at about four and does not come down. **AND ON THE EIGHTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE REGISTRAR REFUSED IN FOUR SECONDS TO PUT A NAME ON A LINE FOR A TRIP THAT WAS ABOUT TO BE DONE, A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM SAID THAT A PIECE OF WOOD OFF A WOMAN'S STONES IS A JOB AND THE JOB IS HERS, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE SAID IN FIFTY-ONE WORDS THAT HE WOULD GO UP AND GET IT HIMSELF AND THAT NOBODY HAD TO BE ASKED.**

## Chapter 0209 — The Stones

**The ninth of April, the seventh day of the man with the portfolio, about nineteen people in the yard, and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is at the bottom of the bank with his hands behind him and has not gone up the hill.** **A clerk entered that on the eighth he said in this yard that he would ask the woman of fifty-eight himself in the morning and in his own words, and that he did not ask her, and that nobody asked him why, and that a man who says a thing in a yard at four in the afternoon has said it in a yard and has not made a promise.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam was not asked to go, was not sent for, was in the yard the whole time, and was asked nothing. **The yoke came off the stones at about eleven in the morning with nobody's name on it, and there were about four people on the top of the hill and none of them had come up on purpose. The woman of fifty-eight had been on the hill at about eight and had gone down to the counter at the sluice end of the loams at about a quarter to nine with two buckets and had left the yoke on the stones, and nobody sent for her at any point in the hour and the record says not asked.** The man of about thirty-one says it in the yard at about half past eleven, unasked, in fifty-four words: **I took it off her stones at about eleven and I did not ask her and I want that in the book in my own words and not in yours, and I will carry it down myself and I will not put it on a cart until she has seen it on the cart.** A man of fifty-six says a sentence in a person's own words is not a document this district can produce, and that what is on the top of that hill is a job that was hers and is not hers now, and that this is not a transfer because a transfer is a thing two people agree to and there were not two people on the hill. The man who digs loam says a man who says it afterwards has done one thing better than this district and one thing worse. **She comes up the bank at about half past one with two empty buckets and had not been able to fill them, and stands at the bottom of the yard and does not go into it. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing counted from the bottom of the bank that she had been up it eleven times between about nine in the morning and about one in the afternoon, and was not asked to say he had counted, and said that a yoke lets a person carry two things at once and that without one a person carries one thing at a time, and that a woman who has carried two at a time for nine years does not carry two at a time slower — she carries them one at a time eleven times.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing takes the two buckets off her and carries them up the hill and is not asked to. **She says in sixty-five words: that is my yoke and I have carried four yoke-loads a day up that hill for nine years and there is nobody on that hill but me, and I am not saying no to a man in a yard and I am not saying yes and I am telling you the whole of what I know about it and the whole of it is that.** A man of fifty-six says she has said the same shape of sentence for the third time in eleven days and that the third one is longer and that the length is not progress. **The man of about thirty at the counter comes down the eleven mile road and is not sent for, and this district has no schedule for him and never had one. Asked a new question — not the ninth holding — he says in fifty-three words: my house calls them locks; it has called them that since before anybody living was born; we have a word for the man who keeps one and a word for the night he turns it; and your man from the Registry has been calling them gates in this yard for five days.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says a word a stranger lends you is a word a stranger can take back, and that a man of about thirty at a counter has now given this district a word and a man did not ask him for the man and asked him for the word. **A clerk entered that a word is not a figure, is not a schedule, is not a column and is not a document at all, and that the three documents nobody owns are the printed sheet at the fourth place, the notice about a party of nine who went thirty-four miles east, and a line in a public book with four names on it and a figure under the four names, and that a man gave this district a word instead of a paper and it is not a fourth.** Then Adrian asks for the copy — **the hand copy of the schedule of nine rows, made in about fifty minutes on the back of a sheet of his own buying by a man of about twenty-three who sells nothing and copies for nothing, and given to a clerk, and nobody took it.** **A clerk of nineteen years reads the eight days out and gets them right, and reads them back a second time and gets them right both times:**

> **THE FIRST OF THE FOURTH MONTH. THE SECOND. THE THIRD. THE FOURTH. THE SIXTH. THE EIGHTH. THE TENTH. THE ELEVENTH.**

**A clerk entered that the sheet does not say whether a day under that heading is a day the thing was open or a day it is to be open, that four of the eight had already happened when the schedule was read out on the fifth of this month and four had not, and that nobody in that yard asked for the eight days on the fifth of this month although a clerk of nineteen years was standing in it.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam does the sum out loud and in the ordinary voice, unasked: **eight days in eleven days and three of the eleven with nothing in the column, and one of them a night, and the ninth row is not a road and has a word in it where the other eight have a day, and a run that goes one a night does not stop at eight because it is the eighth. I am not joining that to a road anybody opened in the night nine miles from here. I am saying that eight things are standing open on eight nights in eleven days and that nobody in this basin has said why and that four generations of people who keep them have never been on a page of anybody's.** **AND ON THE NINTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE TOOK A YOKE OFF THE STONES AT THE TOP OF A HILL AT ABOUT ELEVEN IN THE MORNING WITHOUT ASKING THE WOMAN WHO OWNS IT, A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY AT A COUNTER GAVE THIS DISTRICT THE WORD THE HOUSE THIRTY-FOUR MILES EAST USES AND A STRANGER HAS BEEN CALLING SOMETHING ELSE FOR FIVE DAYS, AND A CLERK OF NINETEEN YEARS READ OUT EIGHT DAYS THAT HAD NOT BEEN READ OUT BEFORE.**

## Chapter 0210 — A Dray Going Up A Road

**The tenth of April, the eighth day of the man with the portfolio, about nineteen people in the yard, and the yoke standing against the tailboard of a dray at the bottom of the lane in the open where anybody in four hundred yards can walk round it and look at it. The board at a hundred and seven days, the train at four hundred and twenty-three, the unentered days at a hundred and thirty-seven, and the last of the eight days in a column headed OPEN is tomorrow.** A clerk entered that the yoke is against the tailboard and not on it, that it is a carrying yoke and not a draught yoke and is not the thing that would pull a dray, and that it is on the cart because the man of about thirty-one said on the ninth that he would not put it on a cart until the woman who owns it had seen it on a cart, and she did, and that the reason is not a use. **She sees it at about ten and stands at the bottom of the lane for about a minute and a quarter and does not go up to it and does not touch it and says nothing at all, and about nineteen people watch her not say anything and a clerk entered the not-saying and entered that nobody filled it.** The man of about thirty-one says in fifty words: **it goes up the road in the morning and I will bring it back down the same way and I will put it on those stones myself and I have not got a way to pay you for the two days and I am not going to pretend I have.** **A man of fifty-six says a man who says out loud in front of nineteen people that he has not got a way to pay a person has done the only thing this district has an instrument for, and that the district's instrument is a yard and a yard is a room.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says she has now said the same shape of sentence three times in eleven days to three different men, **and that a woman who says the same true thing three times is not a woman who has run out of things to say and is a woman who has run out of people who can price one day, and that this is the fifth of the five and it is not a wage and it is not going to be.** Then Adrian puts her in the six things for the fifth time and it is the third asking of the same shape: **he asks whether she will come up the hill and walk the yoke down the bank herself, and she says no in four seconds and gives the reason in thirty-two words: then it is not mine to walk down, I have said for eleven days that I cannot say what a day of it is worth and this is a day of it.** A man of fifty-six says a refusal with a reason is not one of the six refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given and the six are six. **Then he asks the registrar for one thing and it is not a name on a line, and she says yes in four seconds and gives the reason in sixty-one words: put the eight days in my book; a day a stranger has printed is a day somebody is going to be told about; a day in a book is a day a person can be asked about; and this basin has never had a book in which a printed day could be found by anybody who wanted to find it.** **Eight days went into a book in that office at about eleven in the morning and a clerk read them back and got them right, and they are days and not figures and not a schedule and not a toll, and the man who asked was not asked why he wanted them.** The panel at about eleven says: **a thing taken from a person who was not asked is not a toll; a toll is a thing a named person pays under a stated term; a yoke-load is a job; a job taken is still a job, and the person it was taken from is still the person who does it.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam reads the last line out loud in the ordinary voice and **does not disagree with it**, and a clerk entered that this is the first of these that nobody in this yard has disagreed with and that she is not going to make anything of it. The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing is in the yard, is not sent for and is not asked anything, **and says that he copied eight days and does not know what a lock is and would have copied it just the same, and that this is not a thing anybody is owed money for, and that he has said so three times and is not going to say it a fourth.** **And on the eleventh of April, out of a night with a hard frost on it, the same as the first, the dray goes up the road at about half past five in the morning with two people in it — the man of about thirty-one on the box and the man with the portfolio beside him with the case under his arm — and the yoke is lashed to the tailboard with about nine feet of line, and a clerk of nineteen years was at the bottom of the lane on her own account, and nobody watched it go except a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat at the third house up the lane from the scale, who was not sent for and was not asked anything.** A man of fifty-six says a dray going up a road with two people in it is the ordinary case and has been the ordinary case for eighteen months, **and that the yoke on the tailboard is not the ordinary case, and that the whole of the difference between those two sentences is that one of them had an owner and the other one did not, and that the owner was asked yesterday whether she would walk it down the bank herself and said no in four seconds and was not asked whether it could go on a cart, and that a thing a person has been asked about, has not agreed to, and is being done anyway is a thing this district has done twice in two months, once in the second month with a round of about ninety houses and once this week with a piece of wood off a stone, and that the two are not joined.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says the last of it to the lane and not to anybody: **eight days in eleven days and one of them a night and the eighth one is tonight and the ninth row is not a road and has a word in it where the other eight have a day, and a dray is going up the only road that climbs with a thing off a woman's stones on the back of it, and the two of those are not joined and I am not going to join them in a lane at six in the morning, and I would like somebody to write down that I said I would not and said it anyway, in my own words.** **Nobody wrote it down, because the man it was asked of is a man of fifty-six and not a clerk and has said so about himself eleven times in four months, and a clerk entered that he asked, that it was not written, and that the asking is entered and the not-writing is entered, and that the count of things this district does not have is five and the count of instruments built and not named is six and the count of documents nobody owns is three and the protected count is five and no sixth was added and the conditions with no end on it are four and there is still no rate in this basin that turns a year into coppers and none of the seven of those moved.** **AND ON THE ELEVENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH, WHICH IS THE LAST DAY IN A COLUMN HEADED OPEN ON A SHEET A STRANGER DATED THE FIRST OF IT, A DRAY WENT UP A ROAD WITH TWO PEOPLE IN IT AND A YOKE ON THE TAILBOARD THAT NOBODY ASKED THE OWNER ABOUT, AND NOBODY WROTE DOWN THE ONLY THING THAT WAS SAID ABOUT IT.**

## Chapter 0211 — The Day After

**The twelfth of April, the first day in eleven with nothing at all in a column headed OPEN, and about nine people in the yard, and two of the people who had stood in it every morning since the second of April are not in it.** The board has carried the twenty-fourth of December for a hundred and nine days, the train has stood four hundred and twenty-five days, and the days nobody has entered stand at a hundred and thirty-nine. A clerk of nineteen years enters that a dray on a road on somebody's account is not a toll and is not a bearer and is not one of the five things this district does not have, and that nobody in that yard has said where the road goes or when the dray comes back. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam does the sum out loud, in seventy-six words, and says that eleven days is eleven days and today is the first of them with nothing at all in the column, and that a run that goes one a night does not stop at eight because it is the eighth, and that he said that in a lane on the eleventh and nobody wrote it down and here it is again in a yard with about nine people in it and it is still not written down.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing **says he is going to ask the woman of fifty-eight, on the sixteenth, in his own words and not in the six things, and says the day out loud so that a clerk can enter that a day was said**; a man of fifty-six answers that a man who names a day in a yard has made a plan and not a promise, and a yard is the only instrument this district has and about nine people were in it this morning and that is the whole of the instrument. The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing is asked what a lock is and says in sixty-three words that he does not know, that he has copied the word nine times and knows it is spelt with a K and that the column it is over says OPEN, and that he would rather have it written down that he has got it than have somebody decide he has not. The woman of fifty-eight is on the hill at eight, comes down the bank at about half past nine, fills two buckets at the counter at the foot of the bank and does not go into the yard. **A man of fifty-six says the strip of paper is in a coat that went up the road at half past five yesterday morning and has not been in this district since, and that a fencing man has just asked a question about a piece of paper to a man of fifty-six who is not its owner**; a clerk enters that a document somebody owns is not one of the three this district does not own and that the three did not move. On the lane four miles down, the butt is at four days and a woman of about thirty-eight who keeps that building says in thirty-nine words that it is for the water off the roof and that she has not asked anybody for a fifth day and is not going to. The registrar is in the yard at about four and says nothing at all for the first time in four months, and **a man of thirty-four who mends fencing enters that a registrar saying nothing is not a refusal and is not a consent and is not a column and is not one of the nine refusals to read.** The six at the sluice end stand at one signed, two refused, three not asked again, unchanged since the twenty-first of last month, which is twenty-two days. **AND ON THE TWELFTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE COUNT OF THINGS THIS DISTRICT DOES NOT HAVE IS FIVE AND THE COUNT OF INSTRUMENTS BUILT AND NOT NAMED IS SIX AND THE COUNT OF DOCUMENTS NOBODY OWNS IS THREE AND NONE OF THE THREE MOVED.**

## Chapter 0212 — The Road House

**The thirteenth of April, a day with nothing on anybody's page, and about fourteen people in the yard, and the man who keeps the road comes in at about half past ten because he has walked nine miles to do it.** He has walked it five times and the fifth was this morning. The board at a hundred and ten days, the train at four hundred and twenty-six, the unentered days at a hundred and forty. **A clerk enters that this district asked a man who keeps a road one question on the first of April and got one answer and has not asked him a second thing in the twelve days since, and that a man who has been asked one question and given one answer is not a man who has been asked.** He takes out a book about the length of his forearm written in more than one hand, and reads eight nights out of it, and a clerk reads them back and gets them right:

> **THE FIRST OF THIS MONTH. THE SECOND. THE THIRD. THE FIFTH. THE SEVENTH. THE NINTH. THE TWELFTH. THE THIRTEENTH.**

**Asked for the ninth, he refuses and gives the reason in forty-two words: *That one is a night another man wrote and not a night I walked, and I keep a road and I am not a keeper of a book, and I am not taking a man's page round a yard like this one.*** A clerk enters that a refusal with a reason is not one of the refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given, that a man who keeps a road is not a clerk of a house, that the seven are seven this morning and the eighth has not happened yet, and that he has refused nothing without a reason at any time this yard has been keeping him in a column. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refuses the comparison, in ninety-nine words: he has been given eight nights he walked and there is a stranger's sheet with eight days printed on it and the two are not the same kind of thing, and a second refusal of the same join is the same refusal and the refusals to join two things are thirteen and are not made fourteen by a man refusing twice.** A man of fifty-six says two records that are not about the same thing cannot agree and cannot disagree. **The route keeper then gives the district two more words, unasked, in fifty: his house has a word for the man who turns one and a word for the night he turns it, and neither of those two words is on any page, and the word he has just given is one you can write down and the other two are not.** Asked why he walked on the twelfth and the thirteenth, he says in forty-six words that he walks it when there is water in the channel and there was none on those two nights, and that this is a reason and is not a keeper's reason and he would know. Asked whether he saw a dray go up, he says no in thirty-six words: he was nine miles off it on the eleventh and was on it this morning at about five and there was no wheel track on it that had not been there when he came down. A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households says in fifty-seven words that a man who keeps a road keeps it the way he keeps a tally and neither is anybody's clerk. **AND ON THE THIRTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A MAN WHO KEEPS A ROAD GAVE THIS DISTRICT EIGHT NIGHTS HE HAD WALKED AND REFUSED THE NINTH WITH A REASON IN FORTY-TWO WORDS AND SAID THAT TWO WORDS HIS HOUSE HAS ARE NOT ON ANY PAGE.**

**REVIEW REPAIR ON THIS ENTRY. The line that said the man of about thirty at a counter gave the word *locks* four days ago and did not give the other two was false on the page: Chapter 209 has him giving the word for the man who keeps one and the word for the night he turns it. He now gave the word and gave two more with it, and a man nine miles off gave the same two again this morning out of a different house, and the clerk's entry says two houses have now given this district the same pair of words in five days. Two houses saying the same two words is the kind of thing this district exists to notice and it had been going unnoticed.**
## Chapter 0213 — The Standing Orders

**The fourteenth of April, a day with nothing on anybody's page until about half past three in the afternoon, and about fourteen people in the yard. A clerk enters that the fourteenth would have been the thirteenth day of a man with a portfolio, counting the day he came in, and that he is not in this district, and that a day a man is not here is not a day he has been here.** The board at a hundred and eleven days, the train at four hundred and twenty-seven, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-one, and the board has not been washed since the twenty-fourth of December and nobody has said out loud that it ought to be. **The dray comes down at about half past three with one man on the box, the yoke lashed to the tailboard with about nine feet of line, and the man with the portfolio is not on it and the case is not on it.** Asked what happened at the top of the road, the man of about thirty-one says in fifty-two words that he was given a sheet, that he did not ask for it and has not read it and is not going to say who gave it to him, and that the man who went up with him is not on this cart and he did not ask him to come down either. **Before the sheet is read, the man of about thirty-one puts the strip of paper on the trestle table face up, and nobody asks him about it, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam says in sixty-six words that it is a document somebody owns that nobody will produce and the three this district does not own are three.** A clerk reads the sheet out in one run of words and reads it back and gets nothing wrong:

> **THE STANDING ORDERS OF THE NINE LOCKS. NINE OF THEM.**
> **A NUMBER IN A COLUMN ON THE LEFT AND A NIGHT IN A COLUMN ON THE RIGHT AND A COLUMN HEADED KEEPER BETWEEN THEM.**
> **THE NINE NIGHTS ARE THE FIRST OF THIS MONTH. THE SECOND. THE THIRD. THE FOURTH. THE SIXTH. THE EIGHTH. THE TENTH. THE ELEVENTH. AND THE TWENTIETH.**
> **THE COLUMN HEADED KEEPER CARRIES A HOUSE ON EVERY ONE OF THE NINE ROWS AND A PERSON ON NONE OF THEM.**

**The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says the whole of what it is, unasked, and no man counts it because the chapter does not put a number on it: a stranger's schedule has eight days and a word in the ninth row and this has nine nights, and the eight of them are the same eight in the same order, so two lists of nine that agree on eight are not one list, and the one they disagree about is the ninth, and the ninth is the twentieth of this month, and that is six days off and nobody in this district can get to it.** A man of fifty-six says a man has just put two pieces of paper next to each other in a yard and that is not a join and is not a rhyme and is a thing anybody can check. Asked whether the house in the keeper's column is the same house nine times or nine houses, the man of about thirty-one says in fifty-four words that he was handed a sheet by a person who did not give a name, that he was not asked a question about any of it, and that he is not going to work out what a column of houses means from the back of a cart in a yard. **Then a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says the rest of it, in ninety-six words: there is a column on that sheet headed with a word and not a name and it carries a house nine times and a person no times, and a man of about forty-four told this yard on the seventh that there are four generations of keepers and that not one of them has ever been on a page of his, and so no keeper has ever been on a page of anybody's, and it is not because pages forget people, it is because the page has a column and the column carries a house.** A man of fifty-six says a Registry prints a house and does not print a person, and that this district's own records office does the same, and that the keepers' page has been doing it for four generations, and **that those three are the same sentence told three times by three bodies and are one finding and not three, and that the three documents nobody owns did not move.** **Asked whether the twentieth ought to go on the board, the man of about thirty-four who digs loam says no in four seconds and gives the reason in fifty-five words: a date is not a finding, the seventh line at the ninth place is ruled and has nothing on it, and a printed day is not a finding.** A man of fifty-six then says that there is still no rate in this basin that turns a year into coppers, that there never was one, and that the ninth of these things is about to be a year somebody has to pay for, and he would like to hear it now. **AND ON THE FOURTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A DRAY CAME DOWN A ROAD WITH ONE MAN ON THE BOX AND A PRINTED SHEET UNDER THE YOKE, AND A CLERK READ OUT NINE NIGHTS WHERE A STRANGER'S SHEET HAS EIGHT, AND THE NINTH OF THEM IS THE TWENTIETH OF THIS MONTH.**

## Chapter 0214 — The Fifteenth Time

**The fifteenth of April, a day with nothing on the printed sheet and one night in a road house's book that came down a road yesterday, and about nineteen people in the yard, and the man of about thirty at the counter comes in at about a quarter to eleven and is not sent for.** The board at a hundred and twelve days, the train at four hundred and twenty-eight, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-two. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing gives the figures out loud and a clerk of nineteen years derives the man's ordinal on the page: the fifteenth time is the fifteenth, and the counting runs eleventh on the second, twelfth on the fourth, thirteenth on the sixth, fourteenth on the ninth, the tenth was on the twenty-seventh of last month, and a hundred and three days separate the second of January and this morning, which is fourteen weeks and five days, and the district has been carrying this man's visits in a state file for four months and has never once counted them off a day.** A man of fifty-six says a count that is carried is a count that is remembered and a count that is remembered is a guess with a date on it. He brings four sheets, which is what he brings, and is asked in the six things, in front of about nineteen people, about the ninth holding on the delivery sheet, and refuses with no reason. **A clerk reads out what that did: the seventh refusal about the ninth holding and the eighth refusal with no reason a clerk of a house has given, and the count of the eight moved today for the first time since the twenty-fourth of last month, and the two figures are not the same figure and are not added together, and this morning is the first time in twenty-two days that a figure in this district moved because somebody said no.** A man of fifty-six says the moving of it cost this district nothing and bought a man of about thirty a day he had to sit in a yard. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing then gives the reason out loud, in sixty-four words: the man has been refused seven times in four months and has never once been asked why, and he is not asking him why this morning, and he wants it in the book that the reason he gives no reason is that nobody in this district has ever asked him for one, and that that is a reason and it is his and not his.** A clerk enters that a reason one man in a yard gives about another man is not a reason the other man gave, **and that the eighth is an eighth and not a ninth, and that the two refusals of a bearing the man of about thirty-one gave on the fourth and the sixth both carried a reason and neither is in the count of eight.** **The two lists are put side by side at about one in the afternoon because a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked for it, and a clerk writes them in two columns on one sheet and reads them back and gets nothing wrong:**

> **IN BOTH. THE FIRST. THE SECOND. THE THIRD.**
> **IN THE ROAD HOUSE BOOK AND NOT ON THE PRINTED SHEET. THE FIFTH. THE SEVENTH. THE NINTH. THE TWELFTH. THE THIRTEENTH.**
> **ON THE PRINTED SHEET AND NOT IN THE ROAD HOUSE BOOK. THE FOURTH. THE SIXTH. THE EIGHTH. THE TENTH. THE ELEVENTH. THE TWENTIETH.**

**A clerk enters that three of the eight are in both lists and five are in the road house's book and not on the printed sheet and six are on the printed sheet and not in the book, and that of the five that are only in the book, three are the fifth, the seventh and the ninth of this month, which are the three days of eleven a stranger's column headed OPEN leaves empty, and that the other two are the twelfth and the thirteenth, which are after the last day any printed column in this district carries.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says the plainest version of it, in eighty-eight words: three is not eight, a man who walks a road eight times and a paper that prints eight days are two different kinds of record, one of them a man and the other one a body, and a district that has both of them in one yard on the same afternoon and cannot say which of them it is looking at has got a yard and not an instrument, and a yard is the fourth thing this district does not have and is the oldest.** A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households says in fifty-two words that the house has to ask him, that he has been asked four times and has not been told, and that a fourth time is not a fifth. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing then asks what a house is doing in a column headed KEEPER, and gets thirty-eight words: **a house is what keeps a lock when a man cannot, and my house has never said a keeper's name on anything and I have asked about it twice in thirty-four miles and nobody would tell me a name.** **AND ON THE FIFTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE COUNT OF REFUSALS WITH NO REASON A CLERK OF A HOUSE HAS GIVEN MOVED FROM SEVEN TO EIGHT FOR THE FIRST TIME SINCE THE TWENTY-FOURTH OF THE MONTH BEFORE LAST.**

## Chapter 0215 — A Body That Writes

**The sixteenth of April, a day with nothing on the printed sheet and nothing in the road house's book after the ninth, and about nineteen people in the yard, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is not in it — he went up the bank at about half past eight with nothing in his hands, and nobody went with him and the record says not asked.** The board at a hundred and thirteen days, the train at four hundred and twenty-nine, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-three, and he gives the figures at about half past ten when he comes back and says he is not going to say what he asked until he does. **He says what he asked, in sixty words: he asked her how many of the ninety houses she can still name, he did not ask her in the six things, he did not ask her what she wants, and he has not asked her that three times in four months and was not going to ask her a fourth time about a thing he cannot price.** A man of fifty-six enters that a man said in a yard on the eighth of this month that he would ask her himself in the morning and in his own words, and did not on the ninth, and nobody asked him why for three days, **and that he asked her on the sixteenth in his own words and named the day when he said he would do it, and that the two of those are the whole difference between a man and a yard.** She answers at about half past eleven, unasked, in forty-one words: **I carried water to about ninety houses for nine years and eleven of them still have a door on them and I could go back to those eleven tomorrow and I am not going to be asked for the other seventy-nine.** A clerk enters that *about ninety* is not a figure and a number taken out of a thing with a word in front of it is not a subtraction, and that eleven is a figure and nobody has done anything with it yet. **Asked whether he was to write the eleven down, she says no in four seconds and gives the reason in seventy-three words: a name said once in a yard is a name four hundred people have and a name said twice is a name a counter can ask for, and you taught that to this yard on the third of this month, and I am not saying eleven names in front of nineteen people so that a thing I have kept in my head since the second month is in a book in a room.** **The letter came down on the dray on the fourteenth under the same fold with a seal on it and sat unopened for two days, and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said a yard reads a document when somebody asks it to and nobody asked it. The man the figure of twenty-one years is against reads it out at about a quarter to two in front of about nineteen people, and a man of fifty-six said before it was read that a document is read out and not discussed:**

> **TO THE PEOPLE OF THE PLACE THIS DISTRICT IS. THE COUNCIL OF THE NINE LOCKS SENDS GREETING AND APOLOGISES FOR THE FIRST OF THESE COMING FOUR MONTHS LATE.**
> **THE COUNCIL IS A BODY AND NOT A PLACE AND THIS LETTER DOES NOT SAY WHERE THE COUNCIL SITS OR WHEN IT SITS AGAIN.**
> **NINE THINGS HOLD BACK A WATER ABOVE FOUR DISTRICTS AND NO PERSON HOLDS ANY OF THEM, AND THE COUNCIL ASKS WHETHER THIS DISTRICT WILL SEND A MAN TO A COUNCIL.**
> **THE COUNCIL ASKS FOR AN ANSWER BY THE LAST DAY OF THIS MONTH. THE COUNCIL SENDS ITS REGARDS AGAIN.**

**A clerk reads it back and gets nothing wrong. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing then says, in fifty-four words, the thing that was actually in the yard: a body that writes to a village is a body that knows the village has something, and it has known for four months because it apologises for the first of these being late, and nobody in this basin is going to tell me that a body apologises for a thing it has not counted.** A man of about thirty-four who digs loam says that this yard has been unable to produce a person on demand for the sixteen days since the thirty-first of last month, and that a name on a line is a name off a sheet, and there is not one name in this district that anybody in this district has the right to put on a line for that letter. A clerk enters that the registrar has refused in four seconds three times in eight weeks and four days, on the fifteenth of the second month, the second of this month and the eighth, **and that a letter asking for a man is not a refusal to read and is not the tenth.** The woman of fifty-eight refuses in advance to have anything to say to a body she has never had a letter from. **And a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says that the Council wants its answer by the last day of this month and a man from a Registry said on the fifth that he has to be at a place with no name on it on that same day, and that he is not joining those two things and is saying both out loud so that somebody else has to.** A clerk enters that the fourteenth is a Tuesday by the road house's reckoning and that no weekday is attached to any date in this district. **AND ON THE SIXTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A MAN ASKED THE WOMAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT HOW MANY OF THE NINETY HOUSES SHE CAN STILL NAME, AND SHE SAID ELEVEN, AND THE FIRST LETTER THIS DISTRICT HAS HAD FROM ANYTHING LIKE A COUNCIL WAS READ OUT IN A YARD AND WAS NOT DISCUSSED.**

**REVIEW REPAIR ON THIS ENTRY. The man of fifty-six's line said *the eleventh of the five things this district does not have* and the list has five entries and the fifth of them is a way to pay a person who is not in a household. It now says the fifth, and the count of the five never moved either way, which is why nothing caught it.**
## Chapter 0216 — Eleven Houses

**The seventeenth of April, a day with nothing on the printed sheet and nothing in the road house's book, and about nineteen people in the yard, and the woman of fifty-eight is in it before the yard is asked for anything.** The board at a hundred and fourteen days, the train at four hundred and thirty, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-four. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says, unasked, that this yard is going to give her eleven houses of the round this morning and is going to ask her first, and that he wants the asking written down as the whole of the reason and not the eleven.** A man of fifty-six says this yard took a job off a woman in the third month without asking her and spent the twenty-four days afterwards calling the not-asking a column, **and that a yard that about nineteen people can stand in and decide something about a woman who is not in it is a door, and that the door which got used last month was not the one anybody was proud of, and that this morning the same yard is deciding something about the same woman in the same room and the whole of the difference is that she is standing in it.** A clerk enters that this is the first thing this district has done in four months that it got right, and that a yard is not an instrument and the six instruments this district built and not named did not become a seventh. **Asked in the six things whether she will take eleven houses of her round, she says yes in four seconds and gives the reason in sixty words: because she said on the first of this month that she cannot say what a day of it is worth and has not said it once this morning, and eleven of the ninety still have a door on them, and she is not going to be thanked by a yard in front of all of them for being sensible.** **The panel at about eleven says: a round is a job and not a figure, eleven is not a round, a round that is smaller is not a round; a figure that prices one day of a person who is not in a household is a wage, and a wage is a figure, and a district with no figure has no wage; and a page that names a house names a thing that can be asked, a page that names a person names a thing that can be found, and a page that names neither names a thing that was not written. The woman of fifty-eight disagrees with the first line of it, out loud, in fifty-one words: I am not a round and I never said I was, and you cannot make a smaller one of me and call it the same thing, and if you want to call it eleven houses then call it eleven houses and stop pretending you have given me back what I had.** A clerk enters that this is the third time in this volume a person has disagreed with one of these, and that one of these came on the tenth of this month and nobody disagreed with it. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam then says the plainest thing in the yard, in ninety-two words: nobody in this basin can say what one day of eleven houses is worth, and that is not a hole in this district's book, that is the fifth of the five things it does not have, and a district that hands a woman a job on the understanding that nobody knows what the job costs has not handed her a job, it has handed her a second thing she cannot refuse without standing in a yard and saying so.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says that eleven is not four and four is not eleven and nobody is going to add them together and call it fifteen, and that the four are still standing. The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale, who was in the yard and was not asked anything, is asked what four houses of a round is worth a day and gives the same answer she gave on the fourth of this month, in forty-three words: **I have weighed nine sacks a day for nine years and I have never weighed a day, and four is not eleven and eleven is not ninety and nobody in this yard is going to make me weigh one of them this afternoon.** **A clerk of nineteen years then does the arithmetic out loud and reads it back, for the first time in a yard: ABOUT NINETY, OF WHICH ELEVEN, WHICH LEAVES ABOUT SEVENTY-NINE.
