AND ABOUT NINETY IS NOT A FIGURE, SO THAT IS NOT A SUBTRACTION, AND THE ROUND IS NOT COMING BACK.** Asked whether the eleven are above the channel or all of them, the woman of fifty-eight says in fifty-three words that six are above the channel and five are below it, and that the water came down about a foot and a half and took the five, and that she has not carried for the five since the twenty-fourth of last month. **AND ON THE SEVENTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH THIS YARD GAVE A WOMAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT ELEVEN HOUSES OF A ROUND OF ABOUT NINETY AND ASKED HER FIRST, AND SHE TOOK THEM, AND NOBODY COULD SAY WHAT ONE DAY OF THEM WAS WORTH.**

## Chapter 0217 — The Road Went Up And Came Back

**The eighteenth of April, a day with nothing on the printed sheet and nothing in the road house's book, and about nineteen people in the yard, and a man of about thirty-four who digs loam has been up the only road that climbs and back down before any of them arrived.** The board at a hundred and fifteen days, the train at four hundred and thirty-one, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-five. **A clerk enters that the fourth holding has not taken a drop since the first of the second month, which is seventy-six days, that the channel at the head of that field has been dry since the same day, and that the man who digs loam opened a sluice for that holding on the night of the thirteenth of last month, which is thirty-six days ago, and that nobody has asked him to do it again and he has not.** He says it himself, unasked, in seventy words: he went up a road with a climb on it, stood at the bottom of two hundred yards of soft ground, looked at a field and came back down, and he has not got a finding, and a road is a place and a place is not an instrument, and he is not going to go up it again on a Friday either. A clerk enters that a man said a day of the week and that no weekday is attached to any date in this district. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing then names the third join and refuses it, and a man of about thirty-four who digs loam's third refusal of the same join in twelve days does not make a fourteenth anything.** The man of about thirty-one is asked where the road goes and refuses in seventy words with a reason. **The registrar of this district's records office was in the yard and came on her own account, and at about twenty to twelve she asked him for one thing in a lane behind the forge end off Salt Row where there was no clerk within nine feet of either of them and nobody counted either of what was said: she wanted the page of the ward market's own book where the figure of twenty-one years came off on the thirty-first of last month, in his own hand, to put in her own book, and she was asking him for it and was not telling him why. He gave her the other side of the same leaf and said that the figure is not on it and the date is on it and his own hand is on it and there is nothing written under it and he has not written anything under it, and that it is a page and it is not the one she asked for.** A clerk entered afterwards that two people said two things in a lane and that nobody counted them, and a man of fifty-six said that a man who gives a woman a different page from the one she asked for has told her what a page says and has not told her what she asked about, **and that the two of them have been in the same yard at the same hour on purpose nine times since the eighteenth of December and this was not one of the nine.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says in fifty-two words that she can weigh the water in both buckets and cannot weigh the walk up the bank eleven times. **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat says in sixty-one words that she stood in the cold because she was awake and that the clerk is not to make it into anything in a yard.** The man of about thirty-one is asked whether the man who gave him the sheet said anything about a man, and says in fifty-two words that he did not say a word about a man or about water, and that he put a folded sheet under a yoke and pointed at it and went back up the hill. **AND ON THE EIGHTEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A WOMAN ASKED FOR A PAGE AND WAS GIVEN A DIFFERENT ONE, AND NOBODY COUNTED EITHER SENTENCE.**

## Chapter 0218 — The Night Before

**The nineteenth of April, the day before the ninth of the nine, and about nineteen people in the yard. The board at a hundred and sixteen days, the train at four hundred and thirty-two, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-six, and the last day of this month is eleven days off and the ninth of the nine nights is tomorrow and this district has no instrument for tomorrow.** A clerk enters that the ninth of the nine nights went into that office on the fourteenth in a printed column headed with a word and not a name on it, and that this district put the book there on purpose in the third week of the month, and that a district which puts a day in a book and cannot get anybody to the day is the ordinary case. **The panel at about eleven says: a dray on a road is a cart, a cart is not a bearer, a person on a cart is a person and not a figure; a road with no term on it is a place and not an instrument, and a place may be walked and may not be asked; and a body that asks for a man cannot be answered with a name, and a name on a line is a name off a sheet. Nobody disagreed with it, and a clerk entered that nobody disagreed and that she is not going to make anything of it, and that this is the second of these in this volume that nobody has disagreed with, the first having come on the tenth of this month, which is nine days ago.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam then says, in a hundred words, that he has disagreed with one of these in fourteen days and has not disagreed with one, and that the one he has not disagreed with is the one about a person, and that he is not going to say any more about that today than he has just said.** The man of about thirty-one says he is going up at about half past five tomorrow morning on his own account and is not taking anybody, and says *I am not going to be asked about it this morning* before anybody has asked him. **A man of fifty-six answers that a man who says that before anybody has asked him is a man who has learned something in a month, and that a letter with a seal on it that asks for an answer by the last day of this month has a day on it and is the opposite of a condition with no end on it.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam then says the thing the whole of the last ten days has been standing behind, and a man of fifty-six is asked to write it in his own words and cannot, and the not-writing is entered for the second time in nine days. **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against is asked, in the six things, in front of about nineteen people, whether this district will send a man to a council, and refuses in seventy-eight words with a reason: he is not going to say no in a yard about a thing nobody has asked him properly, and a body that wants a man out of this district has to come to this district and put the question to him in a room with a clerk in it, and he is not going to be a man who is sent to a council because about nineteen people stood in a yard and somebody read out a letter.** A clerk enters that a refusal with a reason is not one of the refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given, that the count of eight did not move, and that a man who says he will not be sent to a council by a yard has not said he will not go to a council. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam adds that the letter asks for a man and not a bearer and not a name and not a holder, and that it is the only paper anybody in this basin has sent them that asks for a person without a column to print one in.** The woman of fifty-eight says in twenty-four words that she will do eleven, one at a time, eleven times, and that she has been doing one at a time eleven times since the ninth of this month anyway. **The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing is in the yard and is owed eleven coppers a week and has said he will not say it a fifth time, and the district is not going to pay him, and both of those are entered and neither is a finding.** **AND ON THE NINETEENTH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A PANEL CAME INTO A YARD THAT NOBODY HAD ASKED FOR AND NOBODY DISAGREED WITH, AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM ASKED FOR THE LAST TRUE THING HE HAS SAID TO BE WRITTEN DOWN FOR THE SECOND TIME IN EIGHT DAYS AND IT WAS NOT WRITTEN DOWN.**

**REVIEW REPAIR ON THIS ENTRY. The clerk's entry said the ninth of the nine nights went into her book in a printed column headed with a keeper's name, and the sheet prints a column headed KEEPER and the block's finding is that the column carries a house nine times and a person no times. It now says the column is headed with a word and not a name.**
## Chapter 0219 — The Ninth

**The twentieth of April, which is the ninth of the nine nights on a sheet a body printed. The dray went up the only road in this district that climbs at about half past five in the morning with one man on the box and nothing else on the cart, and the yoke was not on it.** A clerk of nineteen years was at the bottom of the lane on her own account and a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat was at her own door and was not asked anything, and a clerk enters that a yoke which was on a tailboard in this lane on the tenth is in a forge being looked at by the man who owns it, and that nobody put it on the cart and nobody asked the man who owns it whether it should go. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says a man who does not take a yoke up a road has done one thing this district did not expect and nobody is going to be told what it was.** About nine people are in the yard at about half past ten; the board at a hundred and seventeen days, the train at four hundred and thirty-three, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-seven, and **not one person in this district can see whether it happened.** On the lane four miles down, the butt at the back of the brick building is at nine days and the tank four hundred yards from it is within a hand's breadth of its lip for the second time since the first of the second month and the crack is running, and a clerk enters both figures and that the butt was at nine days on the seventeenth of last month and at four days on the first of this month, and that a figure which describes a butt does not describe a night nine miles off, and that she entered the figure and did not enter a finding because nobody found one. **Asked whether he wants a fifth mend in it now, the man of about fifty-five who keeps that building says no in about two seconds and gives a reason in fifty words: a tank with a mark on it is a tank I can see and a tank with a mend in it is a tank that comes out in three pieces, and I have said that in January and on the first of this month and I am saying it now.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says the plainest thing about the tank, in a hundred and four words: a tank filling is a river doing what a river does and it is not a thing standing open on a hill nine miles off, and I have put those two next to each other twice today and I am telling this yard that I have put them next to each other twice, and a resemblance noticed a second time is not a finding and a third time is a habit, and I am not going to let a pail of water be the witness.** The man of about fifty-five says, unasked, that he has nine years of beds in that building and has not lost one and is not going to lose one because a crack is wet, and that he is fifty-five and has stopped expecting to know where the water comes from. **The woman of fifty-eight carries two buckets up the bank eleven times between about seven and about one, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing does not carry them for her, and a man of fifty-six says a man who has stopped doing a thing he was never asked to do has not refused anything and the record says not asked.** She is asked in the six things whether she is going to say anything to the Council when it writes and says in twenty-eight words that she has not got anything to say to a body she has never had a letter from and is not going to be asked to have one. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing goes up the road at about five in the afternoon to look and will not say when he will be back, and the man who digs loam says in sixty-two words that four people have gone up that road in ten days and not one of them went up it to ask anybody anything, and that a road that is walked and not asked on is a road and not a corridor.** **AND ON THE TWENTIETH OF THE FOURTH MONTH A DRAY WENT UP THE ONLY ROAD IN THIS DISTRICT THAT CLIMBS WITH A YOKE LEFT IN A FORGE, AND A TANK CAME UP WITHIN A HAND'S BREADTH OF ITS LIP, AND NOBODY SAID THE TWO THINGS WERE THE SAME THING.**

## Chapter 0220 — Nine Days

**The twenty-first of April, a day with nothing on the printed sheet and nothing in the road house's book, and about nineteen people in the yard, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing had been up the only road that climbs since about five yesterday afternoon and had come down it in the dark and had not slept.** The board at a hundred and eighteen days, the train at four hundred and thirty-four, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-eight, and **the last day of this month is nine days off and there are two things falling due on it and this district can answer neither.** **A clerk enters that a letter from a body that is not a place asks for a man by the last day of this month, and that a man from a Registry said on the fifth that he has to be at a place with no name on it on that same day and there is nobody at the place but him, and that a man at a counter has refused the ninth holding seven times since the second of January and a body that prints a column headed OPEN has never once been asked to send anything, and that a second refusal of the same join is the same refusal and there have been four in twelve days and they are one.** **He says what he went up for, unasked, in eighty-two words: the channel at the head of the top field has about a foot of water in it and has been dry since the first of the second month, and the fourth holding took a drop sometime in the night, and there was no dray on that road and no yoke on it and no man in this district but me.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam says it out loud before anybody can find it, in eighty-two words: that is the third time this month somebody has put a wet thing next to a night nine miles off, and every one of the three was put there by a man with a bucket, a pail or a channel, and a third time is a habit, and he will keep saying it until somebody in there can tell him which of the three of them is a keeper.** The man of about thirty at a counter did not come, and a clerk enters that this district has no schedule for him and never had one, that a man who does not come has not refused and cannot be counted either way, and that his fifteenth time down that road was the fifteenth of this month and the sixteenth is not due from anybody. **The woman of fifty-eight says in thirty-three words that she started at seven and will do eleven and has not been asked what it is worth today and that this is the first morning anybody has left it alone.** **The registrar of this district's records office says one thing in the yard at about one in the afternoon, unasked, in fifty-one words: that her book now holds eight printed days off a stranger's sheet and nine printed nights off a body's own paper, and that one of those two is a word where the other is a day, and that the twentieth of this month is in one of them and not in the other, and that a person may look at both of them in that office and may not carry either of them out of it.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud that a body which writes to a village is a body that knows the village has something, and that a registrar who has put the day a body printed into a book four hundred yards from a yard has made that body findable, and that the door that got used this month was not the one anybody was proud of. **The man of about twenty-three who sells nothing and copies for nothing copies the standing orders in about forty minutes, gives the copy to a clerk, and says twelve words: *Nobody took it. I gave it away. Put both of those down.*** A clerk reads the copy against the sheet and gets nothing wrong and enters that the three documents nobody owns did not move, and that this district now holds two copies of two different documents in two different rooms four hundred yards apart. **On the lane four miles down, at about half past four, the man of about fifty-five is on his own step with a pail in his hand and is not mending the tank, and he is not asked anything.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam has walked down the lane and was not sent for, and says the last of it there, in eighty-six words: the ninth of the nine was yesterday, and this district has got a pail, a crack in a tank and a channel with a foot of water in it at the top of a field, and not one of those three is a keeper, and the lease has to be answered in nine days at a place with no name on it, and there is nobody in this district who can get there and nobody in this district has ever met a man who keeps one.** A clerk enters that nobody wrote it down, because a man of fifty-six is not a clerk, and that the asking was entered on the eleventh of this month and again on the nineteenth and this is the third time, and that the conditions with no end on it are four and the boards are five and the lines full are six and the columns of not-askings are four and the protected things are five and there is still no rate that turns a year into coppers, and none of the seven moved. **AND ON THE TWENTY-FIRST OF THE FOURTH MONTH THE FOURTH HOLDING TOOK ITS FIRST DROP SINCE THE FIRST OF THE SECOND MONTH, A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY AT A COUNTER DID NOT COME, AND THE LAST DAY OF THE FOURTH MONTH IS NINE DAYS OFF.**

**REVIEW REPAIR ON THIS ENTRY. Two figures were wrong and are corrected. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said he had said the third-time sentence in this yard three days running; the sentence is in Chapters 212, 214, 216, 219 and 220, which are the thirteenth, the fifteenth, the seventeenth, the twentieth and the twenty-first, so the true run at the end is two days and the chapter now says two days running. And the man of fifty-six's line about two words for a man had given the whole pair, including the half that is not on any page, to the man of about thirty at a counter, when that half is the man who keeps a road's line in Chapter 212; the pair is now the counter man's, said on the ninth, and the not-on-any-page half is the road man's, out of a different house. The keeper's column in the same line is now headed with a word and not a name.**
## Chapter 0221 — On The Table Again

**The twenty-second of April, colder than that yard had been since the second month, and a gate that will not open without a foot against the post, and about nine people in the yard of Lot Seventeen.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam had it open by about a minute past ten with his right arm held against his ribs and said nothing about it, and a clerk of nineteen years entered that he said nothing and that she was not asked to make anything of it. He had put a spade into a wet bank at the sluice end of the loams at about half past six and could not lift a spade above his head, and he told the yard before anybody had thought to ask him, in **sixty-four words**: *I put a spade into a wet bank at the sluice end of the loams at about half past six and I have not been able to lift a spade above my head since, and I dug for nine years, and I am telling this yard about it because nobody in this yard is going to find out by asking me how I am.* A man of fifty-six said a man who announces a thing about his own body before anybody can ask him about it is doing the one thing that yard has which is worth more than the yard itself, and **that an arm is not an instrument and did not become the seventh of them.** The three figures: the board at a hundred and nineteen days, the train at four hundred and thirty-five, the unentered days at a hundred and forty-nine, and **the last day of the month eight days off**. **There is a second sheet lying on the trestle table that was not there yesterday morning and none of the nine of us put it there.** The strip of paper the man of about thirty-one brought down on the fourteenth is under it, face up, where he left it, and the trestle table belongs to nobody. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked for the sheet to be read and said out loud why he was asking, which is that a yard reads a document when somebody asks it to read one. A clerk read it out in one run of words and read it back and got nothing wrong. It is dated the eighteenth of this month, two days after the first letter came out of the same fold:** > **A BODY THAT WRITES TO A VILLAGE OFFERS A VILLAGE ONE SHEET, ONE HOLDER, ONE TERM, AND ONE NAME.**
> **THE ONE HOLDER IS TO SIT IN A CHAIR. THE CHAIR IS TO BE CALLED A FOUNDER'S CHAIR. THE TERM IS A TERM WITH NO END ON IT.**
> **THE NINE THINGS THAT HOLD BACK THE WATER WILL BE HELD BY THE ONE HOLDER FOR THE FOUR DISTRICTS UNDER THEM, AND THE OFFER WILL WORK FROM TOMORROW.**
> **AN ANSWER IS ASKED FOR BY THE LAST DAY OF THIS MONTH AND THE BODY SENDS ITS REGARDS AGAIN.**
**Nobody said anything for about nine seconds, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the whole of what it was and nobody asked him and no man counted it: one sheet, one holder, one term with no end on it and one name, and the name is not on it, and there is a place ruled for it the way there is a place ruled at the ninth on a board outside the room, and a person is a name, and this district does not put a name into a ruled place because somebody printed a line round it.** A man of fifty-six said a place ruled for a name is the same shape of nothing as the seventh line at the ninth place, and that two shapes of nothing are two shapes and are not one finding. **Asked, in the six things, whether a term with no end on it is a fifth condition in this district, the clerk refused the entry in about four seconds and gave the reason, in eighty-three words: a condition with no end on it is a thing a body can be held to, and this district has nobody in it to be held, and those are two different sentences, and there are four of the four and I did not rule a fifth this morning and I am not going to, and a term with no end on it printed on a sheet is an offer, and an offer is not a refusal, and this yard has not refused anything.** Asked what a chair is, the man of fifty-six answered in **forty-six words**: *a chair is a place a person is put, and a founder is a man somebody has already decided about, and the two of them together on one line of one sheet is a thing that does not need a man in the room to work.* The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale stood at the back of that yard for about ten minutes and went out of the gate without saying anything, and her four houses still stand and were not added to eleven. **The woman of fifty-eight came down the bank at about eleven and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing walked up to her at the foot of it and stopped about nine feet short and asked her, in his own words and not in the six things, whether a chair over a water is a thing she has an opinion about, and she said the honest answer in seventy-five words: I have carried water up that bank for nine years and nobody has ever asked me once whether I would rather somebody else carried it, and there is a chair in that sheet and somebody is going to sit in it, and I know which of those two things I have been for nine years, and I am not going to say it in front of nine people on the second day of eleven houses.** She picked the two buckets up and went out of the yard and up the bank, about a hundred and forty steps, without stopping, and it was entered that nobody asked her to and that nobody followed her. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam then asked, in front of everybody, for the offer to be written down in his own words, in sixty-three words: I would like that offer written down in my own words. I have asked three times in ten days and I have not been written down three times, and this is the fourth time, and I am telling you now that it is not going to happen, so that the not-happening is a thing this yard did and not a thing I did. A man of fifty-six said he is not a clerk and has said so about himself eleven times in four months. Nobody wrote it down.**

## Chapter 0222 — What A Chair Is For

**The twenty-third of April, cold enough that the trough at the foot of the bank had a skin on it, and the smell off the channel came up under the gate and stayed about an hour, and about nineteen people were in the yard of Lot Seventeen because there was a chair in it and not because anybody had sent for anybody.** The board at a hundred and twenty days, the train at four hundred and thirty-six, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty, **the last day of the month seven days off, and there is still a chair printed on a sheet on a table and nobody has said yes to it.** A chair is not an instrument and the six did not move, and the conditions with no end on it are four and the seat of a chair is not a fifth. **The chair itself came out of a building at the end of the lane at about half past ten, carried on its back by a man of about twenty-three who does not keep anything and sells nothing, who said he was going past with it. Nobody moved it, nobody sat in it, and it stayed in that yard for the rest of the day and the next.** **Then the man of about thirty at a counter came down the eleven mile road at about a quarter to eleven, and it was the sixteenth time since the second of January, a hundred and eleven days, which is fifteen weeks and six days, and the counting runs eleventh on the second, twelfth on the fourth, thirteenth on the sixth, fourteenth on the ninth, fifteenth on the fifteenth and sixteenth today, and this district has never once given that man a day to be here on.** He brought four sheets, which is what he brings, and stood at the end of the trestle table for about a quarter of an hour before anybody said anything to him. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing did not ask him about the ninth holding on the delivery sheet and said out loud in front of about nineteen people that he was not going to, in ninety-five words: I am not going to ask him that again today, and I am saying it in front of nineteen people so that if anybody in this yard says I did not ask, I have got it in the minute, and the reason is that a man who has been refused seven times in four months has never once been asked why, and if I put him an eighth time in a yard he is going to say no again and the eight is going to turn into the ninth for asking and not for refusing.** **The pool of refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given stands at EIGHT and the refusals about the ninth holding at SEVEN, and the two are not the same figure and are not added together, and neither moved.** A man of about thirty-four who digs loam said a man who has worked out how to stop a counter moving is a man who has found out that the count in this district runs on people and not on yards, and that he had not thought of that until this morning. Asked what his house thirty-four miles east would do with a chair over that water, the counter man said in **eighty-two words**: *My house would want it before the water did. I have been asked what a house is doing in a column headed with a word instead of a name for four months and I could not tell you then and I cannot tell you now, and a chair is not a house and a house is not a chair, and the day they are the same thing this district is going to hear about it from a body and not from me.* **Asked, in the six things, what a chair is worth, the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale gave the same answer she has now given three times in eighteen days, in sixty-three words: I have weighed nine sacks a day for nine years and I have never weighed a day, and I have never weighed a chair, and a man who wants one of those three priced for him is asking the wrong person, and I have been the wrong person four times now and I am going to stop being asked and start being told.** The clerk entered that a man who gives the same answer four times has not refused four times, that a person who has not been asked has not refused and cannot be counted either way, and that the four columns of not-askings are four. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said in **seventy-nine words**: *A chair is a thing you cannot see from where I am standing and a founder is a man somebody has already decided about, and a sheet can say both of those things in one line and mean every word of it, and a page that says it is a true page, and the whole of what this district has is a yard where anybody can stand up and say no, and a chair takes the standing up away.* **The panel at about ten past twelve, unasked, which nobody disagreed with:** > **A CHAIR IS A PLACE A PERSON IS PUT. A PLACE A PERSON IS PUT IS NOT A FIGURE. A FIGURE A PERSON IS PUT IN IS A JOB, AND A JOB TAKEN IS A JOB, AND THE PERSON IT IS TAKEN FROM IS STILL THE PERSON WHO DOES IT.**
> **A TERM WITH NO END ON IT IS NOT A LONG TERM. A LONG TERM ENDS. A TERM WITH NO END ON IT IS A HOLDER.**
> **A NAME IN A RULED PLACE IS A NAME IN A RULED PLACE AND NOT A PERSON WHO HAS BEEN ASKED.**
**A clerk entered that this is the third of these in this volume nobody has disagreed with, the first on the tenth of this month and the second on the nineteenth, and that the man who disagreed with the one on the nineteenth said he was going to keep the rest to himself.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam put his hand on the gate post and tried to lift it with his right arm and could not, and did not try a second time in front of anybody. The second sheet was read out again at about two in the afternoon for about eleven people who had not been there in the morning, and it is the standing orders and the council's first letter and this one that are three documents this district has got and did not write and cannot produce. **A man of about nineteen who counts was in that yard all day and had not been asked to do anything, and asked what he counts and why and said he counts because a yard is a place where a number can be checked, and that he did not know why the number mattered, and that he had been counting since the second of April, which is twenty-one days.** The counter man went out at about half past three with his four sheets, and it was entered that the sixteenth time cost him a road and not a refusal, and that a man who came eleven miles and was not asked about the thing he has refused seven times has been in a yard for about four hours and got nothing out of it.

## Chapter 0223 — A Room With A Door In It

**The twenty-fourth of April, the fifth day since the fourth holding took water in the night of the nineteenth into the twentieth, and about nine people in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-one days, the train at four hundred and thirty-seven, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-one, and the last day of the month six days off, and a man went to the records office at about half past eight and came back at about eleven and said he was going to say why.** Nobody asked him anything else about it. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing had asked for a clerk in that room on the nineteenth of this month and the asking is in the book, and the registrar of this district's records office said that the clerk is nineteen years old and is at the ward market and has not left it in four months, and that she was not going to have somebody walk four hundred yards in the middle of a working morning to stand in a room and write down two sentences, and that a person in a room is not a clerk and cannot enter anything, and that this is the reason a room is the right place to say a thing like this and is not the wrong one, and that the same rule keeps a stranger out of a book and it is a good rule and it is hers.** **The records office is a room with one door and a fanlight over it, and the door shuts, and the draught goes across the floor because the floor is stone and the stove has not been lit since the twentieth of the third month. The books are on two shelves along the north wall and on the table, and the table is the same table he wrote the twenty-one years on and then did not write anything under, and the eight printed days and the nine printed nights are in one book on the upper shelf in her hand, and a person may look at both and may not carry either out.** There is one chair in that room besides the one by the door, with a loose arm tied to the seat with a length of line, and it is the chair she sits in and her predecessor's and **the chair nobody has ever been offered.** Nobody has ever sat in it opposite anybody, and she said so, unasked, and a man of about nineteen was not in the room and did not count it. **She asked him the question herself, in the ordinary voice, and did not read it out of the sheet, and he stood, and she did not ask him to sit down. He said no, in a room with the door shut, with nobody in it but her, and a man of about nineteen was not there and did not count it and a clerk of nineteen years could not enter it, and the whole of it was a hundred and seventy words: I will not put a name in that place and I will not sit in that chair, and I am going to give you the reason once and I am not going to give it to a yard afterwards. A single holder cannot be asked a question by anybody, and a charter with no end on it that begins tomorrow decides what is sown in the season after this one, and the four districts under that water would not be asked about either, and there is nobody in this room to ask. My name is the only name this district has at the back of a book, and a name at the back of a book is a person a toll can be fastened to, and I have been at the back of that book since the twenty-seventh of last month and I know what it feels like from the wrong side, and I am not going to be the reason it feels like that to somebody above the water.** At about half past ten he put his hand on the back of the chair by the door and did not sit in it, and she did not offer it and said nothing about the hand. **She did not argue and did not agree, and she asked him one thing because she wanted an answer and not because a form wanted one, and she did not write it down and it is not in a book: Then somebody in this district is going to be a figure on a page by the last day of this month, because a letter wants a man and a lease wants a man, and I am the only one who can put a name down, and I am not going to put one down that nobody has said to me.** **He came back into the yard at about eleven and stood at the gate and did not go through it for about a minute, and at about two in the afternoon he said the whole reason out loud in the yard, in front of about nine people, in seventy-five words: A single holder is a thing nobody in four districts can ask a question, and the only instrument this basin has that a stranger can walk up to is a yard, and a yard is a door, and a chair over a charter is a door somebody else holds the key to, and I have said that in a room this morning and I am saying it here so that it is in a book.** A man of fifty-six said a man who says a thing in a room and then says it again in a yard has said it twice, and that two is not a habit, and that the first one is in a room with a shut door in it and a clerk cannot enter what was said in a room she was not standing in, and that the whole instrument of this district is a page. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the part nobody had said, in sixty-eight words: A room with a shut door is the only place this district has found where a man can be told no to a thing and mean it, and a yard is the only place this district has found where nineteen people can hear him say it, and he has now been in both, and that is two instruments in a week and neither of them has a name.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the two sentences are one fact told by two people to two rooms, and that a fifth time of his own sentence is a habit of a habit and he is not going to say it a sixth. The second sheet was read out a third time for four people who had come in after eleven, and among them a man of about twenty-three who said the word founder out loud when the second line was read and then said he had said it because it is on the sheet and not because he had anything to say about it.

## Chapter 0224 — Five Days

**The twenty-fifth of April, flat and grey, and the wind came in under the gate and could not find anything worth taking, and about nine people in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-two days, the train at four hundred and thirty-eight, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-two, and the last day of the month five days off, and a dray went up the only road in this district that climbs at about half past five on the morning of the twentieth and it is the fifth day and nobody has come down it.** A clerk entered that it is the fifth day counted off the twentieth and that a day a man is not here is not a day he has been here. **The lane four miles down from the lower terrace had about four people on it and nobody had sent anybody, and the butt at the back of the brick building had not been looked at since the twentieth, and the tank four hundred yards from that butt was over its lip.** The tank is a brick tank about five feet high with a lip about nine inches wide, and there is a line on the inside of the brick where the water stood on the seventeenth and there is a second line above it now, and the man of about fifty-five who keeps that building has been up a ladder with a piece of chalk twice this month and has not told anybody what the lines are for. **He had not slept and he had a pail in his hand that he was not using, and the second room of that building has nine years of beds in it, put there at about two in the morning that day, and nobody knew that until about half past ten in the morning.** Asked, in the six things, in front of about four people, what he had done, he said in **sixty-six words**: *I did what I said I would do on the twentieth, at about two in the morning, and I did not tell anybody until the morning, and there are nine years of beds in the second room and I have not lost one of them, and the water is where the water comes from and I am fifty-five and I have stopped expecting to be told.* **The woman of about thirty-eight who keeps that building was at the end of the lane with a pail of her own and did not come over, and a building which has denied nobody entry in nine years has not denied anybody entry this morning either, and the protected things are five and no sixth was added.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said a man who decides in advance to say nothing for about eight hours is a man who has decided that a figure gets to be a figure for about eight hours, and that this is the second time and a second time is not a habit. **He then said the other half of it before anybody asked him and it was not counted: I took the beds up and I left the crack alone and the crack is running and I have not asked anybody for a fifth mend in that tank in four months and I am not going to ask anybody today, and a man who has moved nine years of beds up a stair at two in the morning in the dark is not a man who is going to be told anything about water by a yard.** A refusal with a reason is not one of the refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given, and a man who keeps a building is not a clerk of a house, and the count of eight did not move. **The dray came down at about half past four with one man on the box and about nine people at the bottom of the lane who had not been sent for. The man of about thirty-one got down and stood at the bottom of it and said nothing for about a minute, and the yoke was not on the tailboard and was in a forge at the end off Salt Row.** He said one thing to the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing and not to the yard, and that man repeated it in the yard because he had not been asked not to, and a man of about nineteen counted the repetition and got **fifty-two**: **I have got a thing to say and it is not for a yard and it is not for a table, and I am going to say it in the room where the books are, and I want the man who keeps that road to be asked to leave this yard first.** **The man who keeps a road was not in that yard and had not been since the thirteenth, and a man who is named in a yard in order to be somewhere else is a man who has been put in a column by a person he cannot see, and the four columns of not-askings are four.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said a man who comes down a road after five days and asks for a room instead of a yard has changed his mind about something in five days. **The man of about fifty-six said the man of about thirty-one had put the strip of paper back on the trestle table without being asked, in the same place and the same way as on the third and the fourteenth, and that a man who puts a thing in the same place three times is a man who is asking to be asked and has not been asked.** Asked one question, in the yard and not in the six things, he gave the honest answer in **sixty-four words**: *I did not go up there for the sheet. I went up there because somebody was going to be at the top of that road on the twentieth and I wanted to see whether they were, and I did not find out, and I am not going to say that I did not find out as though it were a thing I had done.* Asked how long the road is at the top, he said in **thirty-one words**: *about a mile of it after the turn and there is a house on it and no name on the house and a wall.* **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam went up the bank at about half past four to take a bucket up for the woman of fifty-eight and could not get it off the ground, and she took it off him with one hand without a word.** At about half past five the man of about thirty-one walked four hundred yards to the records office and the door with the fanlight over it shut behind him, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing stood at the gate and did not go with him.

## Chapter 0225 — The Room With The Door Shut

**The twenty-sixth of April, the day after a man had said a thing in a room and had come out of it, and about nineteen people in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-three days, the train at four hundred and thirty-nine, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-three, and the last day of the month four days off, and a man said a thing in a room last night and did not say it in a yard, and one of us is going to have to.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing had been on the step of that office from about half past five the previous afternoon until the door opened at about nine, and had eaten nothing, and nobody sent him and nobody asked him. **At about a quarter past ten, in front of about nineteen people, he said what the man of about thirty-one had said to him at the gate the night before, in a hundred and eleven words, in his own words and not in the other man's: A page in a Registry's hand has got his name on it against this district's records office and it says he serves it, and the name went onto that page on the second of this month, the day he gave a name at a counter that takes names off sheets, and he read the page on the twenty-second in the room where the books are with the registrar in the room, and he has said nothing to anybody for three days and has told me and me only, and I am saying it here because I have been in this yard for four months and I know what a page is.** Nobody picked it up. **The registrar was in that yard on her own account and was not asked a first thing, and said one thing in eighty words: I have seen it. I may not carry it out of this office and I may not give it to a man of about nineteen and I may not read it into a yard, and that rule is mine, I wrote it on the twenty-first of this month, and I wrote it before I knew there was anything on it, and I am not lifting it today for a man who has done nothing wrong and has been paid nothing.** It was entered that the rule is the only rule in this district anybody has ever written down and enforced, and that a man who would have been protected by it is not protected by it today. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the shape of it, with his right arm in the sling, in a hundred and thirty-eight words: That is the third column this district has found in a month that is headed with a word and not a name, and the first carried a house nine times and a person no times, and this one carries a man once, and a column is a column, and a man who is in one is not in it because anybody asked him. What is in front of this yard is not that a man has been stolen. It is that a name went off one sheet at a counter on the second of this month and onto a page in a body four hundred miles away that nobody in this district has ever been inside, and nobody told the man whose name it was, and the page says he serves an office he has never been given.** **Nobody said the word abuse and the clerk was not going to say it either, and a word a body has for a thing is not a thing this district has, and the six instruments did not become a seventh because a man said a long sentence in a yard.** **Then the man of about thirty-one came into that yard at about half past eleven and was not asked anything, and asked one question, not in the six things, he gave the honest answer in a hundred and nine words: I did the work. I carried a ledger from that room to the counter at the end of the lane and back on the twenty-second, and I did it because the man who keeps that counter was not well and because I was in the room and it was in my arms, and I have carried things for that office nine or ten times since the second of this month and nobody asked me to and I would have carried them anyway. I am not a man of that office. Nobody has ever given me a job in it and nobody has ever paid me a penny of it.** Asked what he thought he was going to get out of saying it in a yard, he answered in **twenty-five words**: *I thought somebody would take my name off it. I was wrong about that, and I knew it was wrong, and I said it anyway.* **The clerk entered that the name of a man put on a figure cannot be taken off that figure by the man it is put on, and that a man who says a true thing out loud today and finds out this afternoon that the saying of it has made the name harder to take off is a man who has paid for saying it and has lost nothing that was his.** **A man of fifty-six entered that a man counting his own unpaid labour is a man keeping a tally and not a clerk, and that a clerk cannot count another man's days for him and is not going to.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said he did not lose the ledger work and lost the right to be a stranger four hundred yards from his own forge, and that the name was askable from the second of April and is now findable, and that the finding out is the price and is not a figure anybody in that yard can pay him back with. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said what three and a half hours on a step is, uncounted because he did not ask: the door of that room has a fanlight over it and the fanlight does not light anything, and he could see about nine feet of lane and the bottom of the step and a bucket that was not his.** **The registrar then said, in front of about nineteen people, because she had decided to and not because anybody asked, in seventy-two words, what she was going to do about it: I am going to enter in my own book that a page exists, that it is in a hand that is not mine, that a person has read it in this room, and that I may not produce it and may not carry it out. That is four lines and none of them is the name, and it is the whole of what this room can do, and it is not nothing.** The four lines were written out in the yard and a clerk of nineteen years wrote them down in her own book and not in the market's, and it was entered that four is a figure and that four is not a page about a man. **The man of about thirty-one said one more thing before he went, in fifty-eight words: I have not slept two nights running and there is a day of the week I could tell you and it would not help you, and I am not going to say who gave me the sheet on the road and I never was, and that is the last thing I am going to be asked about today.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the whole of what this yard has now, in sixty-two words: A sentence in a man's own words in a yard is the best instrument this district has, and a sentence in a man's own words in a yard is not a page, and the whole of the difference between this district and a body four hundred miles away is that this one can be stood in front of and that one cannot.**

## Chapter 0226 — Where The Work Went

**The twenty-seventh of April, the day after a man said a true thing in a yard, and about nineteen people in the yard, and the second sheet on the trestle table with the chair standing beside it. The board at a hundred and twenty-four days, the train at four hundred and forty, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-four, the last day of the month three days off, and nobody has been asked to carry a ledger since yesterday morning.** Nobody had been asked and the record says not asked, and the four columns of not-askings are four. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the half of it nobody had said, in eighty-five words: The work did not stop. The work stopped for one man and went somewhere else, and it will go somewhere else again tomorrow, and the page that has his name in a column on it does not need a reason and does not need him and does not need anybody to have been asked. A column is a column. I have said that about three of them in a month and this is the third and I am saying it before somebody else counts it.** A man of fifty-six said a habit of a true sentence is still a true sentence. **The registrar said the other half of it in the ordinary voice and did not wait to be asked, in a hundred and eight words: The man of about nineteen at the ward market is not well four days a month and on those four days a ledger goes from this room to a counter at the end of the lane and back, and until the twenty-second of this month it went with a man of about thirty-one who was in the room. It has not gone since. It is a book of nineteen rows and four columns and it weighs about as much as a full bucket, and it is two hundred yards each way, and I am not going to have it carried by a man who has not been asked.** Nobody said they would take it, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said out loud in front of about nineteen people that this yard was not going to ask anybody to carry it, and it was entered that a yard that stops itself from asking has not refused anybody. **The counter at the end of the lane is a counter in a room nine feet by eleven with a hatch in the wall of it, and the ledger stands on a table under that hatch with nineteen ruled rows and four columns and about nine pounds of paper in it, and the man of about nineteen who keeps that counter was not well on that day, and the table is four feet high. The room has a stove and a chair and a jug of water and a window about the size of a hand with the shutter closed against the wind, and the ledger has been on that table since about ten in the morning and it is a quarter past two in the afternoon, and nobody in that room knew it was there except the man who was not well and had gone home at about eleven.** **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat was asked, in the six things, in front of about nineteen people, whether she would carry it, and said in eighty-three words: I carried one on the twenty-second because it was in my arms and I did not know it was a job, and it was a Friday and I did not know that either, and I have not thought about it once since, and I am not going to say yes to it in a yard and I am not going to say no to it either, and I want it written down that nobody asked me and that I have not said no.** **A clerk of nineteen years entered that a woman said a day of the week and that no weekday is attached to any date in this district, and that she was not going to correct her because she was not wrong about the carrying, and that it is the first time in four months that a person in that yard has asked for the not-asking to be written down, and that she wrote it down, and that a request for a thing to be written down is not a column.** **The panel at about half past twelve, unasked:** > **A NAME PRINTED AGAINST AN OFFICE IS A NAME SERVING AN OFFICE. A NAME SERVING AN OFFICE IS A FIGURE.**
> **A FIGURE SERVING AN OFFICE CAN BE ASKED WHAT THE OFFICE ASKED OF HIM, AND HE CAN ANSWER, AND THE ANSWER IS GOOD ENOUGH.**
> **A DISCLOSURE IS AN INSTRUMENT. A DISCLOSURE PREVENTS A THING BEING DONE WITHOUT A PERSON KNOWING.**
**The man of about thirty-four who digs loam disagreed with the last of those three out loud, in eighty-three words: A disclosure is not an instrument, it is a man telling a thing once, and what it prevents is somebody being able to say afterwards that nobody knew, and it does not stop a page having a column in it with a name in it, and it does not stop the name being the next one. I said a true thing yesterday and it is on nobody's page and the ledger is still going to have to go two hundred yards each way.** A clerk entered that this is the first time in this volume that one person has disagreed with two of these out loud, and that a man who disagrees with a rule twice is not becoming a habit of disagreeing, he is two rules and two disagreements. **Asked, in his own words and not in the six things, what she thought about a name being on a page, the woman of fifty-eight said in a hundred and six words: A name said once in a yard is a name four hundred people have and a name said twice is a name a counter can ask for, and that is the rule of this yard and I learned it here and I said it on the sixteenth of this month about eleven doors of my own, and nobody has ever once used it about anybody but me. He has been in this yard three days and his name is being said in every yard in four districts and he is not mine and I am not going to be the woman who did it to him.** **A man of fifty-six said that a rule a person makes for herself and then uses against somebody else's harm is the only instrument this district has built and not named in four months, and a woman saying a true thing of her own about a man is not a seventh of them.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam went up the bank at about four to carry a bucket he could not carry, and she took it off him and said nothing at all.

## Chapter 0227 — The Second No

**The twenty-eighth of April, the last full day before the last day of the month, and about nineteen people in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-five days, the train at four hundred and forty-one, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-five, the last day of the month two days off, and a yard is going to be asked this morning about a chair, and it is going to be asked by a man who was not going to be asked in a yard on the nineteenth of this month.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing put the question himself, in the six things, in front of about nineteen people, and the man the figure of twenty-one years is against answered in about four seconds and gave the reason in a hundred and sixty-two words: No. I said in this yard on the nineteenth of this month that I would not say no in a yard about a thing nobody had asked me properly, and a body that wants a man out of this district has to come and put it to me in a room. It came to a room on the twenty-fourth and I said no in it, and I am saying no again here, and the two are not the same no. The one in the room was that nobody could ask a single holder a question. This one is that a yard is not a body and nineteen people standing in the cold are not a council, and if I took that chair off that table in front of all of you it would be because you were here and not because a man said no to a piece of paper, and I will not be a founder because a yard is full.** **A man of fifty-six said that a man who refused in a yard on the nineteenth on the ground that he had not been asked properly and refused in a yard today on the ground that a yard is not a body has spent nine days learning the difference between the two, and that a room is what made the yard possible, and that a yard which never sends anybody to a room only ever gets the answers it deserves, and that a yard that does send somebody to a room gets a different man back in the afternoon.** A clerk entered that the two refusals are two sentences about two different men and that a man who had given the same reason twice would have given it once, and that the eight did not move. The chair was moved four feet by somebody at about half past ten and was not moved back and nobody sat in it. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the other half of it, in ninety-one words: A Registry's page with a man's name in a column on it and a body's sheet with a chair on it are two records about two things four hundred miles apart, and one of them is a man who carried a ledger and the other one is a man who would sit in a chair, and two records that are not about the same thing cannot agree and cannot disagree, and I am refusing that join a fourth time in this district and it does not make a fourteenth of anything.** The refusals to join two things are thirteen and the registrar's own count is twelve and neither moved, and a fourth is the same refusal with a bigger number of people watching it. **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against wrote it himself at about one in the afternoon at a table in the back room of a building at the end of the lane, with the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing sitting opposite him and not helping and a man of fifty-six at the end with a slate. The pen had to be warmed in his hand for about a quarter of an hour before it wrote anything, and the ink went thin twice and he had to wait for it, and the second time he wrote the last word of the third sentence on a second line.** He wrote four sentences, then a line at the bottom, and signed it, and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said that a name at the bottom of a letter is not a name in a column and is not a bearer and is not a holder and is not on the standing orders of the nine. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam was in the yard the whole time and was not in the room and was not sent for and was not asked a first thing about the letter.** What was going on it, said to the yard and not to anybody, in **seventy-nine words**: *A body that writes to a village asks for a man by the last day of this month. This district is not sending one and is not saying it cannot, it is saying that it has nobody to send who has been asked, and a letter that asks for a man is not answered by a page and is not refused by a page. That is the whole of it and I will not put a chair in it.* **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked him one more thing in front of about nineteen people and he refused in about four seconds and gave no reason, and it was entered that a clerk does not put a number on the absence of a reason, and that no ninth refusal with no reason a clerk of a house has given was entered today, because a man the figure of twenty-one years is against is not a clerk of a house and has never been one, and that the pool is eight and the refusals about the ninth holding are seven and neither moved.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing then said out loud that he was not going to ask a man who had just been asked a question he had not been asked before about a second thing on the same afternoon. **The woman of fifty-eight, carrying her eleventh up the bank and down at about half past one, refused in thirty-nine words to hear the letter read out: It is a letter about a chair and I have carried water up a hill for nine years and I am not a housekeeper of a water. Write it and send it and do not read it to me.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale was not there that afternoon and her four houses still stood and were not added to eleven, and there is still no rate in this basin that turns a year into coppers and a charter with a term with no end on it is not a rate and is not the fifth of the five.

## Chapter 0228 — Up The Climb

**The twenty-ninth of April, the last day before the last day of the month, and about nine people in the yard, and nobody had sent anybody anywhere, and four men went up the only road in this district that climbs at about five that morning and came down it at about five in the afternoon.** The board at a hundred and twenty-six days, the train at four hundred and forty-two, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-six, the last day of the month one day off, and a clerk is not going to say what they went up it for until they are back down it. **A man of about thirty at a counter had said four words to the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing at the gate on the twenty-third as he was leaving, and nobody heard them, and the man who heard them entered them the next morning: a house at a road nine miles up will let a page be copied and will not let a page be carried. Asked where the house was, he said he had asked twice in thirty-four miles and had never been given an answer and had stopped asking, and that he was giving them a word and not a name and had never been given a name either, and four words at a gate are not a document and did not become a fourth of the three.** **They took a lamp and a slate and a pencil and about nine feet of line, and the man of about thirty-one who mends a dray went because he is the only one of the four who can find the turn, and he was asked about it plainly and said yes in about four seconds, and it was entered that a man who is asked and says yes is not a person who has been taken.** The climb is about four miles and the last half mile of it is not a road: a goat track along a shoulder with about two feet of drop on the left for a quarter of a mile at a time, with rock on the east side so the light goes early. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the plainest thing about the climb, in seventy-nine words: Four miles of it is a road and half a mile of it is a track that a goat uses, and there is a wall on the east side of the track for about a quarter of a mile and the light goes an hour early behind that wall, and we were carrying a lamp from half a mile below it and none of the four of us had been up that road before the twentieth of this month.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam carried the lamp in his right hand the whole way up, changed it to his left after about nine minutes at the top of the climb, changed it back, and nobody said anything about it and the clerk was told about it at nine in the evening by the man who had carried it.** **The house at the top of the road is one of the houses that is in the column headed with a word and not a name, and it has kept a thing in the water for four generations and has never been asked to say who kept it. Asked, standing in the doorway, who kept them, the answer came out of the house in eleven words and a man of about nineteen was not there and did not count it and it went into no minute and no book.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam asked the same question in his own words and not in the six things, and got a second answer in **fifty-six words, in the words of the man who asked it and not of the house: We have not had a name for him in four generations and it is not that we have lost it. It is that we have never had it. There was never a page with his name on it. There is a page with our house on it and it goes back further than the four generations.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said on the track going down that the only name anybody in this basin has ever had for the man who turns one is a word two houses gave this district in five days in the last month, and that a word is not a name and is not a page and is not a column, and that the ninth place on a page in a cave being ruled and empty is the same shape of nothing as the ninth place on a board in this district and he was saying that before anybody else did.** **The caves are a shoulder of rock with records in them and the records in them are four hundred years old in one case, and the light in there is a lamp and a doorway, and a house that has never let the caves be emptied will let four men in and hold a slate and will not let a page out — a page is copied and not carried — which the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said out loud twice on the track so that a clerk would have it twice.** **The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing said what the doorway was like before he copied anything, in sixty-six words: It is a doorway and not a room and the lamp is on the step and the wind comes round the corner of it every nine or ten minutes and puts the flame over, and I copied with my left hand on the slate and my right on the pencil, and a man cannot do that for fifty minutes and I did it for fifty minutes.** **He copied the page in about fifty minutes in his own hand. It has nine places on it and eight of them carry a name, and he would not read the eight names out in the doorway and would not read them out in a yard either, and gave the reason, which is this district's own rule about a name said twice, learned off a woman of fifty-eight in the last month: he was not going to be the man who used it on eight people who were not in the room.** They came down at about five. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam put the lamp down at the bottom of the climb and could not get his fingers to close on it for about a minute, and the man of about thirty-one took it off him without being asked.** **The man of about thirty-one said the thing he had said at the top of that road, in seventy-three words: There is a house at the top of that road with a wall round a shoulder of rock behind it and a man was copying a page in a doorway with a lamp on the step, and I have been up that road three times since the eleventh of this month and I did not know any of that until this morning, and I am not going to be asked about it here.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said at the gate that the copy is a copy and not a fourth document nobody owns, and that this district now holds a copy of a stranger's schedule, a copy of the standing orders of the nine and a copy of a page out of a cave, and that all three are copies a man made in this district for nothing, and that the district is still not going to pay him and has now said so four times.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said a district with one hand doing all of its copying and no money for it is a district with one finger on three documents, and a hand is not a seventh of the six. **The man of about twenty-three went home with his hand open, and a clerk of nineteen years entered that she was told about it by the man of about thirty-four who digs loam and not by the man it belonged to.**

## Chapter 0229 — The Last Day Of The Month

**The thirtieth of April, the last day of the month, and there were two things falling due on it, and about nine people were in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-seven days, the train at four hundred and forty-three, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-seven, and one of the two things falling due is a man and the other one is a lease and this district has neither.** **A clerk entered that the last day of the fourth month is a hundred and eighteen days after the second of January.** **The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households was asked, in the six things, in front of about nine people, about the three households that have not been asked again, and said in seventy words: The house has to ask me. I have been asked five times now to be told what to do about the three and I have not been told, and the state of those six has not moved since the twenty-first of last month, which is forty days, and I would like it written down that five is not a sixth and that a fourth time was not a fifth either.** **THE LEASE WAS NOT ANSWERED, and a clerk of nineteen years read that out at about eleven in the ordinary voice, because nobody else was going to say it and it was going to be said, in ninety-two words: A lease has to be answered on this day at a place with no name on it, and there is nobody at that place in this district but a man who is not in this district, and a lease that has not been answered on the day it falls due has not been refused and has not been taken up and has not been given up, and the record says not asked, and I am not going to enter a refusal on a lease nobody in this district was ever asked about.** **The man of about fifty-five who keeps that building was in that yard for about twenty minutes with a pail he was not using, and the nine years of beds are in the second room of a building that has denied nobody entry in nine years and still denies nobody entry. The woman of about thirty-eight who keeps that building sent word by a boy of about nine that the beds were in the second room and the water had not come over the step, and a boy of about nine is not a bearer and is not a clerk and is not anybody's instrument.** The man who keeps a road walked through that yard at about one in the afternoon without stopping and was not sent for and was not asked a first thing, and has been asked one question in this district since the first of this month and has given one answer. **Then the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked the man of about thirty-four who digs loam what should go on the sheet that is going up the road, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the plainest thing anybody has said in that yard in a month, in ninety-nine words, and a clerk of nineteen years wrote it down in the minute in his own words without being asked to by anybody: That a yard is the only instrument this district has that a stranger can walk up to, and that a book a stranger can walk up to is a door, and that the door which got used this month was a door with a fanlight over it and not this one, and that a man is a person and not a figure, and that a person who has not been asked has not refused, and that is all of it and I have said it four times in eleven days and a man of fifty-six is not a clerk.** **A man of fifty-six said a clerk of nineteen years wrote it down unasked at about half past eleven on the last day of the fourth month, and that the man of about thirty-four who digs loam asked for it on the eleventh, the nineteenth, the twenty-first and the twenty-second and was not written down four times, and that this morning he did not have to ask. He said the rest of it in forty-four words: That is not the same thing and I know it is not the same thing, and it is the best that has happened to me in a month, and I am not going to say thank you for it in front of nine people. Nobody thanked him for it, and a man of fifty-six said a man who says he is not going to be thanked in a yard has said that yard's own rule back to it, and that the rule was taught here on the third of this month by a woman of fifty-eight, and that it has now been said by three people in three weeks, and that three is not a column.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing read the board out himself at about ten past twelve without being asked and got all three figures right, and it was entered that he has read that board after another man about forty times in four months and has never once been asked to. **THE MONTH TURNED IN THE NIGHT, and about nine people were in that yard on the first of the fifth month. The board at a hundred and twenty-eight days, the train at four hundred and forty-four, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-eight, and the month turned in the night and nobody in this yard has said so and is not going to this morning, and a sheet is going up a road at about half past five this evening. A clerk entered that the month turned in the night, and that this district said a month turn out loud once, on the first of April, and that she was not going to say it twice in a yard, and that it will be said once more when there is something in it, and that she had not been asked when that would be and was not going to say.** The sheet went up the only road in this district that climbs at about half past five with the man of about thirty-one on the box, who was asked and said he was going up anyway, and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing read out what was on it before it went, and a clerk of nineteen years wrote it down and got nothing wrong, and no man counted it because a clerk does not put a number on a letter: > **A BODY THAT WRITES TO A VILLAGE ASKS FOR A MAN BY A DAY THAT HAS PASSED. THIS DISTRICT SENT NO MAN AND IS NOT SAYING IT CANNOT.**
> **A BODY OFFERED A CHAIR AND A RULED PLACE FOR A NAME, AND THE OFFER WAS REFUSED IN A ROOM ON THE TWENTY-FOURTH OF THIS MONTH AND AGAIN IN A YARD ON THE TWENTY-EIGHTH, AND THE TWO REFUSALS WERE NOT THE SAME NO.**
> **THE LAST THING ON IT WAS FOUR WORDS AND A LINE THAT A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM SAID AT A GATE AND A CLERK ENTERED, AND THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST PUT HIS OWN NAME AT THE BOTTOM OF IT AND NOBODY ASKED HIM TO. THE DISTRICT SENDS ITS REGARDS.**
**A man of fifty-six said a man who signs his own answer is the only one of that yard who is not a clerk and not a man of about thirty-four and not a woman of fifty-eight, and that a name at the bottom of a letter is not a name in a column and is not a bearer and is not a holder and is not on the standing orders of the nine.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing stood at the bottom of the lane until the dray was half a mile up the road, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam came out of the gate about ten minutes after it and did not go up the road after it and said nothing about it. **The day came down and the road took one man up it, and the slate was on the trestle table under the second sheet, and nobody had read the slate out.**

## Chapter 0230 — Nine Places And Eight Names

**The second of the fifth month, cold and clear, and about nineteen people in the yard. The board at a hundred and twenty-nine days, the train at four hundred and forty-five, the unentered days at a hundred and fifty-nine, and a hundred and twenty days separate the second of January and this morning, and this yard is going to say one thing out loud today that it has said once already and is not going to say again.** **The month turned on the first of this month and a clerk of nineteen years entered that nobody in that yard said so on the morning it turned because the yard was at the counter at the last hour of the last day of the last month, and that it was being said now, once, in fifty-six words: The month turned on the first of this month and the month before it was the fourth and the month before that was the third, and that is all of it, and this district has said a month turn out loud twice in a year and the other one is on a board nobody has washed. A clerk entered that the year that began on the first of January is the eighteenth after the Long Fracture and that the train on the siding came in on the eleventh of February of the year before that one, and that the year is in no chapter anybody has read and it is the one thing a man who works it out from the eleventh of February alone will get wrong.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked for the slate to be read because it had been on that trestle table for a day and a night and nobody had read it, and a clerk of nineteen years read it out in one run of words and read it back and got nothing wrong, and the man of about twenty-three stood beside her and did not read the names and was not asked to:** > **NINE ARE TO WRITE THIS AND NINE ARE TO KEEP IT. NO PERSON SHALL HOLD THIS ALONE.**
> **THE NAMES ARE THE NINE. THE FIRST. THE SECOND. THE THIRD. THE FOURTH. THE FIFTH. THE SIXTH. THE SEVENTH. THE EIGHTH. AND A LINE.**
> **AND UNDER THAT, IN A SECOND HAND, ONE SENTENCE UNDERLINED TWICE: A MAN WHO IS THANKED FOR THIS HAS BEEN MADE SOMETHING ELSE, AND THE SOMETHING IS NOT A CHAIR.**
**Nobody said anything for about nine seconds, which is the second time in eleven days that nobody in that yard has said anything at all and the first was on the twenty-second of this month, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the whole of it and nobody asked him for it, in eighty-nine words: Nine places and eight names. A page that has been copied out of a cave by a man of about twenty-three in his own hand in about fifty minutes, and the ninth place is ruled and it is empty, and that is the same shape of nothing as the seventh line at the ninth place on the board outside the room, and I am telling this yard that those are the same shape of nothing before somebody else puts them next to each other and calls it a finding.** A man of fifty-six said a page is a page and is not a board and is not a column and is not a document nobody owns, and that the three are the printed sheet at the fourth place, the notice about a party of nine who went thirty-four miles east, and a line in a public book with four names on it and a figure under the four names, and that a copy of a page that is four hundred years old in a house that has never let the caves be emptied is not a fourth of them. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the part about the underlined sentence in a hundred and ten words: Nine people wrote a rule that says nobody holds this alone, and one of the nine sat down afterwards and wrote that a man thanked for it has been made something else, and the something is not a chair, and that sentence has been underlined twice by somebody who was not in the room, and I am going to say the date of it out loud so that a clerk can enter it. It is the eighteenth of this month, in a hand that is not the other hand, and there is a body's sheet on that table with a chair printed on it and a ruled place under it.** A clerk entered that the hand under the rule is not the hand the names are in, that two hands is not two findings, that the second hand is a different person out of the eight, and that this district cannot say which of the eight and is not going to ask a house nine miles up a question about a page it was allowed to copy. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing also said, in eighty-two words, what that page does to a question: A page with nine places on it and eight names in it is a page anybody can walk up to and ask a question of, and that is the whole of what this district has spent four months trying to
