build, and it has been sitting in a cave the whole time, and the one thing it does not have on it is a name in the ninth place, and I am not going to be the man who fills that in.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join in eighty-one words: A page in a cave and a sheet in a yard are two records about two things, and one of them is nine people four hundred years ago and the other one is a body that says it will work from tomorrow, and two records that are not about the same thing cannot agree and cannot disagree, and I am refusing that join a fifth time in this district, and a fifth is the same refusal with five people watching it.** The refusals to join two things are thirteen and the registrar's own count is twelve and neither moved. **The registrar, in the yard on her own account and not asked a first thing, said one thing in a hundred and two words: That page has been in a cave in this basin longer than any book in my office, and it is a page with nine places and eight names and one rule, and the rule is the only line on it that says what a person may not do, and the whole of this district's answer to it this month is a letter with four words of a man who digs loam at the bottom of it and a book with four names and a figure under the four names, and neither of them is a copy and neither of them is nine.** **The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing said one thing, and a man of about nineteen counted it because he had not said not to, and it got to eighty, and he said afterwards that he should not have said not to: I have made three copies in a month and I have been paid for none of them and I have said so four times and I am not going to say it a fifth, and my hand does not close on a cold morning and it did not close yesterday, and I am telling this yard about that because the yard is the only thing in this district that has ever written down one word of anything I have said.** **Asked how many places the ninth place on that page is, he said in twenty-eight words that it is a place about the width of a finger and a half and about the height of two fingers and that it is ruled and it is empty, and it was entered that a place is a figure about a page and not a figure about a person.** **The woman of fifty-eight said one thing to nobody in particular and nobody counted it because nobody was asked to: I said the name of a yard's rule out loud for a man who is not mine, and I have carried eleven houses for ten days and I have not been thanked for it once and I did not want to be, and that is the first time in nine years a thing I have done has gone unwritten and I find that I do not mind.** **The eleventh bucket went up the bank at about ten past twelve and nobody counted the buckets. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam put a spade in the ground at about eleven in the morning at the edge of the yard, where the gate wants a foot against the post, and he got it up above his head, and it took him two goes, and nobody counted that either.**

## Chapter 0231 — Four Places And A Word

**The third of the fifth month, clear and hard, and the yard of Lot Seventeen filled from about a quarter to ten. The board at a hundred and thirty days, the train at four hundred and forty-six, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty, a hundred and twenty-one days from the second of January, and a thing on that table that was not there when the man who mends fencing walked in.** A third thing was on the trestle table: a new sheet folded once across, put there by nobody in that yard. He said that first, in **forty-eight words**: *That table has now had three things put on it by somebody in another county and not one of them by anybody in this district, and I am saying that first because in about four minutes somebody is going to stand on all three of them at once.* Then the reason it was worth a morning, in **ninety-two words**: *That sheet has one word on it four times and the word is the one this district took off a stranger's schedule in the first week of the fourth month and has not said out loud in a yard since. The gate at the end of this yard is a gate and has been a gate for four months and I am not confusing the two. A body four hundred miles away can print what it likes and this district is not going to start saying it again because a sheet arrived.* **THE TOLL SHEET, READ OUT AND READ BACK, WHICH IS THE RULE OF THE COUNTER:** > **A BODY THAT KEEPS RECORDS OF HOLDINGS SENDS A TOLL ON A GATE TO EACH OF FOUR PLACES BY THE ROADS BELOW THE WATER.**
> **THE TOLL ON THE GATE IS ONE TOLL AND IT IS PAID BY THE PARTY OF EACH OF THE FOUR AND BY NOBODY ELSE. A GATE HOLDS A ROAD SHUT AND A ROAD BELOW A GATE IS A ROAD THAT IS SHUT.**
> **THE FOUR ARE NUMBERED AND NOT NAMED. THE FIRST OF THE FOUR. THE SECOND OF THE FOUR. THE THIRD OF THE FOUR. THE FOURTH OF THE FOUR. A PLACE IS THE FOURTH OF THE FOUR BY ITSELF UNTIL SOMEBODY SAYS IT IS NOT.**
> **THE TOLL IS PAID ONCE AND IS NOT PAID TWICE, AND A BODY THAT KEEPS RECORDS OF HOLDINGS IS NOT ASKED WHETHER A GATE HAS BEEN PAID.**
**A sheet from a body is a document somebody owns and did not become a fourth of the three.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said what the sheet was, in **eighty-four words**: *A toll is a thing a named person pays under a term. That is what a toll is and that is the only thing a toll has ever been in this basin. This sheet has got four parties on it and not one person on it, and a party is not a person, and a toll that cannot be fastened to a person cannot be collected and cannot be refused either, and the record on all four of these is that nobody was asked.* He refused the join of the toll sheet and the chair a sixth time, in **sixty-six words**. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said out loud that he is not going to be the man who decides that this district is the fourth of the four, in seventy-nine words, so that if somebody does decide it there is a line in the book saying a man noticed.** The woman of fifty-eight, asked in his own words whether a toll on a thing that holds the water back is a toll on the woman who carries the water, answered in **seventy-two words**: *I carry water to eleven houses and I have never been asked to carry it for anybody's gate and I am not going to be asked now. If a body wants my two buckets it can come up this bank and say so to my face and I will put them down, and until it does there is a woman on this bank and not a party of the fourth of four.* **The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing refused to copy the toll sheet, in eighty words, because a district that owns a toll sheet is a district that is arguing about the toll.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said, uncounted, that his shoulder is going the other way and that he does not know whether that is good news or a thing that gets worse later. **THE COUNTS ARE FIVE, SIX, THREE, THIRTEEN, TWELVE, EIGHT AND SEVEN, AND NONE OF THEM MOVED, AND THE NINTH PLACE ON THE SLATE UNDER THE SHEET IS STILL RULED AND EMPTY.**

## Chapter 0232 — What A Pin Is For

**The fourth of the fifth month, the first hard frost of the year, ice in the trough at the foot of the bank at about nine, about nineteen people in the yard, and a barrow at the gate with a thing on it. The board at a hundred and thirty-one, the train at four hundred and forty-seven, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-one, the twelfth of the month eight days off.** A lock came out of the channel at the sluice end of the loams at about seven in the morning onto a barrow and went up a bank in about eleven minutes with two men on the shafts, the man who digs loam at the near shaft and the man who mends fencing at the far one, and was put down at the end of the trestle table. **It is a frame of cast iron about four feet high, bolted to two pieces of oak let into the bank, with a screw through the middle, a handle about a foot and a half long, a pin through the screw where the handle is pinned on, and a stone sill with a notch cut in it, and it weighs about a hundred and ninety pounds.** The man who digs loam asked for it to be read, not to be carried, in **eighty words**. **THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST READ IT IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINETEEN PEOPLE AND WAS WRONG, IN NINETY-SIX WORDS: This is a lock and it holds a road shut. What it holds with is the pin. The pin is the piece that takes the load. The frame is only a frame and the screw is only a way of lifting, and if the pin is out then the screw runs free and the weight of the water goes through the frame and out, and so a lock is a safe thing exactly as long as a man is near it, and it is not a safe thing on a night when nobody is near it.** Nobody said anything for about nine seconds. **THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX WAS RIGHT, IN A HUNDRED AND TWELVE WORDS, WITH HIS OWN HAND ON THE FRAME NEXT TO THE OTHER MAN'S: The pin takes nothing. I have been putting a stick through that pin to turn that screw with both hands in the water for nine years, about four thousand times, and if the pin took the load my hands would have gone under the water with the screw. It is there so the screw cannot turn on its own in the night. What holds that channel is the frame and the weight of the water sitting on the frame, and the screw is a lever and not a wall, and a man who puts his weight on that lever is lifting the whole weight of a channel on one thread of iron.** **He said the whole of what he was wrong about out loud himself, in a hundred and forty-seven words: A pin is not a wall, a screw is not a wall, a frame is not a wall, and a lock does not need a man to hold it and never did, and a yard is the only place in this basin where a man is allowed to find that out for nothing.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said in **ninety-seven words** that this is a seventh instrument — a man saying out loud in front of other people that the thing he knows best in the world is not what he said it was — **and the clerk refused it in about four seconds and the six did not move.** The man of about fifty-six said that a rule about being thanked and a rule about being wrong are the same shape of rule. **The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing refused to draw the lock, in sixty-five words, because there are nine of them and not one of them is ours.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam put his arm back in the sling without being asked and said, in **eighty-three words**, that he was fine at the bottom of the barrow and is not fine now, and asked for it to be written down that the man at the near shaft is the one who pays for it in the shoulder. The second sheet with the chair printed on it was read out for the fourth time and the chair has not been sat in by anybody.

## Chapter 0233 — The Fourth Of Four

**The fifth of the fifth month, bright and freezing, about nine people in the yard, and a man came down the only road in this district that climbs at about half past seven and nobody in that yard sent for him. The board at a hundred and thirty-two, the train at four hundred and forty-eight, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-two, the twelfth of the month seven days off.** **A man of about forty-one who keeps a road house at the top of that road, past the turn, nine miles with the last half mile a goat track along a shoulder with about two feet of drop on the left, came down in about four hours with a stick and came into the yard at about half past eleven.** The sheet was read out to him and he put one hand flat on the third line of it and said, in **eighty-six words**: *That is the fourth of the four and it came to the top of our road on the third of this month, the same morning it came to you, and I have had two days to think about it and walk down about it. Our house did not sign for it. Our house has not signed for anything since my father was alive, and he signed for nothing, and I am not a man who signs for other people and I have not been asked to.* Asked whether a party of the fourth of four is a person at his house, he said in **twenty-three words**: *It is a house with my name over the door, and a name over a door is a name on a house and not a name on a figure, and I have never in forty years been asked to hold anything, and I would know, because everybody in this basin who holds something is talking about it by now.* **A clerk entered that two places now hold the fourth of the four and that neither of them can be shown to be right and that the record about both says not asked.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing refused the join of the number four and a man at the top of a road, in **sixty-nine words**, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join of the toll and the man on the road a seventh time, in **seventy-nine words**, and neither of the two counts moved. Asked, in the six things, whether his house would pay the toll, he said in **eighty-four words**: *The party of a house is a house. There is nobody in our house who has been asked to pay anything and I am not going to be the first one they ask, because the day they ask me is the day the house has signed without anybody going into the room. My answer is the same as the one your woman of fifty-eight gave about her two buckets, and I have not heard her say it and I have not read it out.* **A fifth column of not-askings was proposed — a column of the places a toll has landed in that have not signed for it — and refused in about four seconds. THE PANEL, ARRIVED UNASKED:** > **A TOLL ATTACHED TO A GATE ATTACHES TO THE PARTY NAMED AGAINST IT. WHERE THERE IS NO PARTY NAMED, THE TOLL ATTACHES TO NOTHING.**
> **A TOLL THAT ATTACHES TO NOTHING IS NOT A TOLL. IT IS AN INSTRUCTION TO A HOUSE, AND A HOUSE THAT PAYS AN INSTRUCTION HAS PAID IT, AND THE HOUSE THAT DOES NOT PAY IT HAS STILL BEEN INSTRUCTED.**
> **A LOCK STANDS OPEN OR SHUT. IT DOES NOT STAND IN A DOOR OF A RECORD.**
**The man of about thirty-four who digs loam disagreed with the second of those three out loud, in a hundred and four words: That second one is wrong and it is wrong in the direction that costs somebody. You cannot get a house out of a toll by taking the name off it. The name was never what makes it heavy. A thing with no name on it that arrives at a house is a thing the house is afraid of, and a house that is afraid of a thing is a house that will find a person in it to be angry at, and there is no name on the toll for the anger to land on, so it lands on whoever is in the room.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale stood at the back of the yard at about three and went out without saying anything and her four houses still stand. **The man from the road house went up the road at about two in the afternoon, it takes a man about five hours going up in the cold, and he was still on the goat track when the light went, and nobody in that yard offered him anything to eat and nobody thought to.**

## Chapter 0234 — A Rival Record

**The sixth of the fifth month, the wind out of the north going through a yard with nothing high on three sides, about nineteen people standing in it at about ten. The board at a hundred and thirty-three, the train at four hundred and forty-nine, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-three, the twelfth of the month six days off.** A fourth thing was on the trestle table: a notice, folded once, pushed under the leg of the table where the ground had gone soft, and it had taken the shape of the leg. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said that first, in **eighty-three words**, and said that a man who pushes a paper under a table leg is a man who has stood in that yard and looked at that table. **THE NOTICE, READ OUT AND READ BACK, WHICH IS THE RULE OF THE COUNTER: READ *IDENTIFIED*, NOT *NAMED*.** > **A NOTICE IS PUT UP BY A BODY THAT KEEPS RECORDS OF HOLDINGS. IT NAMES NO PLACE.**
> **IT SETS DOWN A RIVAL RECORD. A RIVAL RECORD IS A RECORD IN A BASIN WHERE A BOOK IS KEPT IN ONE ROOM BY ONE PERSON, AND THAT PERSON IS THE ONLY ONE WHO MAY LOOK IN IT, AND THAT ROOM STANDS FOUR HUNDRED YARDS FROM A YARD A STRANGER CAN WALK UP TO AND WALK IN.**
> **A RIVAL RECORD IS ASKED AGAINST. THE BODY WILL ASK IT AGAINST FOUR PLACES AND TWO OF THE FOUR HAVE NOT ANSWERED.**
> **THE NOTICE TAKES EFFECT ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE NEXT MONTH.**
**A notice from a body is a document somebody owns and did not become a fourth of the three.** The registrar of this district's records office was in that yard on her own account and was not asked a first thing, and read it a second time standing at the end of the table with her hands behind her back and said nothing about it for about a minute. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked her, in the six things, in front of about nineteen people, what she thought a rival record was, and she answered in a hundred and ten words, and the answer is the block's political cost and it is not a comfort: A rival record is a record a body can ask against another record, and it is not a finding and it is not a truth and it is not a charge, and the only thing about it that is a fact is that somebody four hundred miles away has written down the shape of my room. My name is not on that notice. Nobody's name is on that notice. I have read it three times to be certain and there is not one name in it.** And then: *The only line in that notice that has anything to do with me is the second one, and the second one is my own rule, and I wrote it on the twenty-first of the fourth month, and I wrote it before I knew there was a page in my office with a man's name on it or a chair on a table in this yard or a word printed four times on a piece of paper. A book is kept in one room by one person and that person is the only one who may look in it. That is my rule. It is the only rule anybody in this district has ever written down and enforced, and I enforce it, and I have turned people away from that door with it. It is also, this morning, the only reason that notice could be written.* And then: *And nobody has put my name on anything, so I cannot stand in this yard this morning and say that a name put on a figure cannot be taken off it by the person it is put on, which is this yard's rule and a good one and which I would like very much to have today. What is on that notice is not my name. It is my room, and my books, and the fact that I am the only one who may look in them, and all three of those are true, and I wrote two of them myself. I cannot get a true thing off a notice by writing to a body four hundred miles away, because I would be writing to tell them a thing they have already got right, and the day I understand that is the day I stop being angry about it, and I am not at that day.* The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the half of it nobody had said, in **seventy-nine words**: *A body four hundred miles away has worked out how to find her in about a fortnight, and she has not been asked one question about it by anybody in this yard including me, and every one of us in this yard has spent four months learning how to let a stranger walk up to a book, and a body can walk up to a book, and that is not the same door but it is the same skill.* **She refused a fifth condition with no end on it, before anybody asked her to, in eighty-four words: A rival record is asked against four places and two of the four have not answered. That is a record that stands until somebody in another county says something, and a thing that stands until a body has finished with it is a condition with no end on it, and there are four of those in this district and I am not ruling a fifth, because a body can be held to a condition and this district has nobody in it to be held.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing refused the join of the two fours, in **a hundred and twenty-five words**, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused it an eighth time, in **sixty-seven words**, and neither of the counts moved. The notice was read out again at about three for people who had come in after eleven and the clerk entered that the notice has now been read out in that yard twice.

## Chapter 0235 — Fifteen Days On A Table

**The seventh of the fifth month, the coldest morning of the year so far, ice in the trough at about nine, about nine people in the yard. The board at a hundred and thirty-four, the train at four hundred and fifty, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-four, the twelfth of the month five days off, the first day of the next month twenty-five days off.** A ledger of nineteen ruled rows and four columns, about as much as a full bucket, that has been on a table four feet high under a hatch in a counter room nine feet by eleven at the end of the lane since about ten in the morning on the twenty-second of April, **went back two hundred yards on a morning when the man of about nineteen who keeps that counter was well, and he carried it himself, and nobody asked him and nobody was in the room.** A clerk entered that a person who has not been asked to carry a thing and has carried it has not refused it and has not consented to it, and that the record about the fourteen days before that morning says not asked. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the thing about the fifteen days in **a hundred and thirteen words**: *Fifteen days on a table four feet high under a hatch, and on four of those days a man was not well and on one of the other four a woman carried it two hundred yards because it was in her arms and did not know it was a job, and on this morning the man carried it himself because he was well. Nobody has been asked. The work did not stop. It stopped for one man in the third week of last month and it went somewhere else, and on this morning it went somewhere else again, and a book that moves itself is not the same as a book somebody carries.* The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households said out loud that nobody had asked him anything that morning and that he was going to stop, in **a hundred and six words**, and named the **sixteen days** the six have not moved. **THE TANK FOUR MILES DOWN A LANE** — brick, about five feet high, a lip about nine inches wide, the water a hand's breadth over the lip, two chalk lines on the inside of the brick, a crack about the length of his hand — and the man of about fifty-five who keeps that building said, in **eighty-five words**: *The crack has gone about the length of my hand since the twenty-fifth of last month and it is not a crack that is going to close and I have not asked anybody for a mend in that tank in four months and I am not going to ask anybody today, and the water is where the water comes from, and the second room still has nine years of beds in it and the water has not come over the step since I moved them.* The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing said in **eighty-nine words** that his hand closes about halfway now, and that he said he was not going to say it a fifth time and is not, and that somebody asked him about it and he answered, and those are two different things. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked the yard out loud whether this district answers the notice, in eighty-seven words, and nobody answered him: Does this district answer the notice. That is the question and I am asking it out loud and nobody is going to answer it, and I want a clerk to write down that I asked it and that nobody answered it, because on the first of the next month that thing takes effect whether it is answered or not, and a notice that names no place and no person has got nobody in it to answer and that is not the same as nobody being in it.** The notice was read out a third time at about half past two. The chair has now stood at the end of the trestle table for **fourteen days** and nobody has sat in it. **The twelfth bucket went up the bank and nobody counted the buckets.**

## Chapter 0236 — A Mark Is Not A Job

**The eighth of the fifth month, the first day of the year with a sun in it that felt like one, about nine people in the yard at ten and the same nine on a road four miles out past the loams at about half past one in the afternoon. The board at a hundred and thirty-five, the train at four hundred and fifty-one, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-five, the twelfth of the month four days off, and the fourth holding took water in the night of the nineteenth into the twentieth of last month, which is eighteen days ago.** Nobody sent for anybody. The road goes up past the top field of a farm whose legal owner is not in this district; the last half mile before the channel is a cart track between two banks with a wire on the left of it that has not been tight since before anybody in this district was born; the water can be heard from about nine hundred yards out and there is a smell off it in the afternoon of wet iron and nothing else. **THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST READ THE SILL AND WAS WRONG, IN NINETY-ONE WORDS, IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINE PEOPLE: The sill has a notch cut in it and the notch is the record. That is the height the water is held to and everything below that notch is what a lock is for, and a notch cut in a sill is a mark somebody made to come back to, and the water that came in the night of the nineteenth into the twentieth stood up to that notch and not an inch past it, and a mark like that is a gauge and a gauge wants somebody to read it.** A man of about forty-one who keeps a road house at the top of the road was standing about four feet away and had been there since before any of them arrived. **He was right, in eighty words, with his thumb in the notch: That is not a mark of how high the water has been. That is where the board goes. There is one notch in that sill and there has been one since before my father walked this road, and it is a cut for the edge of a plank, and a plank goes in it and the water is held above the plank about a foot deep and no more, and the board is not there now because it is May.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the rest of it, in **a hundred and three words**, and named that the man of twenty-one years has now been wrong twice in six days and said so out loud both times before anybody found it. **He said the whole of what he was wrong about out loud himself, in a hundred and sixteen words: I read a mark as a job. That is the whole of it and I am not going to dress it up as a discovery about water. There is one notch in that sill and it is a place a plank goes and I have spent two days building a gauge out of a hole a board fits in, and a man of about forty-one who walks past it four thousand times put his thumb in it and had it finished before I had finished saying it, and that is what nine years of digging is worth against two days of reading, and I would rather it was worth nothing than have it be worth that.** A clerk entered that a mark may not carry a job and that **the seventh instrument was proposed once, in a yard, on the fourth, and refused once, and has not been proposed a second time, and a rule refused once and never again after that is a rule that has been learned and not a rule that has been dropped.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join of the notch and the two chalk lines on the inside of a brick tank while he was standing next to both of them**, in a hundred and four words, and the man of about fifty-five who keeps that building was not there and nobody sent word to him. The man of about forty-one went back up at about a quarter past three and was gone over the turn at about half past seven. They came back at about half past four and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam held his left hand over his eyes against a low sun for the last mile, and the man who mends fencing walked on his left side for about nine minutes and was not asked why.

## Chapter 0237 — Four Hundred Yards

**The ninth of the fifth month, still and pale, the wind round to the east, about nineteen people in the yard, and one of the people who is usually in it at that hour was not in it. The board at a hundred and thirty-six, the train at four hundred and fifty-two, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-six, the twelfth of the month three days off.** The clerk wrote first, before anything else, that the man of about thirty-four who digs loam was not in that yard in the morning and not in it at about two in the afternoon either, and that nobody sent for him and that the record says not asked. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said in the figures that he is not going to say the reason and does not know it. A man of about nineteen said, in **sixty-eight words**: *A yard is a room with no roof and I have been counting in it every morning since the second of April and this morning I counted nothing before about eleven, and it is the first morning since the second of April that I have counted nothing, and I am saying that and not making anything of it, and I do not know what I would count instead.* The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households said in **a hundred and seven words** that a body four hundred miles away is going to ask four places a question and that he will not work out which of his six is in which of the four. **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale, in that yard for the first time in a fortnight, said in a hundred and nine words: I have four houses standing empty and I have had them standing empty since the last day of last month, and the reason they are empty is not the water and it is not a chair. It is that nobody has asked anybody in those four houses a single question. I have been asked four times in eighteen days what a chair is worth and I have been asked nothing at all about four houses, and if a body four hundred miles away is going to start asking places questions in three weeks, then the first thing anybody in this basin has to do is go and ask somebody.** **IN THE ROOM WITH A SHUT DOOR IN IT, at about half past eight before the yard**, the man the figure of twenty-one years is against asked for the notice to be read into her book and was refused and gave no argument, put his hand on the back of the chair by the door and did not sit in it, and said both of the things he had been wrong about, in about four minutes, and gave no reason for either because he had given both reasons in front of other people twice. She asked him one thing, uncounted and not written down and not in a book: **Why are you telling me and not the yard.** And he answered: **Because the yard will write it down and you will say what you are going to do about it, and I do not know yet what either of us is going to do, and there is one page in this district I am allowed to say a thing in that is not a book and I have been putting things in it for four months and there is nothing in it yet.** **THE PANEL, ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT FOUR IN THE AFTERNOON:** > **A NOTICE NAMES NO PERSON. A NOTICE IS EFFECTIVE ON A DAY. A PERSON IS AFFECTED BY A NOTICE ON THE DAY IT IS EFFECTIVE AND NOT BEFORE.**
> **AN OFFICE THAT KEEPS ITS BOOKS IN ONE ROOM WITH ONE PERSON IS THE MOST FINDABLE OFFICE IN A BASIN, AND THE FINDING IS AGAINST THE ROOM AND NOT AGAINST THE PERSON.**
> **A BODY THAT PUTS UP A NOTICE DOES NOT PUT UP A NAME, AND A BODY THAT PUTS UP A NOTICE HAS DECIDED IN ADVANCE THAT IT WILL NOT NEED ONE.**
**The registrar disagreed with the first of those three out loud in front of about nineteen people, in ninety words: That is wrong on the first of them and it is wrong in the way that costs a person. I am not affected by that notice on the first of the next month. I am affected by it now, this afternoon, because a man stood at the leg of that table and pushed a paper under it, and everybody within four hundred yards has read it out loud twice since, and the first of the next month is when it stops being true that nobody has asked me about it.** And: *The third of them is the one I will not let go. A body that has decided in advance it will not need a name has decided that in advance, and the decision is printed, and the decision is the finding, and I would like it observed that nobody in this yard has made a decision of that kind about anything.* **This is the fourth time in this volume that one person has disagreed with two of these out loud.** The chair has now stood at the end of the trestle table for **sixteen days** and there is still no date in any ledger for the next reading and the refusals to read are nine and the departure is unspent.

## Chapter 0238 — What He Said About It

**The tenth of the fifth month, grey from the west, about nineteen people in the yard, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam was in it with his right arm in a sling that was not his. The board at a hundred and thirty-seven, the train at four hundred and fifty-three, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-seven, the twelfth of the month two days off.** The sling came from the woman of fifty-eight and nobody asked her and the record says not asked. The woman of fifty-eight said one thing about it, uncounted, that a yard in this basin has four things a person is asked about and none of them is a piece of cloth. Asked, in his own words, what had happened to his arm, he said in **a hundred and fifty-two words**: *I carried a barrow up a bank on the fourth of this month at the near shaft and I told this yard at the time that I was fine at the bottom of it and not fine now, and then I told the truth about it, which is that I was not fine at the bottom of it either and I could feel it going on the last four or five steps and I said fine because it was nine people and a barrow. On the eighth I put my hand into the channel to feel the bottom of a sill because a man of about forty-one asked me to, and on the ninth I was not in this yard at all, and I want it written down that nobody sent anybody to find out where I was on the ninth and that is correct and I would have said so myself.* **THE FINDING OF THE BLOCK, IN EIGHTY-NINE WORDS: A lock is the only instrument in this basin that has never needed a person. Not one. There is a pin in it that takes nothing and a frame that holds a road shut on the weight of the water and a screw that is a lever and not a wall, and the whole of it works in the middle of an ordinary night with nobody anywhere near it, and every one of the nine of them will do it again tomorrow without asking any of us for anything.** And: *And I am not going to stand in this yard and use our own rule about people on a piece of iron. Our rule is that a person who has not been asked has not refused, and that rule is true and it is about people, and iron is not people. The honest way to say what has happened in this basin for forty days is that nine structures in the water above four districts have been standing open on eight of eleven nights, one a night, since the first of April, and nobody in this district has been to look at one of them. Not once. We have stood in this yard and given a number out loud every morning and we have never walked to a lock.* And: *And the ninth of the nine printed nights was the twentieth of last month, and this district has said for three weeks that it cannot say whether that night happened, and it cannot say whether that night happened because nobody has been, and the reason nobody has been is that a lock cannot be asked, so there is nobody to ask, so it is nobody's.* Asked one question about it in his own words, in **ninety-nine words**: *Eight of eleven nights, one a night, since the first of April, and nobody opened them, and I have been in a yard every morning of that saying a number out loud and reading it back off a board that has not been washed since the twenty-fourth of December, and I could have walked to one of them in a morning. Any of us could have. That is the thing I am saying and I have not said it before, and I have had a day off and a day of a channel and I have said it now.* **A clerk entered that a thing a man could have done and did not do is not a refusal and is not a consent and is not a count.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the part about the nine, in **ninety-nine words**: *Nine of them. Not eight and not five. A district that has spent four months proving that one holder cannot be asked a question by anybody has nine of the things sitting up there that do not need to be asked a question by anybody at all, and a body four hundred miles away has offered one man the lot of them in a sheet with a chair printed on it, and the whole of what I know about the difference between that sheet and those nine is that those nine work and the sheet has not been tried.* The notice was read out for the fourth time. The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road walked through the yard and did not stop. **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against said in eighty-nine words that he does not know what to do about four places and a notice and a pin, and is not going to say he does.** **Nobody has put a line on a board on any of the forty days since the first of the fourth month.** The tank sent nothing and nobody sent anything to the man of about fifty-five.

## Chapter 0239 — Nine Feet Of Lane

**The eleventh of the fifth month, bright, and the channel down about a foot from where it stood on the eighth, about nine people in the yard. The board at a hundred and thirty-eight, the train at four hundred and fifty-four, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-eight, the twelfth of the month one day off, the first day of the next month twenty-one days off.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said in the figures that about nine of them are going to go and ask somebody something today. **THE LANE TO THE NINTH OF THE ELEVEN HOUSES, about two hundred yards, a step at that door of two stones not repaired since before the woman of fifty-eight started carrying water there, and a plank door with a hasp on it and not a handle, and the hasp lifted and no knock.** The man at that house is about forty-four and was sitting by his own door with a mended sleeve and no coat on, and a clerk entered that he is not a clerk of a house and does not keep a tally and has never been asked a first question in this district in nine years. **The question put in that doorway was in forty-eight words: Would your house pay a toll on a thing that holds back the water, if a body four hundred miles away sent you a sheet that says one of four places owes it. I am not asking you to pay anything. I am asking you whether you would.** And the answer: **No. And I am not going to tell you what I think about the four places, either, and I do not know which of the four we are, and I have not got a copy of it and I am not going to get one.** **The woman of fifty-eight was asked first**, in his own words, whether he could ask a man at the ninth of her eleven houses a question about a toll, and she said yes in about four seconds with a reason, in **seventy-two words**: *Go and ask him. I have been carrying water to that house for nine years and I have never once asked him whether he would rather somebody else carried it, and you are going to ask him something smaller than that and you are going to ask it in his own doorway, and if you ask him it in a yard I will take the bucket off you in front of everybody.* Reported in the yard afterwards, in **eighty-seven words**: *He said that nobody has ever asked him a question about anything, and that he has heard about the sheet from his own wife, and that a toll is not a thing he has and it is not a thing his house has and he is not going to be told by a body four hundred miles away that he is the fourth of anything, and then he said that if anybody came from this yard and asked him a second thing he would answer that too.* **THE ROMANCE MOVES, IN ABOUT NINE FEET OF LANE, AT ABOUT TWENTY TO FIVE.** He asked her one question he had asked her once before, in a room, on the twenty-fourth of last month, and been refused in four seconds: **On the twenty-fourth of last month, in that room, I asked you whether you would come and stand in a channel and look at one of them, and you said no, and I said nothing at all afterwards, and I have said nothing at all for about a fortnight, and I want to say the nothing out loud so that it is not sitting in me any more.** And she said, after about nine seconds, in which the fanlight over the door did not light anything: **No. You did say nothing, and you did it on purpose, and it was the correct thing to do and I would not have forgiven you for not doing it.** Then he asked the other half: **Whether you will come on the twelfth and look at it without anybody asking you a question about it. Not because the lock needs a witness. Because you have been four hundred yards from a piece of iron for nine years, and you told about nineteen people three days ago that you wrote the rule that made this district findable, and I would like to see what you do when you are standing next to it instead of four hundred yards away from it.** **And she said she would come at about half past ten on the twelfth if the gate would open, and that a yard is a place a person walks into and not a place a person is put, and that if he tells anybody at that yard that she said she would come she will not come at all.** A clerk entered that about nine feet of lane is not a room with a shut door in it and is not a yard either, and that a sentence said there is not one of the three documents nobody owns and is not a fourth of them. A man of fifty-six said that a man who has been asked one question in nine years and has answered it is the only instrument this basin has that the four districts have not built yet.

## Chapter 0240 — Two Of Four

**The twelfth of the fifth month, clear, the frost gone out of the trough, about nineteen people in the yard, and the first day of the next month twenty days off. The board at a hundred and thirty-nine, the train at four hundred and fifty-five, the unentered days at a hundred and sixty-nine, a hundred and thirty days from the second of January.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing put two documents into the same paragraph himself before anybody else could, in **a hundred and twenty-five words**: *The sheet on that table wants one toll paid by four places, and the notice under the leg of that table wants a record asked against four places, and two of the four have not answered, and this yard has held the fourth of the four since the third of this month and a house nine miles up has held it since the same morning. I have said twice in this yard that those two fours are not the same four, and I am saying it a third time this morning with both documents in the same breath, and if a clerk writes down that they are one four then a clerk has written down the only false sentence in this district's books this month.* The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused that join, in **ninety-seven words**. **THE DECISION OF THE BLOCK, IN A HUNDRED AND TWO WORDS: Here is what I am going to do and it is two things and not nine. A body four hundred miles away has said two of its four have not answered. I am going to go and ask the two that have not answered, each of them separately, in their own doorway, about the thing they have not answered, and I am not going to take the other two with me and I am not going to ask one about the other, and I am not going up there with a sheet and a chair and a body that wants a man.** A man of fifty-six said that a body distributes a toll to four places at once and a man of twenty-one years proposes to do the opposite in a lane, and that there is no name in this basin for what a man does when he refuses to distribute. **SHE CAME AT ABOUT HALF PAST TEN, ON FOOT, WITH NO BOOK UNDER HER ARM, AND STOOD ABOUT FOUR FEET INSIDE THE GATE AND LOOKED AT THE TABLE AND THE CHAIR AND THE GROUND UNDER THE LEG WHERE THE NOTICE HAD BEEN, AND TOUCHED NONE OF THEM.** Nine people went out past the loams at about eleven and she walked at the back and not beside anybody, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing walked beside her for about half a mile and then fell in behind her and was not asked to. **THE LOCK WAS IN ITS CHANNEL WITH ABOUT NINE INCHES OF WATER STANDING ABOVE THE SILL. She stood on the bank for about a minute before she said anything and nine people stood behind her and nobody came past her.** The man of about forty-one who keeps the road house was not there and nobody sent for him. **She crouched down and put her thumb in the notch, the way a man of about forty-one had put his thumb in it on the eighth of this month, and the stone was wet and she had to take her hand off and dry it on her coat and do it again, and said, in a hundred and four words: This is where the board goes. It is not a record of anything and I can see now why a person who reads for a living would want it to be a record, and it is a hole a plank fits in, and somebody cut it four hundred years before me, and my name is not anywhere near it and my room is not anywhere near it, and I wrote a rule that made my room findable and the rule was mine and I would not write it a second time differently, and that is the whole of what I came down here for.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam said the half of that which is about the pin, in **eighty-eight words**, and said out loud that he was the one who put the seventh instrument up and did not want to be the one who took it back, and that a rule about being wrong is the same shape as the rule about being thanked that one of the nine wrote four hundred years ago and underlined twice. **AND THEN SHE SAID THE OTHER HALF OF IT BEFORE SHE GOT UP, AND IT CAME TO FOURTEEN WORDS, AND THEY ARE THE LAST EXCHANGE IN THE BLOCK: "**You did not tell anybody at that yard that I said I would come.**" / "**No.**" / "**Good. I would not have come.**"** The notice was read out for the fifth time in seven days and a clerk entered that a notice read out five times in a yard a stranger can walk into is a notice this district has made its own without anybody deciding to. **THE NINE PLACES ON THE PAGE IN THE CAVE STILL HAVE EIGHT NAMES ON THEM AND A NINTH PLACE THAT IS RULED AND EMPTY, AND NOBODY HAS PUT A LINE ON A BOARD ON ANY OF THE FORTY-TWO DAYS SINCE THE FIRST OF THE FOURTH MONTH, AND ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE NEXT MONTH A RIVAL RECORD TAKES EFFECT AGAINST FOUR PLACES OF WHICH TWO HAVE NOT ANSWERED.**

## Chapter 0241 — The Second Question

**The thirteenth of the fifth month, warm and westerly, about eleven people in the yard of Lot Seventeen at about ten, and the chair at the end of the trestle table twenty days old and unsat in. The board at a hundred and forty, the train at four hundred and fifty-six, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy, a hundred and thirty-one days from the second of January, the first of the next month nineteen days off.** **A man of about forty-four walked two hundred yards down behind the bank on his own two feet at about half past nine and sat down on the step of the building at the end of the lane, and nobody sent for him**, and a clerk entered that a person who walks into a yard has not been brought into it and can go out of it. He said so himself first, in **eighty-three words**: *A man of about forty-four walked two hundred yards down behind the bank this morning on his own two feet and sat down on the step of the building at the end of the lane, and nobody sent for him and nobody asked him to come, and nobody has asked that man a question since the eleventh of this month and I am going to ask him one now and write down what he says and not argue with a word of it.* **THE SECOND QUESTION, IN THE MIDDLE OF THE YARD AND NOT IN THE SIX THINGS, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FOUR WORDS: Last time I asked you in your own doorway whether your house would pay a toll on a thing that holds back the water, and you said no, and you said you did not know which of the four places you were and that you would answer a second question. Here is the second one. If a body four hundred miles away and a yard four hundred yards from your door were both writing down what happens to the water above this valley, and neither of them was asking you for a penny, would you want your own house's answer to be in there. I am not asking you to agree with anybody. I am asking whether you would want to be in it.** And the answer, in **a hundred and sixteen words**: *I would. And I want it in my own words and not in yours, and I want it to say that I have got a well and a drain and a woman carries water to my door and that I have not been asked about one or the other of them by anybody in nine years. And I am saying the second thing as well, which is that I do not know what is in any of it, because nobody has ever come up this lane and told me, and if a clerk writes down that I want to be in it then she has to write down that I do not know what it is.* **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join of a settlement and a household and a page with nine places on it, in a hundred and six words: Nine doors, nine pieces of paper, nine people who have to walk to the ninth one. The day somebody writes all nine of them on one sheet is the day the ninth one is a ruled place with nobody standing at it, and I have held that page in my hand and I know what an empty ruled place looks like.** A man of fifty-six said nine doors is the whole of it and that the ninth door is the one that will go wrong, and that the light was going. **The woman of fifty-eight, asked whether her eleven houses were nine doors or one, answered in a hundred and eleven words: They are eleven doors and I know the difference between a house and a place because I have been carrying water into both of them for nine years and only one of them has ever said thank you.** The man of about twenty-three refused to copy a thing this district had made up that morning, in **eighty-four words**, and asked for eleven coppers a week, and was refused, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam said what the no is, in **a hundred and twenty-three words**, and named the fifth of the five things this district does not have and said there is still no rate in this basin that turns a year into coppers. **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against was not in that yard at any hour and a clerk entered that nobody sent for him. And at the end of the day the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said, in a hundred and eight words, that about nine of them are going to walk up the road that climbs in the morning to look at one of the nine, and that nobody is sending for anybody.**

## Chapter 0242 — One Of The Two

**The fourteenth of the fifth month, bright, about nine people in the yard, a trough with four inches of rain in it, a gate that wanted a foot against the post, and the chair twenty-one days old. The board at a hundred and forty-one, the train at four hundred and fifty-seven, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-one, a hundred and thirty-two days from the second of January, the first of the next month eighteen days off, and the sheet came to this yard on the third of this month, which is eleven days back, and to a door four miles down a lane on the same morning.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam had his right arm out of the sling for the first time since the seventh of this month. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said what the day was for, in **a hundred and three words**: *I have asked one man one question in one doorway and I have been asked a second thing and got it, and this morning I am going to walk up behind this bank and ask two more people one question each, in their own doors, and I am not taking the sheet and I am not taking anybody with me, and if either of them says no then what goes in the book is no, and if neither of them says anything then what goes in the book is not asked, and not asked is not no and it is not yes either.* **THE LANE BEHIND THE BANK, about two hundred yards, a wire on the left that has not been tight since before anybody in this district was born, a woman keeping a goat at the second gate, a step of two stones and a plank door with a hasp and not a handle, and a second door about nine yards up with a handle and a curtain, and the hasp lifted and no knock on the first and a knock on the second, and nobody has ever been told the difference between the two doors in this district and it is in the minute.** **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against came into that yard at about two in the afternoon with his coat open and one boot caked to the ankle and said, in ninety-eight words, that he had been to one of the two and was not going to say a word about the other one, and that the reason is that he has not been to him, and that a man of fifty-six said going is not the same as arriving and he was right.** **WHAT THE MAN AT THE FIRST PLACE SAID, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY WORDS: He is about fifty-two and he keeps about nine people in a house on the far side of this valley with a slate roof and the sheet came to his door on the third of this month, the same morning it came to ours, and it went under his door and nobody in that house read it out. He has still got it. He said he will not answer it, he said the reason is that he is not going to be a party of the fourth of four, and then he said the second thing, which is that nobody has come up that lane since his wife died and he would like somebody to come, and he said that to a stranger in his own doorway, and I have written down both of those and I have not written down which of them he said first.** The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join of a sentence said at a door and a line on a page, in **ninety-seven words**. **THE REGISTRAR, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY WORDS: That notice takes effect on the first of the next month and it says a record is asked against four places, and two of them have not answered, and as of this afternoon one of the four has been asked a question in a doorway by a man who went on his own two feet and wrote down what he was told, and that is an answer, and it is not mine, and a body four hundred miles away does not have to be told it twice, and I am not going to be the one who tells them, because a registrar who carries another district's answer down a road is carrying a page out of her own office and there is a rule about that and I wrote it.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing came back at about four with nothing in his hands and said what the two answers were, in **ninety-nine words**: *One man said no with a reason. One woman said she would answer in four days and gave a reason about a child, and I am not going to argue with either of them, and I am not going to count them, and I am going to write both of them down in their own words this evening and to-morrow morning. I have asked three doors out of nine. That is three and I am not going to say it out loud twice, because a number that a yard says twice is a number a clerk can ask for.* **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A TALLY FOR SIX HOUSEHOLDS ASKED WHETHER HIS SIX WERE ONE OF THE NINE SO THAT HE WOULD NOT HAVE TO LIE ABOUT IT LATER.**

## Chapter 0243 — Past The Turn

**The fifteenth of the fifth month, clear and cold, about nine people on the lane behind the bank by ten past seven, and a clerk of nineteen years wrote the figures on the back of a document somebody else printed and said she would not do it again. The board at a hundred and forty-two, the train at four hundred and fifty-eight, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-two, a hundred and thirty-three days from the second of January, the first of the next month seventeen days off, and the ninth of the nine printed nights was the twentieth of last month, which is twenty-five days back.** Nobody sent for anybody. The road out past the loams is about four miles, the top field of a farm whose legal owner is not in this district is on the left as the road starts to climb, the climb is about nine miles, and **the last half mile of it is a goat track along a shoulder with about two feet of drop on the left and a wire along the top of that drop that has not been taut for longer than anybody up there can remember.** **AT ABOUT HALF PAST TEN THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING PUT HIS LEFT HAND ON THAT WIRE TO GET PAST A MAN CARRYING TWO BUCKETS, AND IT TOOK THE SKIN OFF THE INSIDE OF HIS WRIST ABOUT THE LENGTH OF A THUMB, AND HE DID NOT STOP AND HE DID NOT SAY ANYTHING.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road went past the field gate this time and did not turn back. At about eleven the man of about forty-one who keeps the road house at the top of the road was standing outside it with his stick, and he had not been sent for and had not been asked a first thing, **and a clerk entered that this district has never once given that man a day to be here on.** The board that goes in the notch at the top lock stands on end against the north wall of that building. **He said, in a hundred and fifty-six words, that he did not put it in and his father did not, that there is somebody who comes in March and goes down again before the water is down, that he does not know what he is called and has not asked and is not going to ask now that they have all been standing on his shoulder, because the day he asks is the day there is a man at the top of that road instead of a house, and this house has never had a man in it.** **Then about two hundred yards above the road house, on a piece of ground about the size of a room with a drop on two sides, THE BOARD WAS IN THE NOTCH, wet to about a hand's breadth above the water, the water standing about a foot over the board, and the road below about a foot under.** A man of about fifty-six said it out loud, in **a hundred and twenty-five words**: *Four generations of people have kept this and not one of them is on a page and not one of them has ever been asked, and none of us is a keeper, and I want that written down before anybody starts.* **THE FINDING, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTY-THREE WORDS: We can say the board is in. We can say the water is about a foot over the board and the road below is shut. And we can say that nobody in this basin is holding this, because there is nothing on it to hold. What we cannot say is whether the ninth of the nine nights happened on the twentieth of last month, and we cannot say it because a plank does not keep nights, and there is not one mark on this frame and no book and no man, and it is not anybody's job to remember it, and that is the first thing this district has ever found out by walking to a thing instead of saying a number out loud.** A man of about nineteen who counts said, in **a hundred and five words**, that he counted nine going up and nine coming off and did not count the board or the water or the four generations, and that if he had put a figure on it that afternoon he would have been the first man in this basin who made one up. **They came down about five hours with the light going, and a man of about thirty-four who digs loam went with the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing as far as the top field and took the wire in his own right hand and twisted it and pushed it through in about four seconds and said nothing about it.**

## Chapter 0244 — The First Of Nine

**The sixteenth of the fifth month, overcast and warm for the hour, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the man the figure of twenty-one years is against gone out of it at about six that morning with a coat and a knapsack. The board at a hundred and forty-three, the train at four hundred and fifty-nine, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-three, a hundred and thirty-four days from the second of January, the first of the next month sixteen days off.** **THE FIRST OF NINE LOCAL CHARTERS WAS READ OUT AND READ BACK, WHICH IS THE RULE OF THE COUNTER:** > **THIS IS ONE CHARTER OF NINE AND IT STANDS ALONE. THE OTHER EIGHT ARE IN EIGHT OTHER PLACES AND MAY NOT BE WRITTEN ON IT, AND A PERSON WHO HOLDS ONE OF THEM IS NOT A BEARER OF THIS ONE.**
> **IT IS FOR THE GROUND, THE DRAINS AND THE WATER OF ONE SETTLEMENT. THE NUMBER OF THAT SETTLEMENT AND THE NAME OF IT ARE NOT ON THIS SHEET, AND A HOUSE IS NOT A BEARER, AND A LIST OF HOUSES IS NOT A BEARER.**
> **THE BEARERS ARE THE FIVE NAMES UNDER THE LINE. A NAME GOES UNDER THE LINE ONLY IF THE PERSON IT BELONGS TO PUT IT THERE, IN THEIR OWN ROOM, WITH SOMEBODY STANDING IN THE DOOR, AND A NAME PUT UNDER THE LINE BY ANYBODY ELSE IS NOT ON THIS SHEET.**
> **THE TERM IS FIVE SEASONS. THE TERM BEGINS ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE NEXT MONTH AND IS COUNTED FROM THAT DAY AND NOT FROM THE DAY THE SHEET WAS SIGNED.**
> **ANY ONE OF THE FIVE MAY GIVE UP THE WHOLE OF IT ON THE FIRST DAY OF ANY SEASON AFTER THE FIRST, BY SAYING SO OUT LOUD TO ANY ONE OF THE OTHER FOUR. THE GIVING UP IS DONE ON THAT DAY. A GIVING UP IS NOT A REFUSAL AND IS NOT A COLUMN.**
> **A GIVING UP DOES NOT GIVE BACK A SEASON. NOTHING SOWN IS PULLED UP. THE COST OF A GIVING UP IS NOT MONEY. IT IS THAT THE ARRANGEMENTS ABOVE THAT SETTLEMENT GO BACK TO BEING HELD BY NOBODY'S AGREEMENT, AND THAT WORK ALREADY DONE IS NOT RETURNED, AND THAT THE SPENDING A GIVING UP DOES NOT RETURN IS NOT PRICED HERE BECAUSE THERE IS NO RATE IN THIS BASIN THAT TURNS A YEAR INTO COPPERS AND THERE NEVER HAS BEEN ONE. NOBODY OWNS THE WATER. A BEARER HOLDS A SHARE IN AN ARRANGEMENT, A SHARE IS NOT THE WHOLE OF IT, AND THE WHOLE OF IT IS NOT ANYBODY'S.**
**The first name this district has said out loud in a yard this month, and the sheet is in a room four hundred yards away in a book that cannot be carried out of it.** **THE COST OF A GIVING UP, IN TWO SPEECHES BY A MAN WHO IS NOT ONE OF THE FIVE AND DOES NOT LIVE IN ANY OF THEIR ROOMS. The first, a hundred and twenty-five words: A giving up does not cost money and it does not cost a season and the sheet says both of those, and the sheet is not wrong. It costs the work. There are about four hundred yards of drain round that house that have been dug this month by a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing and by two other men, and the dig does not come back out of the ground because five people decided in one week that they would rather not.** And the second, a hundred and fifty-nine words: *If those five give it up then whatever is being agreed about the water above four districts stops being agreed by them, and nobody else in this basin has agreed to anything yet, and so what happens above that water after a giving up is not nobody's business, it is everybody's, and everybody includes the people who are not in the five, and there is not one of them in this yard today who has been asked what they think about that.* **A clerk entered that no bearer was asked what a withdrawal would cost them and that the record about the five says not asked.** **THE REGISTRAR REFUSED TO SIGN IT, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-ONE WORDS: I have not signed that sheet and I am not going to sign it, and it is not a small thing to say in a yard with about nineteen people in it, because a registrar who signs a charter is a registrar who has put her room behind somebody's arrangement.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale gave the yard nothing, in a hundred and five words, and said she would read the record back at the counter herself. **THE METHOD OF THE NINE, SAID OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY WORDS SO THAT A CLERK COULD WRITE IT DOWN**, and a clerk entered that nine sheets in nine places is not one sheet with nine places on it, and that a page with nine places ruled on it is the shape of a page that came out of a cave and nobody in this district is going to make that shape again.

## Chapter 0245 — Eight Other Places

**The seventeenth of the fifth month, grey and still, about nine people in the yard at ten, and three of the sheets read out the day before were not in it. The board at a hundred and forty-four, the train at four hundred and sixty, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-four, a hundred and thirty-five days from the second of January, the first of the next month fifteen days off.** The clerk of nineteen years carried the three back about four hundred yards in a coat and entered that she had not been asked to. **THE THREE TERMS, IN NINETY-SEVEN WORDS: The first one is five seasons. The second one is three. The third one is five. They are not the same sheet and they are not the same term and I did not write any of them and I could not have written one of them without somebody telling me what the term was, and I did not suggest a single one of the three. A charter whose term comes out of the middle of a yard is a charter with a holder in it and this one has got five names under a line instead.** **The fourth sheet is one season, and a man of seventy-one gave the reason out loud with nobody standing in the room, in a hundred and seven words: he does not know what there will be in this valley when he is not here to ask anybody, and a man who is seventy-one does not get promised five seasons by a yard of about nineteen people on the strength of a good morning.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam refused the join of a building and a settlement, in a hundred and twenty-three words: a building four miles down a lane that has denied nobody entry in nine years is not a settlement, it is one man and one woman and about nine years of not being turned out, and if anybody puts them under a line then a clerk is asking a question they have not been asked.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road came in at about half past eleven with a fifth sheet inside his coat which he had not been asked to fetch, **and there were no names under the line on that one.** **A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX ON THE NINTH, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THREE WORDS: That is the ninth. Nine sheets and eight of them have got names under the line and the ninth one has not got any, and I am going to say the two things about that and I am going to stop at the second one on purpose. The first is that the people at that place have not been asked. The second is that I am not going to say whether an empty line on a sheet from this month is the same shape of thing as an empty place on a page out of a cave, and anybody who wants to know can go and work it out and I am not going to work it out in a yard.** The man of about twenty-three refused to keep a copy of it, in **a hundred and eight words**: *A copy of a sheet with five names on it is a copy of a sheet. A copy of a sheet with nobody on it is a copy of an absence, and a man who hands a copy of an absence round this basin is a man who has made the absence into a thing that travels, and the day it travels somebody is going to fill it in.* The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households asked whether his six were one of the nine, in a hundred and twenty-four words, **so that he would not have to lie about it later.** **THE FOUR THIS YARD CANNOT PRODUCE, IN A HUNDRED AND SIXTY-THREE WORDS: The fifth is with a woman who keeps a goat. The sixth is in a village about nine miles the other way and sent me four words back down the road on a piece of paper with nothing else on it. The seventh and the eighth I have not seen and I am not going to describe them to you this morning because I have not been in the rooms they were made in. You have four of the nine in front of you and there are four of the nine that this yard cannot produce, and I would rather say that out loud than have somebody find out in June.** The woman of fifty-eight said a bucket of hers is about eleven pounds full and that nobody in this basin has ever asked her.

## Chapter 0246 — The Third Column

**The eighteenth of the fifth month, warm and low, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the man the figure of twenty-one years is against came through the gate at about a quarter to ten with a knapsack he did not have on the fourteenth. The board at a hundred and forty-five, the train at four hundred and sixty-one, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-five, a hundred and thirty-six days from the second of January, the first of the next month fourteen days off, and there is a sheet on this table that is not any of the nine and is bigger than any of the nine.** **WHAT HE HAD DONE, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIVE WORDS: the second of the two is a man of about sixty who keeps a house with about five people in it, and the sheet came to his door on the third of this month and he read it out to his own household, which nobody in four places has done, and he said he would not answer it and that he would put his own name under a line of his own if there was one, and then he said he would not do that either until he had been asked what the line was for.** **THE COVENANT, READ OUT AND READ BACK, WHICH IS THE RULE OF THE COUNTER:** > **THIS IS A COVENANT AND NOT A CONSTITUTION, AND IT SAYS SO AT THE TOP OF ITS OWN FACE.**
> **NINE STRUCTURES STAND IN THE WATER ABOVE FOUR DISTRICTS. THEY ARE NINE AND NOT TEN. THEY ARE FINITE. NO PERSON AND NO BODY IS THE HOLDER OF ANY OF THEM, AND NO PERSON SHALL HOLD THIS ALONE.**
> **THE STEWARDS ARE FOUR. THEY ARE CHANGED EVERY SEASON BY A SHOW OF HANDS IN THE PLACES THEY COME FROM. A STEWARD CARRIES THE FIGURES FOR ONE SEASON, IS NOT A HOLDER, IS NOT A BEARER OF THE WHOLE, AND MAY BE ASKED A QUESTION BY ANYBODY AT ANY TIME INSIDE THAT SEASON AND BY NOBODY AT ALL OUTSIDE IT.**
> **THE TOLLS ARE PUBLIC. EVERY FIGURE IS WRITTEN WHERE A STRANGER MAY WALK UP AND READ IT. NO TOLL IS FASTENED TO A PARTY OF A PLACE AND ONLY TO A NAMED PERSON, AND A TOLL THAT CANNOT BE FASTENED TO A NAMED PERSON IS NOT COLLECTED AND IS NOT REFUSED, AND THE RECORD SAYS NOT ASKED.**
> **THE TERM IS THREE SEASONS AND BEGINS ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE NEXT MONTH. ANY ONE OF THE NINE LOCAL CHARTERS MAY GIVE ITSELF UP ON THE FIRST DAY OF ANY SEASON AFTER THE FIRST, AND A GIVING UP DOES NOT GIVE BACK A SEASON AND IS NOT A REFUSAL.**
> **IF A PLACE DISAGREES, THE DISAGREEMENT GOES BY ITS OWN ROAD TO ANOTHER REGION AND IS PUT IN A BOOK THERE THAT A STRANGER MAY WALK UP TO. THE GOING IS CARRIED BY A PERSON AND NOT BY THE TOLL, AND THE COST OF THE GOING IS NOT PRICED HERE.**
> **THE TABLE HAS THREE COLUMNS. THE FIRST IS HEADED BY WHAT IS HELD. THE SECOND IS HEADED BY WHO HOLDS IT. THE THIRD IS HEADED BY A WORD AND NOT BY A NAME, AND THE THIRD IS EMPTY ON THE FACE OF THIS SHEET, AND IT IS EMPTY BECAUSE THE PEOPLE WHO WOULD GO IN IT HAVE NOT BEEN ASKED, AND A PERSON WHO HAS NOT BEEN ASKED HAS NOT REFUSED AND CANNOT BE COUNTED EITHER WAY. NOBODY IS TO FILL IT WHO HAS NOT BEEN ASKED. NOBODY IS TO GO TO A HOUSE THAT LET A PAGE BE COPIED AND ASK IT ABOUT A PAGE IT WAS ALLOWED TO COPY, IN ORDER TO FILL IT.**
**A clerk wrote the three columns out in chalk on a slate she had brought from her own office and had not been asked to bring, and the third was about the width of a finger and a half and about the height of two fingers, and it was ruled and it was empty.** **WHERE THE SHEET IS GOING TO LIVE, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-NINE WORDS: That sheet is going into the book in my room and nowhere else, and a stranger may walk up to that door and ask to read it and will be allowed to read it, and that is the whole of what I have agreed to and I have not agreed to anything else. None of the nine local charters goes in there.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM ON THE FOUR STEWARDS, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FOUR WORDS: you do not write it down by not having a holder. You write it down by having four of them and a door. I am not going to be a steward and nobody has asked me and I am saying so before somebody decides I am the obvious one.** A man of fifty-six said nine people wrote that sentence four hundred years ago to stop a man exactly like this one, and that a sheet in a yard is not a page in a cave, in **a hundred and twenty-five words**. The man of about fifty-five refused to be a steward before he was asked, in **a hundred and twenty-nine words**, and asked for it to be read back. The man of about twenty-three carried the copy to three of the four counters and the fourth is a man of about nineteen who was not well enough to be asked. **The notice was read out for the sixth time in that yard and the first of those six was thirteen days ago.**

## Chapter 247 — Show Of Hands In A Barn

**The nineteenth of the fifth month, warm, about eleven people walking up the road that climbs from about half past six, the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing going up with his left wrist inside his cuff and not taking the cuff off all day. The board at a hundred and forty-six, the train at four hundred and sixty-two, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-six, a hundred and thirty-seven days from the second of January, the first of the next month thirteen days off.** **THE BARN, about nineteen people in it, eleven of whom came up the road and eight of whom live in the country above the water, about thirteen of the nineteen over fifty, one window about nine inches square high up on the south wall, a floor of beaten earth swept that morning and already going brown at the middle, and one lantern on a shelf that was not lit.** **A man of about sixty-three said before the sheet was put that he was not going to put a hand up, in a hundred and thirteen words: I have been in this room since I could walk and I have voted in it about eleven times and I have never once been asked what a thing I was voting on was going to cost me. So I am not putting a hand up today, and I am not putting one down either, and if a clerk wants to write down that I said no she had better come and sit in my house for a week first.** **Then it was put and about seven hands went up and two went down and the clerk did not count the rest of the room on purpose.** **THE PANEL, ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT HALF PAST TWELVE, DISAGREED WITH IN A BARN BY THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM IN A HUNDRED AND SEVENTEEN WORDS: That third one is wrong in the way that costs a person, and I know that because I have watched it cost a person twice this month. A man who gives up an arrangement has not refused it. He has done the thing the arrangement was written to let him do, and if you call that a refusal then the right to give up is not a right, it is a trap with a door on it.** **THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST ON THE FOUR PLACES, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SIX WORDS: A thing was agreed in this room by the people in this room, and the four places by the roads under the water are not in this room and are not in this country, and they are going to sign their own parts on their own doors and nobody in this barn is going to hold their part up and read it out and call it done. One of those four is this yard and one of those four is a house nine miles up a road and both of them say they are the fourth of the four, and that is not resolved and it is not going to be resolved in a barn.** Then a man who had come up from below the water and had not been asked by anybody in this district read out a piece of paper about nine lines long, **saying that a body which administers the water above does not bind anybody in that room, that its answer is not the answer of that room, and that where the two disagree the disagreement is not a fault and is not a vote and does not go in a column.** A clerk entered that a body which has lost the immediate vote has not been asked about losing it, that a body is not a person, and that a council acquires no face in a barn. **And at the field gate at about half past three the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing could not get his hand closed on a fence post, and the man who digs loam took the wire in his own right hand and did it in about four seconds and said nothing about it.**

## Chapter 248 — A Public Index

**The twentieth of the fifth month, warm and still, about nine people going up the road that climbs again at about half past five because the light in a barn with one window is better early. The board at a hundred and forty-seven, the train at four hundred and sixty-three, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-seven, a hundred and thirty-eight days from the second of January, the first of the next month twelve days off.** The man of about forty-one was outside his own door at about ten past seven with his stick and was not asked a first thing, and came up the two hundred yards of shoulder and stood at the edge of it for about four minutes and said nothing at all. **THE GRANT, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-THREE WORDS: They have decided to put my name in a column in that book and to call me the man who convenes, and I am not going to stand in this barn and say I do not know what I think about it, because I do know. Four months ago I stood at the back of a book in this district and I told anybody who would listen that a person at the back of a book is a thing that can be fastened to. This is the same thing happening to me from the other end and I am not going to be the man who is surprised by it.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING SAID IT OUT LOUD, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT WORDS: I asked the nine questions. I want that said out loud in this barn before anybody writes anything, because the man you are putting in that column is not the man who knocked on nine doors, and I knocked on nine doors, and the man who went two days of road to a second place that had not answered is the man you are putting in the column. I am not asking for it to be changed.** **THE PRICE, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-NINE WORDS: Here is what it costs and it costs one thing and I am not going to dress it up as two. On the eighteenth of this month four hundred yards from where I am standing, a woman I have worked beside for eleven months wrote a rule that says a book is kept in one room by one person, and a body four hundred miles away wrote a notice in which that room is described and my name is not on it, and she stood in a yard and said out loud that not being named is not a comfort. Today my name goes in a column in a book a stranger can walk up to, and from this afternoon a stranger can walk into that yard and say his name instead of asking for a page. And this yard's own rule is that a name put on a figure cannot be taken off it by the person it is put on, and I wrote that rule into this district's practice in April, and I am not going to try to take it off me this morning.** **THE REGISTRAR SIGNED ONE LINE OUT OF SIX AND SAID SO OUT LOUD, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-NINE WORDS: I have signed the sixth line and not the other five, and the sixth line is the one about where the figures are written and who may read them, and I have signed it as the person who keeps the book and not as a bearer and not as a holder and not as a steward.
