My authority in that book is the authority of one woman in one room and it is not shared with the man whose name is in the column and it is not diminished by his being in it.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households said he was glad of a thing that was not on the paper, in a hundred and twenty-four words, and asked for it to be said that a hand put up for one reason is not a hand put up for another. **And at ten past seven that evening, at the gate of that yard, a stranger asked whether the man who convenes was there and said he had come about four miles on the strength of the name and not on the strength of a page, and he was told the man was standing about nine feet away with a bucket in his hand, and a clerk entered that this is the first time in four months that anybody has come to that yard asking for a person instead of asking for a page.**

## Chapter 249 — Nine Walls

**The twenty-first of the fifth month, grey, the yard full from about a quarter to ten, five things on the trestle table where there had been three for nine days, and the chair twenty-eight days old. The board at a hundred and forty-eight, the train at four hundred and sixty-four, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-eight, a hundred and thirty-nine days from the second of January, which is twenty weeks and no days, the first of the next month eleven days off, and there is a letter on that table with a man's name on it.** **THE LETTER, FOLDED ONCE, PUT THERE BY NOBODY, READ OUT AND READ BACK:** > **TO THE PEOPLE OF THE FOUR PLACES BY THE ROADS BELOW THE WATER.**
> **A COVENANT HAS BEEN AGREED ABOVE YOU AND IT TAKES EFFECT ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE NEXT MONTH. THE COVENANT HAS NO FOUNDER. IT IS THE FOUNDER OF THIS THING TO SAY THAT IT HAS ONE, AND WE HAVE PUT HIS NAME UNDER IT IN A BOOK THAT ANY PERSON MAY WALK UP TO AND READ.**
> **THE MAN WHOSE NAME IS IN THAT BOOK IS THE MAN THIS DISTRICT FOUNDED IN THE MATTER OF THE WATER ABOVE IT, AND HE HAS BEEN ASKED FOR IT BY A PERSON WHO CAME FOUR MILES ON THE STRENGTH OF THE NAME, AND HE ANSWERED.**
> **HE MAY NOT TAKE THE NAME OFF. HE HAS SAID SO OUT LOUD AND HE HAS NOT ASKED US TO TAKE IT OFF. THE COLUMN IS WHERE A THING THAT HAS BEEN BUILT STANDS, AND A THING THAT HAS BEEN BUILT HAS A NAME ON IT, AND THE NAME IS NOT THE SAME AS THE THING.**
> **WE HAVE LOST THE IMMEDIATE VOTE. WE ARE NOT COMPLAINING ABOUT IT. WE ARE TELLING YOU THAT IT IS GONE, SO THAT NOBODY IN ANY OF THE FOUR PLACES CAN GO ON SAYING THAT A BODY FOUR HUNDRED MILES AWAY DECIDES WHAT HAPPENS TO THE WATER ABOVE THEM. IT IS NOT TRUE ANY MORE, AND THE ONLY THING THAT HAS REPLACED IT IS FOUR STEWARDS AND A DOOR.**
> **THIS LETTER IS GOING ON NINE WALLS. A WALL IS NOT AN ADDRESS. NOBODY IS TO WRITE A REPLY TO IT AND SEND IT BACK, BECAUSE THERE IS NOWHERE TO SEND IT TO, AND A REPLY THAT COMES BACK TO A BODY FOUR HUNDRED MILES AWAY IS A DOCUMENT THIS DISTRICT DID NOT WRITE AND DID NOT ASK FOR.**
**THE WORD, REFUSED, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT WORDS: The word on that is one this district has spent four months refusing, and there are two ways to answer it. A man can stand up in a yard and say he is not one, and then a stranger can come four miles and ask whether he really is not. Or he can say nothing, and then the word stands on a piece of paper that nine walls are going to have on them. I am not going to do the first one. I would like a clerk to write down that I said that and that I did not say the word.** **THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST, IN A HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-TWO WORDS: That letter has my name on it twice and I put one of them there myself in a room yesterday with about nineteen people in it, and I have known since that room that it was going to come out in a piece of paper. What I am going to say is that a letter is a thing with no doorway in it. I have been in about nine doorways this month and none of them was this.** The fourth line of the offer was not read out, in a hundred and sixteen words, and the refusals to read are nine and the departure is unspent. **The registrar asked to read the notice out one more time, got through the first line, came to the second line, which is her own rule of the twenty-first of last month, stopped for about nine seconds, and did not go on, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing read the rest of it, and it is the seventh time that notice has been read out in that yard.** The letter went up the road that climbs at about two and was put into nine things that were not envelopes, and a clerk entered that a wall is not an address. **THE SECOND PANEL, ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT HALF PAST THREE, DISAGREED WITH BY THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SIX WORDS: A figure is a thing. A thing cannot say no. A person can say no, and has been saying no in this yard since the first of April, and the reason a toll can be fastened to a person is exactly that the person can say no to it. The moment a thing with a name on it cannot say no, the name stops being a person and becomes a handle.**

## Chapter 250 — Two Lines On A Slate

**The twenty-second of the fifth month, bright and warm for the hour, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the chair at the end of the trestle table had stood there twenty-nine days and nobody had sat in it on any of the twenty-nine. The board at a hundred and forty-nine, the train at four hundred and sixty-five, the unentered days at a hundred and seventy-nine, a hundred and forty days separate the second of January and this morning, and that is twenty weeks and no days, the first of the next month ten days off, and there is nothing left in this yard to read out this month.** **THE MONTH, GIVEN BY A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX WHO WAS NOT ASKED, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-SEVEN WORDS: Nine charters and nine separate rooms and nine rights to give the whole thing up on the first day of any season after the first, and not one sheet of the nine in this yard, and four terms among the nine and three different lengths in them. Nine pieces of iron in the water and none of them on any of the nine and no person holding any of them. A covenant with a third column in it ruled and empty on its face because the people who would go in it have not been asked. And a letter on nine walls with a man's name on it twice that he put there himself. That is the month and I am not making anything out of it and I am not asking anybody to agree with it.** A clerk entered the notice at **seven times, seventeen days**, that the fourth and fifth charters are in four other places, that this yard has not got the fourth in front of it that morning and has never had the fifth in front of it at all, that the ninth has no names under the line and the record says not asked, and **that this district has four different ninths in it this month and they are four and are not added together and are not a pattern.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale said no before she was asked, in a hundred and five words, and said she would read the record back at the counter herself. The man of about fifty-five sent nothing and nobody sent anything to him. **THE LAST OF THE BLOCK'S FINDINGS, BY THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM, IN TWO HUNDRED AND SIX WORDS: On the first of April there were nine things in the water above four districts and this district did not know whether they were open or shut, and there was nobody to ask, because a thing in the water is not a person. Today this district knows that the board is in the notch at one of them and that the road below it is shut and that nobody is holding it, and it still does not know whether the ninth of the nine nights happened. A month has bought us one lock out of nine and one sentence that cannot be got out of us.** **THE SLATE, at about half past five in the afternoon, carried about four hundred yards of lane and went past the kitchen at the end of it, and it has gone past there eleven times before and this is the twelfth, and no clerk in this district has written down on which days any of the eleven went past, and that was not repaired this month.** She was in the kitchen doorway with her coat off and her sleeves turned up and a cloth over one shoulder and a piece of chalk in her hand before he got to the step. **She wrote two lines and they were about a fanlight over the door of her own office that has not lit anything in four months and that she has not had a man out to, and that nobody has ever asked her about it and that she is not going to tell him either, in that yard or in a room or in a lane.** He said **I am not going to ask you why.** She said that was the second time he had not asked her something, and both of them were on the slate now, and he was not to tell anybody what was on it, and she was not going to be told, because if she was told it back he would get it slightly wrong and in six months one of them would be arguing about what she said on a day in the middle of that week. He said that was fair. **She said it is not fair, it is convenient, and she was telling him it is not fair because he had been fair about nine things this month and she did not want him to be fair about this one without being told that it is not.** A clerk of nineteen years was four hundred yards away and was not told until about seven, and the record came from one of the two and not the other, and he said there were two lines on a slate and he was not going to read them out, and she entered that a slate is not a book and is not a page and is not one of the three documents nobody owns and did not become a fourth of them. **And at about half past six the man of about thirty-four who digs loam went out of the gate with his right arm out of the sling, which was folded in the inside pocket of his coat, and the woman of fifty-eight has not asked for it back and has not said she wants it, and he went down to the sluice end of the loams and stood in the channel up to about the knee with his left arm hanging at his side and did not touch the frame, and the water had come back up over the ten days and there was about a foot of it standing above the sill again, and there was no hand on any part of the frame and there had not been one since he got there.**

The block total is 25,335 words by `wc -w` and the block carries thirty-seven class-one counted claims and one class-two claim, and all thirty-eight reproduce, **all thirty-eight re-measured after the review repair, which moved the Chapter 253 claim from a hundred and eighty-three to a hundred and eighty-seven.****

## Chapter 0251 — Three First Days

**The first of the sixth month, warm and low with a wind off the west, about nineteen people in the yard of Lot Seventeen at about ten, and the chair at the end of the trestle table thirty-nine days old and unsat in. The board at 159, the train at 475 days, the unentered days at 189, and a hundred and fifty days from the second of January, which is twenty-one weeks and three days.** **THREE THINGS START ON THIS MORNING AND A CLERK IS NOT GOING TO WRITE THEM IN ONE SENTENCE**, in **a hundred and thirty-eight words**: the first day of the first season on the first of the nine charters; the first day a record set against four places four hundred miles away is in force; and the first morning on which *last month* and *the month before last* in this district mean two different months than they meant yesterday, because *last month* is now the fifth month, *the month before last* is now the fourth, and the third month is two months back and is to be called that. A clerk entered that she wrote the day on a slate and not in a book, that a slate is not a book and is not a page and did not become a fourth of the three documents this district does not own, and that a clerk who has entered the same sentence three months running has entered a habit and not a finding. **THE EIGHTH READING OF THE RIVAL RECORD REFUSED OUT LOUD BY THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY WORDS: It is in force. That is what in force means… reading it out to a yard a stranger can walk into is not answering it and is not refusing it and is not asking anybody anything, and I have done it seven times in seventeen days for a yard that wanted to hear a piece of paper, and I am not going to do it an eighth time on the morning it starts being true.** A clerk entered that the refusals to read are nine and did not move, that there is still no date in any ledger for the next reading, that the departure is unspent, and that a notice being in force and unanswered is not a smaller thing than a notice not being in force and unanswered and is in one respect a larger one. **The count of the morning given out loud by a man of fifty-six in a hundred and forty-six words, who is not a clerk and has said so about himself about a dozen times in four months.** **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SIX WHO KEEPS A SCALE, WHO HAS STOOD AT THAT SCALE NINE YEARS AND WEIGHS ABOUT NINE SACKS A DAY, REFUSED HER FOUR EMPTY HOUSES AGAIN BEFORE SHE WAS ASKED, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-SIX WORDS: I have four houses standing empty and I offered them to this yard on the last day of the month before last and nobody took them and I am not offering them again… You have got nine sheets out in nine rooms and not one door in any of the nine that a woman at a weighbridge can walk through, and I am not going to be a tenth thing this yard counts. If a clerk writes down that I am in the count I will read it back at the counter myself, and this time there will be two of us reading it.** A clerk entered that the eight did not move and that the refusals about the ninth holding are seven and are not the same figure. **THE SHEET, FOUND AT ABOUT A QUARTER TO TWELVE AND NAiled TO THE GATEPOST AT THE HEIGHT OF A MAN'S SHOULDER BY NOBODY, READ OUT AND READ BACK:** > **THE PEOPLE LIVING IN THE GROUND IN THE SHADOW OF THE HALL, WHOSE NAMES HAVE NEVER BEEN ENTERED, ARE TO BE MOVED.**
> **THERE ARE FOUR HUNDRED AND ELEVEN OF THEM. THAT NUMBER WAS ARRIVED AT BY COUNTING THE PEOPLE WHO ANSWERED A DOOR.**
> **THEY ARE TO BE MOVED ON OR BEFORE THE FIRST DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER NEXT. THE NUMBER IS NOT TO BE ENTERED AGAIN AND IS NOT TO BE ASKED FOR A SECOND TIME.**
A clerk entered that the sheet names one building and no place and no person, that a building is not a place name, and that the sheet says the Hall and does not say which Hall. **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam on the number, in a hundred and thirty-seven words: A number arrived at by counting the people who answered a door is a count of the answerers and not a count of the people… the people in the shadow of that thing did not open. That is the entire method they have got and it is nine years old and it is the only thing standing between them and a piece of paper, and somebody knocked.** **The man of fifty-six derived the day out loud in a hundred and one words — this month has thirty days, the month after has thirty-one, thirty and thirty-one is sixty-one — and a clerk entered that he counted it off this morning and not off a sheet.** **THE GATE WOULD NOT OPEN and it took the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing four goes with his left hand and a fifth with his right, and a clerk entered that nobody counted the goes.** The man of about nineteen who counts stood about four feet off that post for about two minutes and counted nothing. The woman of fifty-eight came down the bank with two buckets at about four and nobody counted them. **A clerk entered FIVE DIFFERENT NINTHS IN THIS DISTRICT AND NOT FOUR, being the ninth of the nine charters, the ninth of the eleven houses, the ninth of the nine printed nights, the ninth place on the page out of the cave and the ninth of the ten days this block runs, and that they are five and are not added together and are not a pattern, and that a clerk who counted four of them last month and counted five this month has not found anything and has done the thing a clerk does.** The sixth month was one day old.

## Chapter 0252 — The Ones Who Answered

**The second of the sixth month, grey and still, about eleven people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty days old. The board at 160, the train at 476, the unentered days at 190, a hundred and fifty-one from the second of January.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said what the morning was for in a hundred and fourteen words: **we are going to work out where four hundred and eleven came from, and we are going to do it in the open and in daylight and with about eleven people watching, and I am not going to knock on one door to find out. If somebody in the shadow of that thing wants to tell this yard how the number was got, they can come down this lane on their own two feet, and if they do not, then this yard's figure about them is nothing.** A clerk entered that the record about the four hundred yards of lane and the two hundred behind the bank says not asked and that the four columns of not-askings are four and she did not rule one. **THE SUM, DONE OUT LOUD BY THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM, IN A HUNDRED AND SEVENTEEN WORDS: You cannot get four hundred and eleven by knocking… Which means somebody opened. Which means somebody in the shadow of that Hall answered a question about their own neighbours, and either they were asked nicely and they answered, or they were not asked and they answered anyway, and the sheet does not say which and does not say who.** A clerk entered that the second line of a printed sheet is a finding about a method and not about a person, and that the record about whoever answered says not asked. **A MAN OF ABOUT NINETEEN WHO COUNTS DECLINED, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY WORDS: I counted four things yesterday and I am going to say what they were and then I am going to stop… that is everything I can see from here and none of it is four hundred and eleven, and I did not go and look for the rest, and I am not going to, because looking for the rest means walking into the ground in the shadow of that thing and asking people how many of them there are, and that is the knock again, only slower.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM WENT DOWN INTO THE CHANNEL AT THE SLUICE END OF THE LOAMS AT ABOUT HALF PAST TWO AND PUT HIS RIGHT HAND FLAT ON THE SILL UNDER THE WATER AND MOVED IT NINE INCHES AND BACK, AND WAS IN IT FOR ABOUT TWO MINUTES, AND COULD NOT CLOSE HIS RIGHT HAND FOR THE REST OF THE AFTERNOON, AND AT ABOUT HALF PAST FIVE COULD NOT GET IT CLOSED ON THE HANDLE OF A PAIL, AND THE WOMAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT TOOK THE PAIL OFF HIM AND DID NOT ASK HIM ANYTHING.** A clerk entered that a hand on a sill is not a hand on a lock and that a person standing in the water next to a thing that does not need him is not holding it, and that this is the same sentence as the one entered on the twenty-second of last month and that a clerk is not going to enter the same sentence a second time without saying she is entering it again. **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A ROAD REFUSED A SECOND THING IN A HUNDRED AND TEN WORDS: I have been asked one question in this district since the first of the month that is now last month and I have given one answer… If this yard wants to know how many doors there are in the ground in the shadow of that Hall then it can come and walk them with me, and I will not write them down and neither will you.** **THE REGISTRAR ON THE WORD LAST MONTH, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT WORDS: Yesterday morning the words last month and the month before last in this district meant the fourth month and the third month, and since six yesterday morning they have meant the fifth and the fourth, and the twenty-fourth of the third month… is two months back and sixty-nine days. I have written the change into the standing page in my own hand and not into a book anybody may walk up to, and a clerk of nineteen years has entered that the usage of this district turned with the month, and that is a fact about words and is not a finding about anybody.** The man of about fifty-five sent nothing. The trough was still about four inches over its lip and had been since the second week of last month. The man of about thirty at a counter is at the sixteenth time down the eleven mile road and the next is the seventeenth, and this district has never once given that man a day to be here on and did not give him one this morning, and the man of about forty-one who keeps a road house at the top of the road that climbs is not in this district on this day either and none may be given to him.

## Chapter 0253 — Four Miles Of Lane

**The third of the sixth month, clear and cold for the hour, about nine people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-one days old. The board at 161, the train at 477, the unentered days at 191, a hundred and fifty-two from the second of January, and a woman standing at the gate who has not been in that yard for eleven months and whom nobody sent for.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale told the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing that the woman of fifty-eight carries water to eleven houses and has a woman in the ninth of them who is being moved on the first of the month after next, and a clerk entered that a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale who has said a thing out loud has not thereby been asked a question about it, that the ninth of the eleven houses is the woman of fifty-eight's and is not hers, and that the record about the ninth of the eleven houses says not asked. **The first draft of the chapter, and of this entry, gave that speech to the woman of fifty-eight, who is not in that scene and says nothing in it, and made the speaker the woman who carries the water; both were corrected in review.** **The first draft of this entry, and of the chapter, gave that speech to the woman of fifty-eight, who is not in that scene and says nothing in it, and both were corrected in review.** **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO KEEPS A ROUTE FOR PEOPLE WHO ARE NOT IN ANY BOOK CAME DOWN FOUR MILES IN THE DARK AND GOT IN ABOUT A QUARTER TO EIGHT WITH A BUNDLE ABOUT THE SIZE OF A LOAF, AND IS THE ONLY PERSON IN THIS BASIN WHO HAS EVER FOUND HIM WITHOUT BEING SENT, AND THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST CAME OUT OF THE GATE AT ABOUT HALF PAST EIGHT AND DID NOT GO WITHIN NINE FEET OF HER.** **THE REFUSAL, IN A HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FOUR WORDS: Put my name under a line on it. That is what you have got in that fold and I have not even opened it and I do not need to. No. And the reason is not that I do not trust you and it is not that I have come four miles to be difficult at a gate. The reason is that a name under a line is a door, and a door is a thing a person comes to, and I have spent nine years making sure there is no door in this basin that has my name behind it. You wrote that rule yourself in April in a yard full of people, that a name put on a figure cannot be taken off it by the person it is put on, and on the twentieth of last month a man came four miles on the strength of one and stood at that gate, and you told me about it in a lane and said findable is a worse door than a page.** **HIS HALF, IN A HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-SEVEN WORDS: there are four hundred and eleven people on a sheet at a gatepost and the first day of the month after next is fifty-nine days off counted off this morning, and the version of this that I can do in a week is a sheet with nine names under a line and one of the nine is you… I know what it is. I have spent five months building doors and I have just watched one of them cost me the last private thing I had, and I brought you one anyway, at six in the morning, down four miles, without asking you first what you were doing this month.** A clerk entered that a sheet with nothing written on it is not a page, not a document, and not one of the three documents this district does not own, and that a man who convenes nine settlements has not convened one and that a door with a name behind it is not a settlement and is not a representation. **SHE ON THE NUMBER, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-TWO WORDS: I am not going to tell you how many of them there is and you are not going to ask me twice… the first time somebody wanted a number about us they would use that one and not go and knock. That is the whole trick and it has taken four generations and I am not the one who is going to undo it for a sheet with a fold in it.** A clerk entered that the record about four hundred and eleven says not asked, and that it says not asked from the person best placed in this basin to answer it and not from a stranger. The bundle was bread and a piece of chalk and two strings. The man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing offered to copy a sheet she had not opened and was refused. **THEY STOOD IN THE LANE BEHIND THE BANK FOR ABOUT NINE MINUTES, AND A CLERK OF NINETEEN YEARS WAS NOT IN THAT LANE AND ENTERED IT AFTERWARDS FROM ONE OF THE TWO OF THEM, AND WHAT HE SAID WAS NOT COUNTED, AND WHAT SHE SAID BACK WAS NOT COUNTED, AND A CLERK ENTERED THAT TWO PEOPLE'S SENTENCES ENTERED IN ONE HAND IS A CONVERSATION AND NOT A RECORD.** The chair stood forty-one days and the fold in the sheet in the inside pocket of a coat had come out on its own.

## Chapter 0254 — The Routes

**About nine people came back down the four miles at about four in the morning on the fourth of the sixth month, in fours and fives, wet to the knee, and a clerk of nineteen years was at the gate because she had not gone to bed. By ten it was warm and about nineteen people were in the yard and the chair was forty-two days old. The board at 162, the train at 478, the unentered days at 192, a hundred and fifty-three from the second of January.** A clerk entered the whole of it before anybody spoke: nine people went out at about eleven o'clock, nobody sent for any of them, the woman of about thirty-one was in front, the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road was in front of her and had not been asked, and the route was not written down before they went and not written down after they came back. **The woman of about thirty-one had a cut about the length of two fingers on the inside of her left shin taken by the wire on the left of that lane, walked four miles back with it and said nothing about it and did not limp.** **THE WOMAN OF FIFTY-EIGHT WAS AT THE TOP OF THE BANK AT ABOUT TEN MINUTES TO FOUR IN THE MORNING WITH A LAMP AND A BUCKET, BECAUSE SHE COMES DOWN AT SIX MOST MORNINGS AND HAD COME DOWN AT FOUR, AND SHE SAW THE NINE OF THEM COME THE LAST TWO HUNDRED YARDS AND PUT THE LAMP DOWN AND PICKED IT UP AGAIN AND DID NOT SAY ONE WORD TO ANYBODY, AND THE RECORD ABOUT THE LAMP AND THE TWO HUNDRED YARDS SAYS NOT ASKED.** **THE JOIN REFUSED A FOURTH TIME, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTY-NINE WORDS: Somebody in this yard this morning was going to say that the four hundred and eleven could be put into nine and that nine lines is nine doors… It is the same join I refused in this yard on the fourteenth and twenty-eighth of the month before last and on the second-to-last day of last month… Nine lines with nobody standing at them is a page with nine places ruled on it. I have held that page in my hand. I know what an empty ruled place looks like and I am not going to help anybody make one.** A clerk entered that the refusals to join two things are thirteen and the registrar's own count is twelve and that neither moved, and that **a join is a join, and a refusal of one join a fourth time is one refusal and not a fourth, and the number of people watching it is not a reason.** A man of fifty-six said a shape noticed a second time is not a finding and a third time is a habit and a fourth time is a man who has made a rule and is going to have to live on it. **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SIX WHO KEEPS A SCALE HAD BEEN STANDING AT THAT SCALE SINCE ABOUT SIX IN THE MORNING, AND ABOUT NINETEEN SACKS WENT ACROSS IT INSTEAD OF ABOUT NINE, AND SHE SAID IT IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-EIGHT WORDS: I have stood at this scale nine years and I weigh about nine sacks a day… If four hundred and eleven people are on a piece of paper then that paper has got four hundred and eleven sacks on it in a man's head, and I have got a beam and a weight and a pair of hands and I am telling you that a man with a paper is carrying a thing I could not lift.** **SHE STOOD AT THAT SCALE UNTIL ABOUT HALF PAST FIVE AND COULD NOT GET HER RIGHT ARM ABOVE HER SHOULDER AND HAD TO TAKE THE BEAM DOWN WITH HER LEFT HAND, AND THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING HELD THE SLATE SHE WRITES THE WEIGHTS ON.** **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A GOAT, AT THE THIRD HOUSE UP THE LANE FROM THE SCALE, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT WORDS: I have kept a goat for eleven years and I have held a bucket and a rope and a kid that did not want to come and nothing else heavier than about nine pounds since the spring. I held that slate for nine minutes this afternoon for a woman who could not lift her arm and I could not get my hand round the edge of it at the end, and it is a piece of slate…** and the twelve words she promised are printed in full: > **I AM NOT A SECOND HAND AND I WILL NOT BE ONE** A clerk wrote them in her own hand, read them back, and she said she had got them. **A MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-NINE WHO DRIVES A CART, who had brought nine sacks down to the scale that morning and had not been asked to, proposed that somebody should walk the ground in the shadow of the Hall and count the doors and put the number in chalk on a board, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam said that a set of doors counted in chalk is a column with a word over it, and a clerk entered that the four columns of not-askings are four and she did not rule one and is not going to, and that a chalk figure on a board is not a finding and is not a person and cannot be asked a question, and that a man who hauls a thing for somebody else all day carries it and is not a bearer of it and has not been asked to be one.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said out loud that his six have not moved in forty-four days, one signed, two refused and three not asked again, and that he was going to say it once and not again this month. The woman of fifty-eight came down the bank at about six with two buckets and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing carried one up, and a clerk entered that nobody counted the buckets and that a bucket is about eleven pounds full and that nobody in this basin has ever asked her. **THEN HE ASKED HER ONE QUESTION, and it is the first question anybody in this district has asked that woman: whether the ninth of the eleven houses she carries to is one of the four hundred and eleven on that sheet at the gatepost. She did not answer him, and a clerk entered that a person who has been asked a question and has not answered it has not refused it and cannot be counted either way, and that the record about the ninth of the eleven houses says not asked, and that nobody sent for him to ask it and that he is going to go on asking it and has not said so out loud. The day came down about half past seven, the first day of the month after next was fifty-eight days off counted off that morning, and a man of about fifty-six said one sentence about the question and it was not counted, because nobody in that yard had been asked to count it, and the record about it came from the man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart and is not a man of about nineteen who counts and the two are not the same and are not added together: I have heard four hundred and eleven out loud in this yard once in ten days and I have heard a figure of my own twice, and the two are not the same size and one of them is on paper and the other one is a man. If he asks her again tomorrow she will not answer him tomorrow either, and if she never answers him then he has spent a year asking a question and got nothing and told people he was asking it. **This close replaced a repeated two-bucket paragraph, which was the chapter's last beat in the first draft and introduced no new question, decision or threat.**

## Chapter 0255 — Two Doors

**The fifth of the sixth month, clear and hot by eleven, about nine people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-three days old. The board at 163, the train at 479, the unentered days at 193, and a hundred and fifty-four days from the second of January, which is twenty-two weeks and no days, counted off the second of January and not off a page.** **THE DAY, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-NINE WORDS: There is a building about two hundred yards down the lane behind the bank at the near end of it with one room in it and about nine people in it most nights, and it has two doors, and the second one is a plank door at the back that opens on a ditch. About nineteen of us slept in that room in the night of the second and were gone before six this morning, and I did not send for one of them and I did not fetch one of them, and I am not going to put a figure on it.** A clerk entered that a person who slept in a room has not been entered anywhere and that **a building with two doors is not a sixth protected thing.** **THE BACK OF THE BUILDING, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-SEVEN WORDS: The second door is not a door. It is a piece of board with a nail in it and it comes off in about two seconds and it makes a noise like a duck and it opens on a ditch that goes down to about four feet of water in the wet and about a foot in the dry, and everything I have spent five months building is a way of getting a person into a room, and that is not what that room is for. That room is for getting a person out of it without anybody seeing them go out of it… I have been wrong about five months of work and I am saying it here before somebody else has to.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING WENT ROUND THE OUTSIDE AT TWENTY PAST TWELVE, WORKED THE HASP WITH HIS LEFT HAND AND COULD NOT GET HIS FINGERS ROUND THE NAIL AND IT TOOK HIM ABOUT NINE SECONDS, GOT IT UP WITH HIS RIGHT HAND, TOOK THE BOARD OFF IN ABOUT TWO SECONDS, AND COULD NOT GET THE NAILS BACK INTO HOLE THAT HAD CLOSED UP ABOUT A SIXTEENTH OF AN INCH, AND THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM HELD THE BOARD FOR HIM WITHOUT BEING ASKED AND NEITHER SAID A WORD.** **THE PANEL ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT HALF PAST ELEVEN, AND THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM DISAGREED WITH THE THIRD OF ITS THREE LINES, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-FOUR WORDS:** > **A BUILDING WITH TWO DOORS IS A BUILDING THAT CAN BE EMPTIED. A BUILDING WITH ONE DOOR IS A BUILDING THAT CANNOT.**
> **THE PEOPLE WHO GO IN THROUGH THE SECOND DOOR WERE NOT ASKED AND HAVE NOT REFUSED AND CANNOT BE COUNTED EITHER WAY. THEY ARE IN THE BUILDING AND THEY ARE NOT IN THE FIGURE.**
> **A FIGURE THAT DOES NOT INCLUDE EVERYONE IN THE ROOM IS A FIGURE OF THE ROOM AND NOT OF THE PEOPLE.**
> — *The second one is our own rule and our own rule is right and I would not change a word of it. The third one is asking a figure to be a thing about people, and a figure cannot be a thing about people however you word it… The difference is in whether anybody asked them, and this yard has spent five months being the only place in this basin where anybody asks anybody, and it just occurred to me that we have been doing it in the front of the building.* **THE REGISTRAR ON THAT BUILDING, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THREE WORDS: That building is not in my book and it is not in any book in this district and it is not going in one… I have been asked about it once in nine years by a man who wanted to know whether a person could be put under a line for sleeping in it, and I said no, and he did not put one there… I am not a keeper of it. I have never been asked to be and if I was asked tomorrow I would have to think about it for longer than this morning has.** A clerk entered that a keeper has never been on a page in this basin and there never was one and no keeper is named. **The man of about twenty-three was refused eleven coppers a week for the eighth time and the district has now said no eight times, and he copied nothing that afternoon and nobody asked him to.**

## Chapter 0256 — A Second Book

**The sixth of the sixth month, warm and low, about nine people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-four days old. The board at 164, the train at 480, the unentered days at 194, a hundred and fifty-five from the second of January, and a second book in a room four hundred yards away that nobody has been in and nobody has been asked to be in.** A clerk entered that a second book is a book and that the two are not the same thing, and that a rule about books is not a document and is not a page and did not become a fourth of the three documents this district does not own. **THE ROOM IS ABOUT NINE FEET BY ELEVEN WITH ONE TABLE, ONE SHELF AND ONE DOOR, AND THE FANLIGHT OVER THAT DOOR DOES NOT LIGHT ANYTHING, AS IT HAS NEVER ONCE LIT ANYTHING IN FOUR MONTHS, AND NOBODY HAS EVER ASKED HER ABOUT IT.** The first book has been on that table for nine years, a bound ledger about two inches thick with a strap, at the left-hand end. **The second is a bound ledger of the same make about three-quarters of an inch thick on the shelf on its side, with a second chair turned up on top of it so that nobody coming in at a hurry can knock it off.** A clerk entered that a second book that has to have a chair turned over it to stay on the shelf is a book its keeper expects somebody to want, and that a person who expects somebody to want a book has not been asked what the wanting is for. **THE DECLARATION, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THREE WORDS: I have opened a second book… I have done the thing I told this yard on the sixteenth of last month I would not do, which is put my room behind somebody's arrangement. I am not going to stand here and be praised for it and I am not going to be told I have broken a rule, because I have broken it, and I wrote it, and I would like it written down in one line that I knew what I was doing at about five this morning.** **THE DOORWAY AT ABOUT THREE IN THE AFTERNOON. He asked her for the number of names in it and she said no in about four seconds and gave a reason, and there was nobody there to count it and a clerk entered that the sentence was entered in the hand of the man who asked and that a sentence entered in one person's hand is a conversation and not a record: No. And it is my own rule and not yours and not his, and I wrote it on the twenty-first of the month before last… You are not that person. You have been in nine doorways in nine days and every one of them was somebody else's rule about somebody else's room… and I have read what you wrote down, and I am not going to be the second person in your book this week who hands you a number and calls it help.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY WORDS: A first book in one room by one person is the reason a notice four hundred miles away could be written without anybody's name on it. A second book in one room by one person is the reason four hundred and eleven people on a piece of paper do not have to be asked anything this month. Both of those are true and they cannot both be a rule.** **A man of about nineteen who counts said, in a hundred and twenty-one words, that he was not going to try to find out what is in the second book and was not going to try to find out how many names are in it, and that he could count the same nine people in that yard at ten and again at eleven this evening and they would be nine, and that he would rather say that out loud in a yard than be a man in a book somewhere with a number in him that he did not put there.** A man of fifty-six had said in the yard on the second that a rule which cannot be looked at by anybody cannot be argued with by anybody. **Nobody put a line on a board on any of the sixty-seven days since the first of the fourth month, counting the first day in.**

## Chapter 0257 — A Room With No Name Over The Door

**The seventh of the sixth month, hot by nine, about eleven people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-five days old. The board at 165, the train at 481, the unentered days at 195, a hundred and fifty-six from the second of January, and about nine people in a room about two miles the other way this evening and a man going there on his own two feet with nothing in his hands.** **The room is behind a building on a lane that has no name on anything, about nine feet by fourteen, and there is nothing over the door. Not a board, not a paper, not a mark, not a line of charcoal and not a line of stones.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps that room has kept it for four years. A clerk entered that four women keep four things inside four hundred yards of each other in this basin and that none of the four is a clerk of a house. **THE THREE TRADES, and a clerk entered the three and did not enter a fourth: where about nineteen people slept last night, said by pointing at about eleven notches cut in the underside of a bench at intervals that are not even, and the woman of about twenty-nine said out loud before anybody asked her that they are not a calendar and are not a count of nights and are not a count of people; which lane is under water where, said by standing up, and nobody in that room raised their voice between about nine in the evening and about half past ten, and a clerk entered that she was not in the room and knows that from the woman of about twenty-nine and not from being there; and the fact that a person has been in a place, said to about four other people and not written down.** **THE THIRD ONE, IN A HUNDRED AND FOURTEEN WORDS: I do not keep the nine. I keep the room and the bench and the piece of chalk and the eleven notches on the underside of the seat… If I wrote it down then in four months somebody would come with a sheet, and if somebody comes with a sheet then the notches go, and if the notches go then I have got a room with no name over the door and nothing else at all, and a room with nothing else at all is a room somebody else can have.** **THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE THINGS THIS DISTRICT DOES NOT HAVE, REFUSED BEFORE SHE WAS ASKED, IN A HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-THREE WORDS: You are going to stand in my room and say the fifth thing this district does not have, and you are going to say it as though saying it out loud fixes it. A way to pay a person who is not in a household. I have had that hole since I was fifteen… and if the fifth of your five is going to be the fifth of mine then somebody is going to come and ask me what I would take for it.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT TWENTY-THREE REFUSED A LIST OF THE NINE HOUSES, IN A HUNDRED AND NINE WORDS: No, and I will not even let you write the nine down first, because a list of nine houses in your hand is a list of nine houses that can be read… I am not refusing you because you would sell it. I am refusing you because you would not.** **A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-TWO WORDS: It is the only public instrument in this basin with no address on it, and I have been a man who reads figures out loud in a yard for eleven years and I have never once been in a room where there was nothing over the door, and I have been in about nine doorways in nine days and thought that made me somebody who had seen both sides of a thing. I have not.** **THE REGISTRAR ASLEEP AT A DESK IN THE ROOM WHERE THE BOOKS ARE AT ABOUT FOUR IN THE MORNING ON THE SEVENTH, and the man of about thirty-four who digs loam put his own hand over hers and asked her to close it on the pen and it would not for about nine seconds, and she woke up and closed it and said nothing about it for the rest of the day, and a clerk entered that a hand that will not close on a pen is a hand and is not a figure about anybody and is not a reason for anything.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said his six have not moved in forty-seven days, and he said it in that yard at about half past four that same afternoon before the light went off the top of the bank. **The first draft of the chapter put that scene on *the next afternoon*, which is the eighth's afternoon, in a chapter dated the seventh, and the review pulled it back onto the seventh, where forty-seven is the seventh's figure counted off the twenty-first of the fourth month, and a clerk entered that a figure said out loud on the seventh of this month is the seventh of this month's figure and not the eighth's, and that the eighth of this month is tomorrow and has not been counted off by anybody. The registrar's four-in-the-morning entry is now entered out of what he said afterwards at about half past eleven at night and not out of having been there, and it names the six hours between that morning and the yard.** **A man of about forty-four came down the eleven mile road in the second week of last month with a case, and did not come into that yard, and did not come back, and nobody sent for him and the record about the case says not asked.**

## Chapter 0258 — A Line Of Print

**The eighth of the sixth month, overcast and warm, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-six days old. The board at 166, the train at 482, the unentered days at 196, a hundred and fifty-seven from the second of January, and a sheet that came into the yard in the night with three lines on it and the middle one of the three is a job.** **THE ORDER, READ OUT AND READ BACK:** > **THE BUILDING AT THE NEAR END OF THE LANE BEHIND THE BANK, WHICH HAS TWO DOORS, IS TO BE CLOSED ON THE ELEVENTH DAY OF THIS MONTH.**
> **IT IS TO BE CLOSED BY ITS KEEPER.**
> **A KEEPER IS A PERSON WHO HAS BEEN ASKED TO KEEP IT AND HAS SAID YES. THERE IS NO COLUMN FOR A PERSON WHO HAS NOT BEEN ASKED AND THE COLUMN FOR THE ONE WHO HAS IS EMPTY.**
A clerk entered that the sheet was printed with its own hole in it, that the hole is on the third line and not the second, and that a sheet carrying a job and a rule about the job and an empty column for the job is a document that has found nobody and has said so in print. **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM ON THE SECOND LINE, IN A HUNDRED AND SIXTY-THREE WORDS: It is to be closed by its keeper. That is the whole of the threat and it is not a threat about a door, it is a threat about a person, and it has gone looking for a person in the only place a document can look, which is a column, and the column is empty and the sheet says so… somebody four hundred miles away has just written down a job and a rule for filling it and has left it empty, and that is the first time in six months that anybody has written down a way of moving a person that does not need the person.** A clerk entered that a mark may not carry a job, and that a printed line may not carry a job either, and that a document which names a job that does not exist has found nobody. **A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTEEN WORDS, UNASKED: I have been in this yard since the second of April refusing to let a document do a job that a person has not been asked to do, and I have said it a hundred times in five months and every time I have said it a person has nodded and went on doing what they were doing. This morning a body four hundred miles away has written my sentence down and printed it and put an empty column under it, and it is better than mine, and the reason it is better is that mine needed somebody in the room to agree with it and that one does not need anybody anywhere.** **WHO WROTE IT: nobody knew, and the sheet carries no name of a person and no name of a body and no name of a place, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said, uncounted, in a minute in his own words, that a piece of paper with three lines on it and nothing else on it is the exact shape of the page that came out of a cave with nine places ruled on it, and that the difference between the two is which side of the door it came in by, and that it is the best piece of printing anybody in this basin has put up in his lifetime.** **ABOUT NINE PEOPLE WENT OUT TO THE RISE ABOUT A MILE OUT PAST THE LOAMS, AND STOOD THERE ABOUT TWENTY MINUTES. The Hall is about eleven miles off in a straight line, a long low building with a roof that is not a roof you can cover a house with and a wall on the end of it that does not come down to the ground. A man of about nineteen counted the two ends and the notch in the middle of the roof and declined the rest.** **THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX AT THE FRONT OF THE RISE, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FOUR WORDS: I have been in this basin fifty-six years and I have never once been closer to that building than this rise… The reason is that everything this district has done since the second of April has been a way of asking a person a question, and a person on the end of a question is a person who has been asked, and I have not asked anybody anything in eleven miles and neither has anybody else in this party, and if I go any further I will be standing on a road in front of a door doing the thing that got a sheet on a gatepost.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO KEEPS A ROAD AT THE TOP OF THE RISE, IN A HUNDRED AND THREE WORDS: I have kept four miles of that road for eleven years and there is a gate on it about a mile before the place where the ground stops, and everybody in this basin thinks the gate is the far end of the road. It is not. The road goes on past the gate for another seven miles and I have not walked the last of it in six years and I am not going to today, and nobody has ever asked me what is at the end of it, and I would like that written down as a thing nobody has asked me.** A clerk entered that the man of about thirty-one's name is still on a page against this district's records office and he has never been given the job and never been paid. The eleventh of this month is three days off.

## Chapter 0259 — The Bell In A Frame

**The ninth of the sixth month, clear and hot by ten, about nine people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-seven days old. The board at 167, the train at 483, the unentered days at 197, a hundred and fifty-eight from the second of January, and about nine people going four miles out past the loams to look at a bell that is not going to be rung, said out loud at the gate before they went so that a clerk could write it down as a thing nine people agreed to before they went and not as a thing nine people found out afterwards.** **THE FRAME IS OF OAK ABOUT ELEVEN FEET HIGH WITH TWO UPRIGHTS ABOUT FOUR FEET APART AND A BEAM ACROSS THE TOP, AND THE OAK IS OLDER AND DARKER THAN THE OAK AT THE LOAMS, AND THERE IS A SECOND FRAME BEHIND IT ABOUT NINE FEET BACK THAT IS NEWER AND IS HOLDING NOTHING.** The bell hangs in the middle on a headstock of iron, about two feet across the mouth, cracked from the rim up about a third of the way on the side that faces the loams, and **the rope is not there: about three feet of it is gone and about nine inches of stub is hanging inside the beam where nobody standing on the ground can reach it.** **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTEEN WORDS: Somebody took it. It was not cut, it was untied at the top and pulled down a bit at a time so that you could not see it from the road, and the nine inches that is left is the length that keeps a man from being able to say he has rung it by accident. Whoever took it did not want the bell gone. They wanted the bell to be there and to be unreachable, and that is a different thing and it is a thing somebody thought about for a long time.** **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO DIGS LOAM, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SEVEN WORDS: A bell is a thing in a frame and a frame holds it up and a bell does not need a person. That is the first honest thing anybody in this district can say about a useful object in eighteen months… But a bell is not a plank. A plank in a notch does not keep nights, and I do not know what a bell does and neither does anybody in this yard, and I have been in this basin eighteen years and I have never once seen it rung, and the two times it has been rung are on two walls about a mile apart and I have read both of them.** **THE TWO WALLS. The first is on the gable end of a barn a mile back toward the loams, in whitewash, and the top of the last rung has gone and there is nothing above the first line of it:** > **RUNG. THE ELEVENTH OF THE THIRD MONTH OF THE FIFTEENTH YEAR. AT NIGHT. NOBODY CAME.**
> **The second is on the inside of a byre a mile the other way, in charcoal, in a different hand, four lines, and the last one has been rubbed at:**
> **RUNG ON A NIGHT IN THE MONTH AFTER THAT ONE.**
> **WE WENT DOWN THE LANE WHEN WE HEARD IT.**
> **WE CAME BACK UP THE LANE WHEN IT STOPPED.**
> **NAMES OF WHO HEARD IT —**
A clerk entered that the two are not one record and are not added together, that a wall is not an address and is not a page and is not a book, that the eleven words under the rubbed line are rubbed, and that **nobody in this district has been asked to say whether they were ever legible.** **THE PANEL ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT ELEVEN AND NOBODY IN THAT YARD DISAGREED WITH IT OUT LOUD, and a clerk entered that nobody did, and that THE RECORD ABOUT THE DISAGREEING SAYS NOT ASKED, and that she did not count the other four people on purpose and is not going to be asked for them:** > **A BELL IS A VOICE. A VOICE REACHES EVERYBODY WITHIN THE SOUND OF IT.**
> **A LIST OF WHO HEARD IT IS A LIST OF WHO EXISTED ON A NIGHT. A LIST OF WHO EXISTED ON A NIGHT IS THE FIRST HALF OF A FINDING.**
> **THE HALF THAT IS WORTH HAVING IS THE ONE WHERE NOBODY IS NAMED, AND A BELL THAT NAMES NOBODY IS A BELL THAT HAS NOT RUNG.**
A man of fifty-six said out loud that he disagreed with the third of the three and then said nothing else at all, and a clerk entered that a man who says he disagrees and does not say why has not converted a disagreement into a finding. **IN A ROOM WITH A SHUT DOOR FOUR HUNDRED YARDS AWAY, AT ABOUT FOUR IN THE AFTERNOON, THE REGISTRAR DISAGREED WITH THE THIRD LINE OUT LOUD, TO HIM AND NOT TO A ROOM, and it was not counted and the record came from him and not from her, in a hundred and thirty-eight words: A bell that names nobody is not a bell that has not rung. It is a bell somebody has written a piece of paper about, and the paper is in a book four hundred miles away, and the paper has got a heading over the column, and everybody within the sound of it is in the column, and there is no third column on that page and nobody in this basin has ever been asked whether there should be one… a rule that says the half worth having is the half with nobody named in it is a rule written by somebody who has a page in front of him and is asking the rest of us to be pleased about it.** **The woman of about thirty-one refused a second thing in a doorway nine yards up the lane and gave as her reason, entered from the man who asked, that if the route passed the frame then four generations of people would have known it, and that a frame in a yard four miles out past the loams that everybody knows about is a thing four generations have arranged to know nothing about.** The eleventh of this month is two days off.

## Chapter 0260 — The Difference Between The Two Numbers

**The tenth of the sixth month, hot by nine, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the chair forty-eight days old and unsat in on any of the forty-eight. The board at 168, the train at 484, the unentered days at 198, a hundred and fifty-nine from the second of January, the first day of the month after next fifty-two days off, and the ninth of the nine printed nights was the twentieth of the month before last, fifty-one days back, and nobody in this yard knows whether that night happened.** **THE SUBTRACTION, IN A HUNDRED AND NINETY-EIGHT WORDS, BY A MAN WHO HAD BEEN IN THE ROOM WHERE THE BOOKS ARE AT ABOUT FOUR IN THE MORNING ON THE SEVENTH AND COUNTED WHAT WAS ON THE TWO OPEN PAGES WITH HIS OWN FINGER ON THE LINES AND WAS NOT ASKED AND DID NOT ASK: The first number is four hundred and eleven and it is on a sheet at a gatepost and it is a count of the people who answered a door. The second number is forty-one and it is the two open pages of one book in one room four hundred yards from this gate at four in the morning on the seventh… Three hundred and seventy is the difference between those two numbers and I am not going to stand in this yard and say that three hundred and seventy people are going to be moved on the first of the month after next, because a subtraction of a piece of somebody else's paper and two pages of one book is not a finding. It is arithmetic.** A clerk entered that a subtraction of a printed number and the open pages of one book is not a finding and is not a column and did not become a seventh instrument of the six. **WHAT THE DIFFERENCE IS MADE OF, IN A HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-ONE WORDS: Three hundred and seventy is the size of the number and it is not the size of the thing. The thing is that four hundred and eleven was got by knocking and forty-one was got by somebody opening a door of their own and saying so, and one of those two methods is this district's whole method and the other one is the one that got the sheet, and the difference between the two methods is the three hundred and seventy people in the middle who have not been asked anything by anybody at all, and have not refused, and cannot be counted either way. They are not a figure. They are the hole in the middle of this month and this yard has been writing things with a hole in the middle of them since the eighteenth of last month and this is the first one that is about people.** **THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST, IN A HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-ONE WORDS: I have got nine sheets in nine rooms and a covenant in a book a stranger can walk up to and a clerk of nineteen years and a notice under the leg of a table, and I can put any nine people in this basin behind any nine of those nine doors in a week. I have not been able to get one name out of the ground in the shadow of that Hall in ten days and I have not tried, and I am not going to, and I want that said out loud in this yard by me and not found out about me in a month. I have been standing next to the thing for ten days and I have been calling it representing it, and it is not that. A door is a thing you build for the people who come to it, and I have built nine, and the only people who have come to them are the ones who already had a reason to come.** **THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX, IN A HUNDRED WORDS, UNASKED: You have been standing next to a thing and calling it representing it. Standing next to a thing is not standing in its place. I have said that sentence in about nine yards and in about nine doorways since the second of April and I have never once said it to you, and I am saying it to you this morning because you have had it yourself this morning and you have done the work and I am not going to be a man who takes a thing off a man who has just found it out on his own.** **A CLERK ENTERED THE THINGS THAT DID NOT MOVE IN TEN DAYS**, and they were in the same hand as the rest and they were not a paragraph: the rival record in force for ten days, read out seven times, unanswered, and nobody going to carry a page out of that office to answer it; the five, the six and no seventh proposed, the three documents nobody owns, the five protected things and no sixth and a building with two doors is not a sixth, the four conditions, the thirteen and the twelve, the nine refusals to read and the unspent departure and no date in the ledger and the fourth line of the offer not read out, the eight and five refusals with a reason and not one of them a clerk of a house, the seven and the two are not the same figure, and no rate that turns a year into coppers; nobody on a board on any of the seventy-one days since the first of the fourth month; the chair moved four feet once and forty-eight days; the six households fifty days and the figure is his and was not asked for; the man of about thirty at a counter at the sixteenth time and the next the seventeenth and no day in ten days; the man of about forty-one at the top of the road not in this district on any of the ten days; the man with the portfolio not in this district; and five ninths that are not added together and are not a pattern. **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat came down the lane and said the woman who keeps the scale had said out loud that she was going to sit at that scale on the eleventh whether anybody was there or not.** **THE SLATE WENT PAST THAT KITCHEN FOR THE THIRTEENTH TIME and he wrote one line on the side that faced her and put it down on the step, and neither of them said anything about what was on it while he was writing it, and a clerk four hundred yards away was not told until about seven and the record came from him and not from her.** He said, uncounted: **I am not going to ask you what is on it. I have asked you nothing for ten days and I am not going to start this afternoon.** And she said, uncounted: **Then do not come and find me about the forty-one. That is what is on it, and you counted them off a page of mine at four in the morning with your own finger on the lines, and I have known that since the seventh… If you come and find me about it, then I am a woman you got a number out of in a room, and that is the fifth thing this district does not have and I have refused it once this month already.**

## Chapter 0261 — Nothing In The Column

**The eleventh of the sixth month, close and hot before nine, about nineteen people in the yard of Lot Seventeen at about ten, and the chair forty-nine days old. The board at 169, the train at 485, the unentered days at 199, a hundred and sixty from the second of January, the first day of the month after next fifty-one days off counted off that morning, and a building with two doors is to be shut today by a person this yard has never asked.** **THE DAY, IN A HUNDRED AND FOURTEEN WORDS: We are going down the lane behind the bank this morning and we are going to stand in front of that building, and I am not going to read that sheet out a second time because it is in a coat in this yard and about nineteen people heard it on the eighth, and the second of its three lines says the building is to be shut by its keeper, and there is no keeper.** A clerk entered that fifty-one is nineteen and thirty-one and one and that **the length of the month after next is not stated in this district and was not used this morning.** **THE REFUSAL AT THE SCALE, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SIX WORDS: you walked down here and you asked me to be the keeper of a building, and you have got a piece of paper with an empty column under a rule about keepers, and you have come down this lane to put the nearest pair of hands in it. My hands are not that pair of hands.** A clerk entered that a refusal with a reason is not one of the refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given and that the eight did not move. **THE DITCH: the man of about thirty-four who digs loam went down about two feet into it, because a building with two doors is only shut if the second one is shut, and came up backwards with the hand hanging and said out loud that it is no use and that he had said nothing about it for nine days and that nine days was too long.** The plank door came off with the flat of an axe in about two seconds and made the noise in the ditch that it made on the fifth, and the same man held the board without being asked. **THE SENTENCE THE DAY WAS REMEMBERED BY, IN A HUNDRED AND NINE WORDS: A year ago that building had one door and a curtain in it. It has had a second door for about six weeks and nobody has ever once counted how many people went out of it, and today a piece of print with an empty column on it has shut nothing and opened everything, and I want the plainest sentence I can find for what that is and I have not got one. The column is empty. The building is not.** A man of about nineteen counted nine mats through the open front door and stopped, and the man of about twenty-three refused in **seventy-four** words to go two miles and write down what was in that room, and said it was a day a man can be found on.

## Chapter 0262 — The Same Question Twice

**The twelfth of the sixth month, low and grey, about eleven people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty days old. The board at 170, the train at 486, the unentered days at 200, a hundred and sixty-one from the second of January, which is twenty-three weeks and no days, the first day of the month after next fifty days off, and the rival record in force eleven days and unanswered.** **THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX ON THE YARD, IN A HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIVE WORDS: Six of you. There were nineteen in here on the first of this month and eleven on the fourth and there are six this morning and I have not seen one person walk out of that gate carrying anything, and I am telling you that a yard with nine fewer people in it is not a yard with a different opinion in it, it is a yard where nine people have gone somewhere else to say a thing.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road refused a second thing at about half past four and said he had been asked one question in this district since the first of the month that is now last month and had given one answer on the second of this month. **A clerk entered out of what he said afterwards and not out of having been asked: that he had seen a man of about forty-four with a case standing about nine hundred yards out on the eleven mile road, and that nobody sent him out and nobody sent for the man.** **THE SCALE KEEPER REFUSED TO BE ASKED A SECOND TIME ABOUT THE NINTH HOUSE, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SIX WORDS: On the third of this month I said out loud in that yard that there is a woman in the ninth of the houses she carries to who is being moved, and nobody asked me a question about it, and that is the only reason it is worth anything to anybody. If you ask me a question now then what I say next is an answer, and an answer is a thing with a person attached to it, and I have spent nine years at a scale not being a thing with a person attached to it.** A clerk entered that this is the third thing that one question has cost somebody this month. **At about six the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asked the woman of fifty-eight the same question he asked her on the fourth, with two buckets, and she looked at him for about as long as it takes to lift a bucket and went up the bank and did not answer him.** A man of fifty-six said one uncounted sentence about what the asking costs him, and the record about that sentence came from the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale. **IN ABOUT NINE FEET OF LANE OUTSIDE THE OFFICE: he offered to sit in that room four hundred yards away for the four nights of this month so that she would not be at a desk at four in the morning, and she refused in about four seconds: if the one person is you then it is not a rule, it is a preference, and a preference can be argued with at four in the morning by a man who has been awake since two. And he said: that is the fourth time in a month you have refused me something and every one of the four has been right, and I am not going to argue with it, and I am also not going to stop offering.**

## Chapter 0263 — Eleven Coppers A Week

**The thirteenth of the sixth month, hot before eight, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty-one days old. The board at 171, the train at 487, the unentered days at 201, a hundred and sixty-two from the second of January, the first day of the month after next forty-nine days off, and there is a man at the gate with a case.** The gate took two goes. **THE MAN HIMSELF, IN A HUNDRED AND SEVEN WORDS: I broker. That is the whole of what I do and I am not going to dress it up, because there is a man in this yard who has been asking everybody to be careful what they call a thing for five months and I would rather be called it early.** **HIS CARD, READ OUT AND READ BACK BECAUSE HE ASKED THE YARD TO READ IT:** > **A NAME THAT IS IN NO BOOK CAN BE PUT IN ONE FOR A CUT OF THE FIRST QUARTER OF WHAT THE NAME BRINGS IN.** > **A NAME BRINGS IN WHAT A PERSON WILL PAY A WEEK FOR THE USE OF SOMETHING THEY HAVE NOT GOT.** > **I DO NOT ASK ANYBODY WHAT THEY ARE CALLED. SOMEBODY ELSE HAS TOLD ME AND I HAVE PAID FOR IT.** **THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE THINGS NAMED AND REFUSED, IN A HUNDRED AND FORTY-EIGHT WORDS, by the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale, and refused by a man of fifty-six with a reason that is not the shape of the other eight: a thing this district cannot pay for is a thing this district has not agreed to be charged for, and a man with a case does not need anybody's agreement and that is exactly what I object to.** **THE YES, IN A HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIVE WORDS: I have been refused eleven coppers a week eight times in this district and I have said yes to it in one second from a man with a case, and the difference is not the eleven coppers. It is that a man with a case has not spent five months asking me to do it. He has not made me a refusal first. I have been a refusal in this district for five months and I have got very good at it and it has never once cost me anything, and I would like to be paid for four months of being a refusal.** He wrote a list of the people in this district who are not in a household for about an hour and his hand cramped, and a man of fifty-six took the pen out of his fingers and said nothing. **THE LANE OUTSIDE THE OFFICE: he offered to stand in the yard the next morning and say out loud what a man with a case is, and she refused in about four seconds — the day a man with a case is a thing this yard is doing something about is the day he has been asked, and he has not been asked, and a man who knows he has been noticed is a man who has been asked whether he was told to come.** The man of about forty-four was given the end of the trestle table.

## Chapter 0264 — What She Would Take

**The fourteenth of the sixth month, bright and still, about eleven people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty-two days old. The board at 172, the train at 488, the unentered days at 202, a hundred and sixty-three from the second of January, the first day of the month after next forty-eight days off, and a man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart took nine sacks off the end of that table at about six for a man with a case and nobody asked him to.** A clerk entered that the man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart is not a man of about nineteen who counts and is not the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door, and that the three are three people in three trades inside two miles of each other. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing walked the two miles to the lane with no name on anything and told the woman of about twenty-nine what the third line of the card said, and she said, in a hundred and fifty-five words: I said no to that on the seventh of this month before I was asked and I gave you the reason and you wrote it down, and the reason was that if the fifth of your five things was going to be the fifth of mine then somebody would come and ask me what I would take for it. He has come. He has not come to me, which is the part I did not think of. A man at a house on this lane told him there was a room behind the building where people slept some nights, and she told him because nobody has ever told her that room is worth keeping.** **She refused the man of about forty-four himself in a hundred and thirty-nine words and told him he could have the bench: the list you are holding was made before I knew you were on this lane, and I have found that out tonight, which means that everything I am saying now is too late to be worth anything to you and is worth something to the nine, and that is the only arithmetic in this room and I would like it noticed that I have been doing arithmetic for four years and this is the first sum of it that came out wrong.** **The goat keeper's printed span, nineteen words, which she would not say twice: > I HAVE SAID NO TO A MAN WITH A CASE AND HE IS NOT A MAN WITH A SLATE** **The woman of about thirty-one refused a third thing in about four seconds, in a hundred and thirty-four words: if a man is hauling sacks for a man with a case then that man is a person who is not in a household by the ordinary meaning of those words, and if his name is on a list then a route is a thing somebody can buy, and I have spent nine years making sure there is nothing in this basin that can be bought that walks.** She had come down four miles on her own two feet. **And the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said the thing about his own hand, in a hundred and seventy words, and it is the first time in fourteen days he has told anybody, and the reason he gave is that a man who asks questions and cannot hold what he is asking about is a man somebody else can use.**

## Chapter 0265 — One Copy Of Anything

**The fifteenth of the sixth month, low and warm, about eleven people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty-three days old. The board at 173, the train at 489, the unentered days at 203, a hundred and sixty-four from the second of January, and a sheet on the trestle table that was not on it at half past six the previous evening and nobody put it there.** **THE SECOND SHEET, READ OUT AND READ BACK:** > **A YARD IN WHICH THINGS ARE READ OUT IS A YARD IN WHICH THINGS ARE KEPT.** > **ONE COPY OF ANYTHING ENTERED IN IT IS TO GO TO THE BODY THAT SENT THIS SHEET.** > **THE COLUMN FOR THE PERSON WHO KEEPS THAT YARD IS EMPTY ON THIS SHEET AND WAS EMPTY WHEN IT WAS PRINTED.** > **NOBODY HAS TO BE ASKED FOR THIS. IT IS A LINE OF PRINT AND NOT A QUESTION.** **THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX, IN A HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIVE WORDS: On the eighth of this month I stood in this yard and said that a printed rule is better than mine because mine needed somebody in the room to agree with it and that one does not need anybody anywhere, and I said it like a man who has won something. I have been pleased about it for seven days. I have been pleased about it while a man with a case sat at that end of that table and I have been pleased about it while a man wrote down a list of people who are not in a household, and being pleased about it is the reason both of those things could happen in this yard and go on happening in it.** **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam, in a hundred and sixty-nine words: Two sheets in seven days and not one name on either of them, and the second of those two is the best piece of printing anybody has put up in this basin in my lifetime, and I have got no answer to it and I have not got one this morning.** **The man of about forty-eight refused a clerk's request for his figure for the first time in five months, in a hundred and thirty-nine words, and the reason is new: a figure of mine in a yard with that man in it is a figure of mine in his list, and he does not have to ask me a second time, and I am not going to be a man who has to be asked twice.** The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road walked his four miles and told the nine houses on it, and not one of them asked him for it, and a clerk entered that a refusal to say thank you is not a refusal. Eleven coppers were counted out onto the end of the trestle table in front of about eleven people, one at a time, and a man of about nineteen counted them and got eleven, and a clerk entered that a cut is not a rate and that there is still no rate in this basin that turns a year into coppers. The second sheet was nailed to the gatepost at about half past five, and the removal sheet's nail hole is beside it with nothing in it.

## Chapter 0266 — A Room With A Roof

**The sixteenth of the sixth month, bright and close, about six people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty-four days old. The board at 174, the train at 490, the unentered days at 204, a hundred and sixty-five from the second of January, and there are six of us in this yard and a sheet about a copy of anything is on that gatepost.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing stood in the gate for about nine minutes and did not say anything, and the yard got quieter rather than louder, and a clerk entered that a yard is a room with no roof and that a yard in which a thing cannot be said is a room with a roof, and that the roof on this one is a sheet on a gatepost and a man with a case at the end of a trestle table. Then he said the same thing in a lane to about four people instead, in a hundred and sixty-two words, ending: this district has had a second book of its own since the sixth of this month, and I want to ask in daylight, out loud, does anybody in this district know what is in it.** Nobody in that lane said anything, and the man of about twenty-three was not one of the four. **THE PANEL ARRIVED UNASKED AT ABOUT ELEVEN:** > **A PRICE PAID TO A PERSON WHO IS NOT IN A HOUSEHOLD IS A WAY OF PAYING A PERSON WHO IS NOT IN A HOUSEHOLD.** > **A WAY OF PAYING SOMEBODY IS A WAY OF FINDING OUT WHO IS NOT IN A HOUSEHOLD.** > **THE TWO ARE ONE INSTRUMENT AND THE SECOND ONE IS THE ONE THAT GETS USED.** **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale disagreed with the first of those three out loud in front of about six people, in a hundred and seventy-three words, and accepted the other two: eleven coppers a week to a man who copies for nothing is not a way of paying a person who is not in a household, it is a way of paying a man who is not in a household for the thing he was already doing for nothing, and that is a lower price for the same work and a higher price for the same man.** Nobody disagreed with the second or the third and a clerk entered that nobody did. **The man of about twenty-three said, in a hundred and forty-five words, that on the afternoon of the seventh of this month he was in the room where the books are and the second book was open on the shelf and he did not read it and has not stopped seeing it, and that he is saying it in that yard before anybody says it for him.** A clerk entered that neither the woman who keeps the books in that room nor the man the figure of twenty-one years is against was in that yard, and that it is the first of eleven days he has not stood in it, and that a clerk did not ask him why. **At about six, in a doorway, she said: I know what is in it. That is all I am going to say about it, and I want it written down that I said the first half of that sentence and not the second half, and that you did not ask me for the second half, and that a person who has said I know and has not said the number has not refused anything and cannot be counted either way.** A clerk entered that about three feet of rope is gone off a beam four miles out past the loams and about nine inches is left, and that nobody in this district has asked for it to be put back, and that the bell was not rung and the record about the not ringing says not asked.

## Chapter 0267 — Forty-One

**The seventeenth of the sixth month, hot before seven, about nineteen people in the yard at ten, and the chair fifty-five days old. The board at 175, the train at 491, the unentered days at 205, a hundred and sixty-six from the second of January, the first day of the month after next forty-five days off, and about nine of the nineteen had come because he had said in a lane the day before that he had one question.** **THE QUESTION, IN A HUNDRED AND EIGHT WORDS: There is a second book in the room four hundred yards from this gate. It has been open since the sixth of this month.
